#3018. Federal Reserve Board releases results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 10:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

Key findings from the 2025 survey include:

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#3017. Investors are doing just as well this year by shunning AI. How to invest if the tech trade keeps fizzling

A version of the S&P 500 without AI briefly pulled ahead of the traditional index this year and is now neck-and-neck as investors rotate into other areas of the market.

A proprietary Goldman Sachs index tracking the S&P 500 without stocks classified as "AI enablers" was up 12.4% through Oct. 22, edging ahead of the standard S&P 500's 12% gain. The Goldman index is now neck-and-neck with the S&P 500 as tech rallied in Monday trading.

Tech and AI-related companies make up about 45% of the S&P 500. That heavy weighting has helped power the index for years, but it's turned into a liability lately; the S&P 500 tech sector is down about 7% this month while other sectors have held up.

Investors have soured on debt and equity issued by companies like CoreWeave and Oracle. Shares of Meta Platforms sank almost 7% Thursday after it outlined more plans to spend heavily on capex and issue additional shares.

Companies supplying power and equipment for the buildout have been hit hard — names like GE Vernova, Bloom Energy, Vistra and Quanta Services all suffered steep declines during the recent selloff.

Goldman, which launched the measure earlier this year with S&P, uses the S&P 500 as its starting universe and excludes companies perceived to be enablers of AI before applying S&P 500 Index weighting methodology to the remaining universe of companies.

The ex-AI index lagged the S&P 500 for most of the year as investors continued to favor chipmakers, cloud-computing companies and other firms benefiting from heavy AI spending. However, that advantage has been narrowing and it disappeared last week.

Meanwhile, investors are rotating into defensive areas. ETFs like State Street Consumer Staples Select Sector SPDR ETF, State Street Health Select SPDR ETF and State Street Utilities Select Sector SPDR ETF are up 7% to 10% this month. Energy is ahead 10% too, though that's more related to the war in Iran.

Some analysts recommend sticking with non-AI companies for diversification and protection against more pain in tech. The "AI pendulum has swung back in favor of non-AI exposure over the last two weeks," Oppenheimer technical analyst Ari Wald said in a note on Oct. 24.

Two industries that Oppenheimer favors now are commercial services and insurance. The firm upgraded those industries to overweight last week, recommending Republic Services, Cintas, Verisk Analytics, Broadridge Financial Solutions, MetLife and Arthur J. Gallagher.

Berkshire Hathaway is also capitalizing on the rotation out of tech. Its Class B shares are up 8% this month, though it's still lagging behind the S&P 500 this year.

Berkshire owns a wide swath of operating companies, including insurance giant Geico and Burlington Northern Santa Fe railroad. It also has a $364 billion stock portfolio including companies like Apple, Coca-Cola, Alphabet and Bank of America. That diversification may help Berkshire continue to outperform if the tech sector drags down the S&P 500.

Trivector Research sees potential in "non-tech compounders." Healthcare giant Eli Lilly is one idea. The stock is up 22% on the strength of Lilly's weight-loss drugs. The company has more in the pipeline, including a next-generation obesity drug that Lilly plans to file for FDA approval early in 2027.

Viking Holdings, a cruise operator, is also proving durable. The company said on its August earnings call that 40% of its river capacity and 60% of its ocean capacity was already booked for 2027. "Based on recent events, we've seen our guests have proved to be resilient and are continuing to book 2027 and future seasons," the company told investors.

Viking stock is up 15% this year but it may still have upside. The average Wall Street target is $109, above recent prices around $82. Barclays recently upgraded the cruise operator to overweight with a $102 target, citing the company's "compelling risk/reward."

#3015. Drones are changing warfare. But defense giants are still making billions from old-school weapons

Drone technology and artificial intelligence are rapidly reshaping modern warfare, but the world's biggest defense contractors are finding that demand for tanks, fighter jets and other traditional military equipment remains strong.

Recent earnings from U.S. and European defense companies show conventional weapons continue to drive year-on-year growth, even alongside surging demand for advanced sensors, autonomous systems and counter-drone technology.

Rather than displacing traditional weapons manufacturers, the rise of drones and autonomous warfare is creating new business for many of the same companies.

From counter-drone defenses to advanced sensors integrated into tanks and fighter jets, the technologies transforming the battlefield are also changing, or even expanding, the markets served by legacy defense giants.

Lockheed Martin reported a 24% jump in revenue from its missiles business in the third quarter, while RTX secured multibillion-dollar contracts for established missile systems. In Europe, German defense supplier Renk saw orders for its military vehicle business surge 46% in the first nine months of the year.

Yet, the same earnings also show how the boundaries between legacy weapons and emerging defense technology are increasingly blurred.

German defense electronics company Hensoldt reported a 52% jump in revenue from its optronics business, with its chief financial officer telling CNBC that much of the growth came from conventional military equipment, including tanks and armored vehicles.

Meanwhile, Rheinmetall's air defense business, which includes counter-drone systems, reported 63% revenue growth, even as the wider company cut its full-year outlook, citing project delays.

The rise of drones is transforming traditional military platforms rather than replacing them, with tanks and other weapons increasingly integrated into connected, data-driven battlefield networks, according to Morningstar.

Over the past five years and since Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine in early 2022, defense primes have seen their order books swell amid higher military spending among governments globally.

Yet, analysts mostly say that the rearmament boom is structural, and even an end to wars in Ukraine and Iran won't derail it. Crucially, Europe now has to play catch-up amid increasing uncertainty over U.S. security guarantees and Russia's next move, after decades of military underfunding.

However, concerns that valuations had run ahead of the industry's ability to deliver dented defense stocks this year amid several instances of project delays and contract cancellations.

Advancements in modern warfare and technology – as proven by Ukraine's use of drones on the front lines – further hit investor confidence in defense contractors amid worries that new technologies may displace demand for traditional military equipment.

Beyond the front line, NATO plans to invest more than $40 billion in counter-drone capabilities over the next five years, as Secretary General Mark Rutte warned that drones have fundamentally transformed modern warfare.

Even so, Hensoldt CFO Christian Ladurner told CNBC on Thursday that demand for traditional military equipment remains a key driver of growth.

Its fast-growing optronics business, which supplies optical and sensor technologies, is benefiting from European governments' efforts to replenish conventional military equipment, even as newer battlefield technologies attract increasing attention, Ladurner said.

He also highlighted growing demand for air defense systems, as militaries confront an expanding range of threats, from small drones to ballistic missiles, adding that the air defense market is "underinvested" despite growing by 20% a year.

Limits to Patriot interceptors produced by Lockheed Martin are being felt hard, not least in Ukraine, after the U.S. depleted much of its stockpile of the state-of-the-art system able to take down high-speed missiles in the Iran war.

More recently, defense stocks have come under pressure amid concerns that the Democrats' U.S. midterm victories could squeeze American military spending.

While the result has dampened expectations for defense spending, with Democratic control of Congress making a proposed $1.5 trillion budget unlikely and potentially weighing on military spending growth through 2029, nothing has materially changed the geopolitical calculations, TD Cowen analyst Roman Schweizer told CNBC's "Morning Call" on Thursday.

"This is a multi-year picture, and I think we've seen those changes both in the U.S. and also Europe," he said, referencing internal budgetary difficulties and questions around NATO's hiked spending targets.

Morningstar analyst Loredana Muharremi told CNBC Wednesday's weakness in European defense stocks was largely sentiment-driven.

"Some investors may now see a greater chance of stronger US backing for Europe, easing the pressure on European governments to spend more [but] even with stronger US backing, we expect Europe to stick to its spending plans," she said.

"It takes years of investment to close the gaps in Europe's own military capabilities and build the industrial capacity it needs to deter Russia."

#3011. Prediction market traders say an Anthropic IPO is near-certain this year after company filed with SEC

Anthropic on Monday unveiled its Form S-1 filing, taking the company one step closer to completing its long-awaited move to the public markets, which is now seen as almost guaranteed to come this year.

Speculators on prediction market platform Kalshi place 95% odds that the artificial intelligence giant officially announces an initial public offering by the end of 2026.

Kalshi defines officially announcing an IPO as either the Securities and Exchange Commission declaring an S-1 effective, a priced IPO or an exchange assigning a ticker to a stock.

The Anthropic S-1 envisions a Nasdaq Stock Market ticker symbol of "ANT."

Anthropic is aiming to start trading the week of Nov. 16, according to a report in the Wall Street Journal. Traders on Kalshi place just a 14% chance that the company begins trading before then.

On the Polymarket platform, traders give a 69% chance that Anthropic will list on a public exchange and open for trading by Nov. 20.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#3010. Hoax bomb threats repeatedly disrupt flights across Europe this week. Here’s what we know

Flights across Europe were repeatedly targeted by "hoax" bomb threats this week, disrupting air travel across London, Copenhagen, Dublin, and Helsinki.

Danish police were investigating threats to air traffic at Copenhagen airport on Friday but did not find any immediate danger to passengers, according to reports from Reuters.

"We are once again at Copenhagen Airport today in connection with threats directed at air traffic," police said in a statement, per Reuters. "We are now investigating the incident and have a visible presence at the airport."

Meanwhile, Czech police also said Friday that there were threats to planes departing from and landing in Prague.

At least 27 different flights were impacted on Thursday, according to Reuters, with some forced to land unexpectedly and others escorted by fighter jets.

Aer Lingus, Finnair, Wizz Air and Polish airline LOT confirmed to CNBC that some of their flights were affected.

It comes after seven passenger planes across Europe, two departing from the U.K., were targeted by suspected bomb threats on Tuesday and were described as "malicious hoax threats" by the U.K. government.

Finnair said two of its flights — one from Helsinki to Rome with 144 passengers and 7 crew members and another from Helsinki to Vienna with 177 passengers and six crew members — received "unfounded or hoax" bomb threats on Thursday, with both flights landing safely and customers disembarking the aircraft.

Local authorities in Rome and Vienna inspected the two flights and didn't find anything out of the ordinary.

"Unfortunately, air traffic is sometimes subject to threats, as we have seen this week, when other airlines in Europe have also faced hoax bomb threats," Finnair said in a statement. "Threats are almost always unfounded."

Hungarian budget carrier Wizz Air confirmed to CNBC that its flight from Milan's Malpensa Airport to Bucharest's Otopeni Airport, as well as another flight to Warsaw, were subject to bomb threats on Thursday.

The flight to Bucharest was approaching its final destination and landed safely when the threat was reported at Otopeni, with security checks subsequently clearing the aircraft. Wizz Air's Warsaw flight was diverted.

"This incident comes amid a series of similar bomb threats affecting several airlines across Europe in recent days," Wizz Air told CNBC. "Wizz Air is not the only airline targeted, with other carriers also experiencing threats resulting in flight diversions and security checks."

Irish airline Aer Lingus diverted its Dublin to Rome flight to Normandy on Thursday morning, while Polish airline LOT said two of its flights from Warsaw received security threats. Both were cleared following security inspections, the airlines told CNBC.

Separately, a French prosecutor opened an investigation into whether the pilot of an Aer Lingus plane refused to land in Evreux in northern France following a bomb threat prompting a diversion.

The pilot reportedly refused to comply with the French authorities' directions to land the plane, before being forced by two fighter jets to comply. Prosecutor Remi Coutin said he'll be probing whether the refusal to comply amounts to a criminal offense.

Air France's CEO Ben Smith said none of its flights were affected, but the coordinated threats are unprecedented. "It's unfortunate, and we hope it won't give consumers any reason to slow down or reduce their travel plans."

U.S. Transport Secretary Sean Duffy told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Thursday that the incidents appeared to be "false attacks."

He added: "We take safety very seriously, which is why, if you see any issue in the airspace or threats in the airspace, we shut it down...and so, with all of our assets in the U.S. government and our partners over in Europe, we are tracking this together and working together to make sure the American airspace, the American traveler, is safe."

European Union Commissioner for Sustainable Transport and Tourism, Apostolos Tzitzikostas cautioned it is "too early to draw conclusions on whether the reported incidents are linked," in a post on X. "The EU-level information-sharing mechanism has been activated related to aviation security."

#3006. As AI becomes more fractured, one name stands out: AMD

As Anthropic rushes to takes its shares public, the AI trade is showing cracks, and they are not confined to fringe players.

We recently wrote about CoreWeave, which fell 15.5% on Oct. 14 as yields on its 2032 notes touched 15%, and its default-swap spreads nearly doubled in October. IREN is down more than 20% this year. Last Thursday, the contagion reached chipmakers, which makes sense since much of CRWV and IREN's spending is going there. AMD dropped 8.1% as Meta's financing plans and a supply warning from Lam Research exacerbated data-center fears about the pace of AI spending.

Because when you screen for factors that demonstrably generate outperformance over time, the list narrows quickly. My screen: price above the 200-day moving average; revenue growing at least as fast as the S&P 500 (5%+ per annum); EPS growing at least as fast (7%+); rating upgrades; a PEG below 3; and ROE and ROIC of at least 12%. Of the names reporting in the next two weeks, AMD is effectively the only one that makes the list. Yes, you can (and arguably should, as our staples article described) position your portfolio with slightly less tech exposure than the broad indices provide, but we can't ignore the market's biggest and most transformative sector entirely.

Especially because the growth in this case doesn't just meet my criteria; it blows past them. Consensus for Q3 calls for EPS of $1.94 on revenue of $13.06 billion, versus $1.20 and $9.25 billion a year ago. That's YoY growth of nearly 62% and 41%, respectively. The recent drawdown came from a sector selloff, not anything AMD reported, but it shows that if some names in the space get hit, even the larger players are likely to feel it. On Oct. 6, Lisa Su said AMD would substantially increase supply in 2027 because AI orders keep outrunning capacity.

So how do you balance factors we know work for a fast-growing company against a backdrop of cracks in the AI trade? Options.

Owning the stock outright means absorbing a roughly 9% to 11% implied earnings-related move (day of). Earnings are about a week out, and over the past four quarters the smallest move over a similar window was more than 9%, and the largest move, after the company reported its Q1 26 results, was 38%! Of course, that kind of volatility means options aren't cheap, and that rich premium is also a reason not to simply buy calls.

A call spread risk reversal does both jobs: it reduces risk if the cracks widen, and the short options offset the elevated volatility we would otherwise pay for. The bottom chart shows that, by sacrificing about $1,700 of upside for $5,000 in outperformance during a severe downside move, and given how jittery this trade has been lately, this is a way to stay in it with considerably less risk. $5,000 less, specifically. Of course, selling that out will tie up some cash, but less so than simply buying the stock. And selling both the out and the call reduces long call purchase by 66%

Disclosures: Tidal owns/holds all the securities mentioned in the article.

#3005. AI stocks hammered by midterm shock as data center buildout becomes a 'war of attrition'

The Democrats' stunning rout of the Republicans during the midterms this week wasn't just a stinging rebuke of President Donald Trump, but also a potential roadblock to huge plans to build artificial intelligence data centers across the country.

Since the landmark results came in, tech and AI stocks have been jolted by market volatility.

The most valuable tech companies in the U.S. and Europe were hit with a massive sell-off on Thursday as investors got nervous.

Nvidia lost 9.3%, with AMD shedding 17.3%, Microsoft 3.6%, Micron 17.5%, Broadcom 10.4%, Tesla 10.8% and SpaceX 9.1%.

Neoclouds, which rely on their ability to serve AI computing power and are involved in massive data center projects, were also hit particularly hard. Nebius dropped 15%, Iren fell 17% and CoreWeave 10% on Thursday.

By Friday morning, U.S. tech stocks were showing signs of recovery, but the market blip is a warning of potentially rougher roads ahead for the AI boom in America.

"The tumble in tech stocks that took hold in the immediate aftermath of the US midterm elections resumed as investors contemplated what a Democrat-controlled Congress may mean for tech regulation and the overall AI boom," wrote Kyle Rodda, senior financial market analyst at Capital.com in a note on Thursday after the bell.

There were dozens of local ballot measures in 10 states up for a vote on Tuesday that would place restrictions on the construction of data centers in different ways, CNBC reported on Wednesday.

"The regulatory and political risks arise at a time when the proverbial AI trade was already on shakier foundations," said Rodda.

Earnings season had been an "overall disappointment" for the markets, he added, with Magnificent Seven results so far "undershooting growth expectations and flashing warning signs that the pace of AI spending is slowing down."

Now, Rodda wrote, the risk that the investment boom could be slowed further "by measures as potentially disruptive as moratoriums on new data center construction, investors are rushing to the exits on so-called AI trades."

Opposition to data centers in the U.S. is a long-standing issue.

Between January 2024 and May 2026, at least 46 AI data center projects across 20 U.S. states, representing more than $170 billion in announced investment, were blocked, withdrawn or stalled following community opposition, according to research carried out by think tank International Data Center Authority, or IDCA.

The midterms will weigh on investors' minds, Dan Ives, partner at Yorkville Ives & Co., said in a note on Wednesday morning.

"For tech investors the big focus is will these mid-term elections slow the pace of the planned 800 to 1,000 datacenters across the US fueling the AI Revolution…the answer in our view is a clear YES," he wrote.

Democrats taking control, particularly over the Senate, will create some major hurdles for AI over the next two years, Ives added.

The figures floating around on the scale of projected AI infrastructure spending are eye-watering.

Goldman Sachs estimates that AI-related capital spending could total about $7.6 trillion from 2026 through 2031, across computing, data centers and power.

But, heading into the election, AI stocks were already looking shaky on Wall Street.

Chipmakers, neoclouds and other data center-related companies sold off in October on concerns about rising interest rates and overspending. Many hyperscalers have turned cash flow negative as they spend hundreds of billions of dollars on AI infrastructure.

"AI pushback is bipartisan but local, making the buildout pricier rather than stalling it," said Dominique Toublan, head of U.S. credit strategy at Barclays Investment Bank, in a note from Wednesday.

"The AI race shifts from a prisoner's dilemma to a war of attrition: scarce power favors the biggest balance sheets."

By Thursday morning, after speaking to investors over the past 24 hours, Ives said it was a confusing time for Wall Street tech backers trying to "discern the poker game in the Beltway and how this will truly impact the AI Revolution buildout."

"Putting all the pieces together we believe Big Tech capex growth rate now peaks in 2027 vs our previous view that would not happen until 2028/2029," he said.

Chinese AI lab Moonshot turned to Europe for Nvidia Blackwell access amid U.S. China curbs, sources tell CNBC.

CrowdStrike launches AI kill switch that it says can stop internal agents that go rogue.

Meta seeks to raise up to $25 billion in debt sale as AI spending jumps.

Brookfield plans for a $27 billion AI data center at UK power station.

Elon Musk's Tesla and SpaceX lose over $420 billion in combined market cap after midterms.

Blue Origin pushes launch to January 2027 as it races to rebuild damaged launchpad.

Elsewhere in midterm-land, it's not just data centers in the crosshairs.

Democrats' sweep of Congress likely resets the political calculus for crypto, my colleague Tanaya Macheel writes.

The result? It could usher in a more adversarial approach in Washington and push industry-friendly legislation like the Clarity Act to the back burner, she says.

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#3002. Ripple strikes deal with Robinhood to expand its RLUSD digital dollar

Ripple has struck a deal with Robinhood to expand the reach of its digital dollar, RLUSD, strengthening trading and financing links between their institutional businesses and improving Robinhood's internal money management.

The partnership involves using RLUSD to settle institutional trades, automate Robinhood's treasury operations and expand access to digital asset trading and financing through Ripple Prime and Bitstamp, the global crypto exchange acquired by Robinhood last year.

It also helps meet Robinhood's ambition of becoming a global financial superapp, centralizing its customers' financial lives while expanding the underlying infrastructure that can serve both retail and institutional clients.

"Utility is what moves stablecoins forward. Working with Ripple, Bitstamp and Robinhood will have access to RLUSD for institutional settlement, Ripple Treasury for our operations and Ripple Prime for financing," Robinhood Crypto General Manager Johann Kerbrat said in a statement shared with CNBC. "That is what a connected, global financial system looks like."

Stablecoins have long been a workhorse of the crypto world, giving traders a way to move money among exchanges, buy and sell crypto assets, and park funds between trades without having to convert back into traditional currency. But as the crypto industry evolves beyond trading and markets move toward 24/7 liquidity, stablecoins are becoming essential financial infrastructure because of their ability to enable near-instant settlement and move dollar-denominated value at any time, regardless of traditional banking hours.

As firms look to do more with stablecoins beyond simply listing them on exchanges, stablecoin issuers are betting they'll favor a one-stop-shop approach that bundles the technology and services needed to move money, settle trades and manage cash.

"Stablecoins have evolved from a crypto trading tool into a critical piece of financial infrastructure," Ripple President Monica Long told CNBC. "The clearest evidence of this is where they're now being embedded — from corporate payments to capital markets. Our partnership with Robinhood reflects that evolution."

"This is about much more than trading," she added. "It's about fundamentally changing how money moves and is managed — giving institutions the ability to operate 24/7/365, unlocking liquidity and putting capital to work more efficiently."

Circle Internet Group, a Ripple competitor, is positioning itself for a similar future, where winning platforms, in addition to issuing stablecoins, also provide the infrastructure to put them to work. Earlier this year, for example, it launched the Circle Payments Network, connecting financial institutions and payment providers using its flagship USDC stablecoin and other digital assets for cross-border payments.

The network is Circle's answer to the 8-year-old Ripple Payments business, a service that helps businesses move money across borders, using any of several assets moving through that system, including USDC, Ripple-created XRP and, as of its debut in December 2024, RLUSD.

RLUSD's market cap has grown to more than $2.5 billion. Tether's USDT and Circle's USDC still dominate the total stablecoin market, which now exceeds $300 billion.

RLUSD will also be listed on Robinhood's trading platform, and is currently available to traders on Bitstamp as well as Robinhood Chain, the company's home-grown blockchain for tokenized stocks and other assets.

Robinhood, which reports third-quarter results for the three months ended in September after the market closes, rose about 2.5% in early trading Tuesday, snapping a three-day decline.

Correction: A previous headline misspelled RLUSD.

#3000. HSA-eligible health plans in 2027: What to know about tax savings vs. higher deductibles

Healthcare affordability was part of the midterm election cost-of-living debate.

It is uncertain how a new Congress might try to address that pain point. Now, as workers choose their 2027 health insurance coverage, millions face a more immediate decision: whether to pick a plan that lets them contribute to a health savings account, or HSA.

The question is whether an HSA-eligible plan with lower premiums and tax benefits is worth the higher deductible and potential out-of-pocket costs.

One noted advantage of an HSA is its tax benefits.

"You have a triple tax advantage," said Kalei Cadinha-Pua'a, vice chair and CEO of Honolulu-based Cadinha & Co., which ranked No. 21 on this year's CNBC Financial Advisor 100 list.

Employer and employee contributions go into the fund before taxes, earnings in the account aren't taxed while they remain in the HSA, and withdrawals for qualified medical expenses are tax-free.

Read coverage of CNBC's 2026 Financial Advisor 100 list, including stories featuring ranked firms and their advisors:

Financial advisors say employees often overlook another potential tax savings. HSA contributions made through an employer's Section 125 cafeteria plan generally aren't subject to Social Security and Medicare taxes.

That can make payroll contributions more tax-efficient than contributing on your own and claiming a deduction later, said certified financial planner and enrolled agent Andy Panko, the founder of Tenon Financial in Metuchen, New Jersey.

Workers should check how their employer processes contributions and ask if they receive the Social Security and Medicare tax break on HSA contributions.

A worker who chooses to contribute to an HSA outside of payroll may be able to deduct eligible contributions on their federal tax return but generally won't get the break on Social Security and Medicare taxes.

HSAs are typically paired with a high-deductible health plan, so consumers generally have to be enrolled in a high-deductible health plan in order to contribute to the HSA.

Such a plan may have lower premiums relative to others available to consumers, but workers can face higher costs on the back end when they need care.

For 2027, the IRS says an HSA-eligible plan must generally have a deductible of at least $1,750 for individual coverage or $3,500 for family coverage. The plan's annual out-of-pocket expenses, excluding premiums, cannot exceed $8,700 for individual coverage or $17,400 for family coverage.

More employers are offering health plans with an HSA option. Across all U.S. health plan sponsors with at least 50 employees, 63% offer a high-deductible plan that qualifies for an HSA, according to a recent Marsh employer survey. More than a third, 37%, of all employees covered by an employer health plan are enrolled in an HSA-eligible plan.

"Employers often offer an HSA plan as a low-cost option, and then because of the higher deductible and the overall cost-sharing structure, these plans are typically costing less for the employer as well as the employee," said Frank Easley, senior vice president of Aon Health Solutions.

An HSA's tax advantages don't by themselves make an HSA-eligible plan the right choice. Workers should consider how the plan's premiums, deductible and out-of-pocket maximum align with expected healthcare needs before enrolling, financial advisors say.

They should also factor in any employer HSA contributions and the cash they have available to pay an unexpected medical bill before they reach their deductible, says Nathan Lochstampfor, CFP, an associate consultant at Harrisburg, Pennsylvania-based Conrad Siegel Investment Advisors, which is ranked No. 45 on CNBC's Financial Advisor 100 for 2026.

If consumers can't afford to pay the deductible of a high-deductible health plan, it may make sense to choose a plan with higher premiums and lower out-of-pocket costs if they need care, he said.

Lochstampfor recommends clients ask themselves: "If I got in a car accident going home today from work, could I afford that deductible? Or would I be better off maybe paying a little bit of higher premiums until I build up those cash reserves?"

HSA contribution limits for 2027 are $4,500 for individuals and $9,000 for families. People age 55 and older can make a $1,000 catch-up contribution.

You can use an HSA to pay for qualified medical expenses now or later. Any money left in the account generally carries over from year to year, and earnings aren't taxed while they remain in the account. HSA balances can also be invested. As of midyear, 11% of HSA accounts held invested assets, according to Devenir, an HSA investment firm.

Ideally, financial advisors say, you keep some of the balance in cash to cover short-term needs and invest the rest for retirement.

"If you have a long time and you can begin to develop a strategy for savings, it can really work and benefit you in the future when you don't have that earned income to pay for your healthcare needs," Cadinha-Pua'a said.

Some HSA owners pay current medical expenses out of pocket and leave money invested in the account so it can grow tax-free. They can use their HSA funds to reimburse themselves years later for those out-of-pocket expenses. Some conditions apply: It must be a qualified medical expense and incurred after the HSA was established, for example.

That strategy can be smart — but it requires good records.

The IRS wants to make sure expenses aren't double counted. Account holders need to keep records showing that any HSA distributions paid or reimbursed qualified medical expenses weren't also claimed as an itemized deduction.

"Be really honest with yourself," Cadinha-Pua'a said. "Are you a good record keeper?"

She recommends making digital copies of receipts, bills and proof of payment, organizing them in an electronic folder and backing up that folder to the cloud. That can help preserve the documentation if a computer fails or the account holder needs to substantiate a reimbursement years later.

#2997. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.

#2994. Nvidia's Huang is asked to brief Congress on reported Chinese spy relationship

Three House Republican committee leaders asked Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang to sit for a closed-door briefing on his relationship with a tech executive reportedly identified as a Chinese intelligence asset.

"Recent reports raise significant national security concerns regarding the PRC's access to sensitive U.S. technology and information," the GOP lawmakers wrote in a letter to Huang unveiled Thursday, using the acronym for the People's Republic of China.

They are seeking information on Huang's interactions with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, as well as Nvidia's "security protocols against foreign adversary espionage," they wrote in the brief message, which is dated Wednesday.

The letter does not specify which reports it is referring to. But The New York Times reported last week that U.S. intelligence found Alice Huang, a Chinese citizen determined to be funneling information to Beijing, had "developed a close relationship" with Jensen Huang.

President Donald Trump, who himself has forged close ties with Jensen Huang as he champions the U.S. artificial intelligence industry, was briefed about a year ago on those intelligence findings, the Times reported.

Trump "chose not to do anything with the information," according to the newspaper, which cited interviews with people briefed on the surveillance and other sources.

The letter to Huang was signed by House Foreign Affairs Committee Chairman Brian Mast, R-Fla., Select Committee on China Chairman John Moolenaar, R-Mich., and House Permanent Select Committee on Intelligence Chairman Rick Crawford, R-Ark.

Nvidia did not directly respond when asked by CNBC to comment on the letter and confirm whether Huang would attend the lawmakers' proposed briefing.

Nvidia instead provided a statement that largely mirrored the one it previously sent the Times, in which a spokesperson suggested Jensen Huang's interactions with Alice Huang were no different from his meetings with other executive clients.

"Our regional cloud customers receive ordinary business information — not sensitive NVIDIA trade secrets—and zero information relevant to national security," the spokesperson said in the statement.

"Meetings with customers are part of a CEO's job; Jensen talks with thousands of customer executives every year," the spokesperson said. "We're confident that if the U.S. government believes that any person is a threat to American industry, it would let us know."

A spokeswoman for Mast did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for additional information. Megaspeed did not immediately respond to an email seeking comment.

The lawmakers invited the Nvidia CEO to appear for an in-person joint briefing on Nov. 18. Nvidia is scheduled to report its third-quarter earnings on Nov. 17.

"China's access to that sensitive U.S. tech is core to the investigative work of our three committees this Congress," the committee leaders wrote. "It is vital that Congress determines the extent to which U.S. national security has been placed at risk."

#2993. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2986. Neocloud IREN says $4 billion in annualized revenue being delayed due to customer request

Following a 26% post-election plunge in its stock price, neocloud IREN said in its earnings report on Thursday that over $4 billion in annual recurring revenue projected to land in 2026 has been pushed out to at least January.

IREN said the move, which could extend payment to February, is due to "a customer-requested change and supplier timing."

On the company's earnings call, IREN co-CEO Daniel Roberts said Microsoft is the customer.

"They made a request to reconfigure and upgrade some of the network infrastructure and the purpose of that was really to enable the 200 megawatts of capacity to service these latest-generation frontier models," Roberts said.

Shares of IREN rose in extended trading, but that comes after a 55% drop over the past month.

The company said revenue climbed 66% from the prior quarter to $228 million, beating the $221 million average analyst estimate, according to LSEG. AI Cloud revenue more than doubled to $200 million. IREN gets the rest of its revenue from bitcoin mining, which was its primary business before the company pivoted to AI.

IREN's net loss narrowed to $263 million from $684 million the prior period. Year-ago figures weren't included in the earnings release.

The company is being closely watched by investors as the AI trade shows signs of unwinding, particularly following the Democratic sweep in Tuesday's midterm elections. Concerns are mounting that cash-burning companies reliant on debt are going to have a hard time meeting their obligations as interest rates rise and lenders back out of the market. The Nasdaq had its worst day on Thursday since April 2025.

"Capital markets have become more selective in the last few weeks, and the questions have undoubtedly moved from how big the contract is to whether it can be delivered, financed, and run at a profit," Roberts said on the call.

CoreWeave, which is set to report earnings on Tuesday, has plunged 60% in the past month. Nebius, which reports next Thursday, is down 48% over that stretch.

IREN said revenue backlog increased 22% from the prior period to $20.3 billion, with "approximately two-thirds with investment-grade counterparties." The $4 billion ARR target is fully contracted and funded, the company said.

"There is a disconnect between what we're seeing in public market and what we're seeing at the customer level," Roberts told CNBC's Seema Mody.

Still, Iren said it planed to shell out $15 billion in capital expenditures the rest of this fiscal year, including $9 billion in cash and committed financing, and $6 billion in "target" financing, which is "subject to market conditions."

The neocloud said that prices for its compute continue to rise and that it expanded an existing contract with a leading frontier AI lab. It said it has contracts with Nvidia, Microsoft and 13 other customers.

Roberts said that some data center construction in the coming year would be backed by Nvidia and Microsoft, but about a third would be to companies that are willing to offer large prepayments for the capacity.

"We just had a customer agree to fund 90% of the GPU capex," Roberts said, referring to graphics processing units.

IREN said that community opposition can be addressed by tapping local suppliers, using water efficiently and creating jobs.

"I think whether it's Texas, whether it's Australia, whether it's Spain, everywhere, all we're seeing is people wanting to ensure that data centers pay their own way and do the right thing, and it it feels completely reasonable," Roberts said.

IREN said it deliberately hadn't yet bought all of the GPUs it needs for next year to reduce risk.

—CNBC's Isabel O'Brien contributed to this report.

#2985. Coinbase net income surges 66% in third quarter, benefiting from bitcoin investment gains

Coinbase Global earned third-quarter adjusted net income of $719 million, up 66% from $433 million a year ago, largely due to a recovery in the cryptocurrency market.

Coinbase said it earned $2.51 a share in the latest quarter, although it wasn't immediately clear if that compared with the 6-cent-per-share profit that Wall Street analysts had estimated. Revenue of $1.20 billion trailed the $1.233 billion expected by analysts polled by LSEG.

Net income at the San Francisco-based platform surged thanks to the value of its volatile cryptocurrency investment portfolio, which can swing wildly even when the composition of the underlying holdings are little changed.

The third quarter value of Coinbase's crypto investment portfolio climbed due to a resurgence in the digital asset market. The price of bitcoin — often used as a gauge for all crypto assets — rose 39% in the three-month period ended Sept. 30 and the flagship cryptocurrency recently changed hands at roughly $80,809.

In the third quarter, Coinbase deepened its push into non-crypto products and services, including artificial intelligence integrations for traders, tokenized real-world assets and prediction markets.

In July, VP of product Max Branzburg told CNBC that Coinbase would debut an "everything exchange" spotlighting tokenized stocks and other real-world assets, derivatives trading, prediction markets and early-stage token sales. The trading platform also introduced Coinbase for Agents in early October, supporting users that want to use ChatGPT, Muse or other AI agents to manage their portfolios or research and trade various assets.

Coinbase also expanded its prediction market offerings, making a wider range of event contracts available to users.

In the third quarter, prediction markets revenue grew to more than $200 million on an annual basis, while the number of traders on those markets increased 60% compared with the second quarter.

#2983. A long-term Elon Musk backer is cashing out of Tesla — and cutting its SpaceX exposure

A key early investor has sold the last of its Tesla shares and sharply reduced its SpaceX holding, taking profits from two of its most successful long-term bets.

Scottish Mortgage Investment Trust, a London-listed closed-end fund managed by Baillie Gifford, said Friday that SpaceX represented 11.1% of its total assets as of Wednesday, down from 15% at Sept. 30.

The position helped drive Scottish Mortgage's 31.5% net asset value return in the six months to the end of September. In its half-year results statement, Scottish Mortgage also said it sold its remaining Tesla stake in April, ending an investment that dated back to 2013.

Scottish Mortgage first invested in SpaceX in December 2018, when Elon Musk's rocket company had a reported valuation of about $31 billion, and committed roughly $200 million through 2021.

The investment surged in value as SpaceX expanded its launch business and Starlink satellite network. By the time of its blockbuster IPO in June, SpaceX represented roughly a quarter of Scottish Mortgage's portfolio by value.

The investment trust has since sold shares as post-IPO lock-up restrictions have lifted in stages, though it said its conviction in SpaceX remains high.

"The decision to reduce the position following its flotation, while retaining a substantial investment, demonstrates the importance of balancing conviction with disciplined portfolio management," Scottish Mortgage said.

SpaceX was a major contributor to Scottish Mortgage's performance during the period, adding 8 percentage points to returns.

The trust's net asset value, or NAV, delivered a total return of 31.5% in the six months through Sept. 30, compared with a 16% return for the FTSE All-World Index. Its share price returned 33.8%.

Proceeds from the SpaceX sales also helped Scottish Mortgage repay borrowings and reduce gearing.

The Tesla sale closes another highly profitable bet for Scottish Mortgage, which invests globally in a mix of public and private high-growth companies, typically with a longer-term investment view.

In a portfolio update in July, the fund said it had invested around $470 million in Tesla, and generated approximately $6.2 billion in realized profit over 13 years.

"Since we first invested in 2013, it has returned 13 times the capital invested, but we felt the share price had come to reflect much of its promise in autonomy and robotics," Scottish Mortgage said on Friday.

The trust also pointed to intensifying competition in Tesla's core automotive business, particularly in China.

Shares in SpaceX and Tesla fell about 9% and 11%, respectively, on Thursday, taking their combined market value more than $420 billion below Tuesday’s close. Investors fear Elon Musk could face new congressional probes after the U.S. midterms, potentially exposing his companies to tighter regulation.

Scottish Mortgage is one of several U.K. investment trusts — comparable to closed-end funds in the U.S. — that were early backers of SpaceX.

Another Baillie Gifford fund, Edinburgh Worldwide Investment Trust, was the focus of a bitter activist campaign by Boaz Weinstein's hedge fund Saba Capital in 2025, with its SpaceX holding becoming a key flashpoint in the long-running battle.

#2981. $6.3 billion Binance hack is largest crypto theft on record

Binance said Friday that a security incident impacted about $6.3 billion in client assets, with around $3 billion frozen on-chain, marking the largest crypto hack on record.

The company later added it began recovering assets impacted by the breach after pledging to cover losses using its Secure Asset Fund for Users, which the company created in 2018 as a protection for clients in the event of a hack.

It wasn't clear how the attack took place. The world's largest crypto exchange initially said the hack may involve "a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop, which allowed an attacker to gain access to a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions." However, Co-CEO Richard Teng later said the attack involved a more complex process rather than the misuse of a single engineer's permissions.

The FBI said Friday it was aware of the incident and was in touch with "relevant public and private partners to understand the scope of the activity, evaluate ​potential impacts, ​and ⁠coordinate appropriate investigative steps."

It wasn't clear either who was responsible for the breach. But TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence and AI investigations company, pointed to North Korean hackers as the potential culprits. "Several features of this attack match earlier North Korea-linked heists, including the Bybit hack and the KelpDAO hack in April 2026: small Tornado Cash withdrawals funding an operator wallet, staging that began well before the theft, and signatures obtained in advance then executed in one burst," the company said in a post.

Blockchain data platform Chainalysis also said this appears "be the work of North Korean cybercrime group TraderTraitor. This group has repeatedly targeted the cryptocurrency industry, exchanges, DeFi protocols, and investors, to raise funds for the regime. We base our intelligence assessment on on-chain activity observed in the hours following the hack."

Binance temporarily suspended withdrawals while it overhauls its withdrawal security systems and has revoked the affected address authorizations, Teng said. The exchange is also working with law enforcement and blockchain intelligence firms to trace and recover the stolen funds, while deposits remain unaffected.

Binance co-founder Yi He said deposits remained unaffected and continued to operate normally, while the exchange was working on enhancements to its withdrawal service and related address controls. It expects to begin resuming withdrawal services within the next 48 hours.

"We have found no evidence of any wallet private key leak. We will share a detailed technical post-mortem once the investigation is complete and the facts are fully verified," Binance said.

The announcement followed moves by several crypto firms, including Circle Internet Group, Tether and Ripple, to freeze funds tied to the hack.

Exploits are not uncommon in the crypto space. In 2025, Bybit suffered roughly $1.5 billion in losses due to a hack. More recently, Bitget lost more than $350 million worth of digital assets in a cyber theft incident. Over the past several years, bad actors have also drained billions of dollars in crypto from decentralized exchanges.

This week's breach "proves our exact point in proposing regulations for crypto markets earlier this month," Michael Selig, chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, said Friday in a CNBC "Squawk on the Street" interview. The U.S. derivatives regulator on Oct. 5 proposed a framework that would effectively put crypto exchanges under federal government oversight — after Congress failed to approve legislation to regulate the asset.

"The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don't have the same sorts of safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States. We want to offer a federal option to these exchanges to come and register with the CFTC, and they would be subject to the same sorts of cyber security safeguards that we have in our markets today," he said.

Bitcoin fell more than 4% to $77,187.60 on Friday as the hack weighed on crypto markets.

Circle CEO Jeremy Allaire said Thursday evening in a social media post that the company had frozen more than 800 million in USD Coin associated with the incident. USDT stablecoin issuer Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino also said in on X that his firm had frozen more than 1.5 billion of its stablecoin that may have been related to a potential breach.

Jack McDonald, Ripple's senior vice president of stablecoins, said it was actively freezing affected funds held in RLUSD, its U.S. dollar-backed stablecoin.

#2979. Could CRISPR cure rare diseases before birth? Scientists are getting closer

One of the keys to curing a rare disease is treating patients early. But what if you treated them before they were born?

Over the past decade, several researchers have pursued this provocative idea. Now, they are starting to make meaningful progress in fetal gene editing, an approach that holds the potential to stop genetic disorders before it's too late.

"If you really want to prevent the worst ravages of the disease, you can't wait until they're born," said Kiran Musunuru, a gene editing expert at the University of Pennsylvania. "You've got to go in utero."

The futuristic technique is benefiting from new advances in prenatal diagnostics and CRISPR, the Nobel Prize-winning DNA editing tool. Other scientists pursuing the idea include David Stitelman at the Yale School of Medicine, Tippi MacKenzie at University of California, San Francisco, and Aijun Wang, a biomedical engineer at UC Davis.

For more stories like this, sign up for the CNBC Cures newsletter.

Musunuru made headlines is 2025 when he helped develop the world's first personalized gene editing treatment for an infant with a rare genetic disorder. But long before that, he began studying the potential for fetal gene editing with William Peranteau, a co-director of the Center for Fetal Research at the Children's Hospital of Philadelphia.

Progress had been slow. But the pair have discovered something they were not expecting.

After babies are born, their bodies' blood vessels erect barriers to help them breathe on their own. That has made it difficult to deliver gene-editing treatments to organs beyond the liver. As a result, scientists are limited in the types of diseases that they can go after.

But before babies are born, it's a different story. To their surprise, Musunuru and Peranteau found that CRISPR's molecular scissors can reach the heart and other hard-to-reach muscles of unborn animals. Their discovery could open the door to fixing genetic muscle disorders like Duchenne muscular dystrophy before the damage is done.

At Children's Hospital of Philadelphia, Peranteau is part of a team that has performed surgeries on fetuses with birth defects like spina bifida, where the spinal cord is exposed during birth. The mother goes under anesthesia while the surgeon opens up the uterus and operates on the fetus to correct the birth defect. Afterward, the unborn baby continues to develop until ready to be born.

Fetal gene editing would be less invasive than that, Peranteau said. It would be similar to how doctors routinely treat unborn babies diagnosed with fetal anemia by performing a blood transfusion through the umbilical vein.

Musunuru and Peranteau have seen promising early results in animals. But they are at least three years away from trying the approach in humans and careful to temper expectations, worrying that they might give families false hope. While the scientists believe they could treat heart and muscle disorders, getting past the blood-brain barrier still remains difficult. That means, for now, neurological conditions are off the table.

The scientists plan to make edits to what are called somatic cells. They want to steer clear of editing reproductive cells, an approach that has become one of science's biggest controversies. To many researchers, editing those cells crosses an ethical line because it can affect future generations and lead to so-called "designer babies" whose parents could modify their genes to make their descendants taller or faster. After a scientist revealed in 2018 that he had edited embryos of twin girls to protect them from HIV, a Chinese court sentenced him to three years in prison. Despite the controversy, some researchers are studying DNA editing of embryos.

Editing the DNA of a fetus has not been controversial. And new breakthroughs are bringing it closer to reality. Scientists can deliver CRISPR components by infusing trillions of microscopic fat bubbles known as lipid nanoparticles into a patient's bloodstream. The tiny containers – which hold the tools to fix DNA misspellings and guide them to the right place – are considered safer than the older method of ferrying the drug through the body inside the shell of an inactivated virus.

Meanwhile, diagnostic companies are offering "non-invasive prenatal testing," promising to identify whether a fetus is at risk of a genetic disease by testing the mother's blood. The approach is seen as less risky than screening a fetus for conditions like Down syndrome by guiding a thin, hollow needle through the mother's abdomen, known as amniocentesis.

"People used to ask `Can you even diagnose any of these things before birth?'" said Stitelman, the Yale professor who has studied fetal gene editing for over a decade. "But the technology to make these diagnoses is improving. It seems less far-fetched now than it did when I started."

For Musunuru and Peranteau, the hardest question ahead may be which diseases to tackle first. They are looking at disorders that start before birth and do not have effective treatments. They want to focus on conditions "where there's a good scientific and biological rationale to doing it before birth," Peranteau said.

"I want to be efficient and develop these therapies as quickly as possible," Peranteau said. "But more importantly, we need to make sure we're doing it in a safe and ethically responsible way."

CNBC Cures is underwritten by Alexion, AstraZeneca Rare Disease and BridgeBio.

#2978. Haaland has the final say as Manchester City survive Leipzig fightback

Looking to extend their unbeaten home run, RB Leipzig welcomed Manchester City to Saxony for the fourth outing of their Champions League campaign.

Meanwhile, City, still engulfed in legal trouble, arrived hoping to return to winning ways after back-to-back Premier League defeats to Aston Villa and Brighton.

It was the hosts who made the livelier start, with Antonio Nusa providing the first meaningful effort of the encounter in the 15th minute. Cutting in from the right, the Norwegian unleashed a powerful strike from the edge of the box, though Gianluigi Donnarumma remained unfazed as the ball flew straight at him.

From there, Leipzig took control, with Nusa at the heart of proceedings as Martín Demichelis' side pinned City inside their own half for the best part of 15 minutes.

Christoph Baumgartner, Brajan Gruda and David Raum all tried their luck, while City had to wait until the 36th minute for their first effort since the early stages, with Phil Foden digging out a shot from the edge of the box following a wonderful first touch.

Yet, a matter of seconds later, Manchester City took the lead completely against the run of play.

Latching onto a long ball to the left, Nico O'Reilly sent the ball back across the box, where it failed to find a teammate. Instead, it reached the unfortunate Castello Lukeba, whose momentum left him helpless as he prodded the ball beyond Maarten Vandevoordt and into his own net.

The visitors may have edged the possession statistics, but it was Leipzig who had every reason to feel hard done by as the half-time whistle sounded.

In years gone by, this would be the point where we wax lyrical about a Manchester City side who overcame a difficult start to produce one of those marvellous midweek performances that so many across the continent have fallen victim to.

Leipzig know that feeling better than most, having conceded 22 goals in seven meetings with the 2023 European champions. This time, however, the Red Bulls had other ideas.

Demichelis' side came flying out of the blocks after the restart, with Baumgartner once again lifting the crowd with an ambitious effort from distance.

The early pressure was soon rewarded, as Leipzig found themselves level less than three minutes into the second half.

Having fallen behind in such unfortunate circumstances, there was perhaps a touch of justice in the manner of their equaliser, as Ridle Baku's attempted low cross somehow found its way into the bottom corner, catching Donnarumma off his line as the Italian anticipated a delivery into the middle.

Gruda, whose late winner against PSV had already made him a Champions League hero in his debut campaign, was pulling the strings down the right, while Demichelis turned to Christopher Nkunku from the bench, a player with a hattrick to his name against City in 2022.

Yet, for all their control, Leipzig once again found themselves staring at a deficit.

City had barely threatened since the restart, but in the 58th minute, Iliman Ndiaye delivered a wonderful ball for Enzo Fernández, who powered home to restore the visitors' advantage.

For the second time, Leipzig had been punished despite looking the better side. And for the second time, they had an answer, though their first deficit had lasted just under 10 minutes of playing time. This time around, they needed only three.

Gruda combined with Ezechiel Banzuzi on the edge of the area before substitute Nkunku picked out Nusa, who drilled an emphatic finish into the net from the left side of the box.

Having tormented City's defence from the opening exchanges, the Norwegian finally had the goal his performance deserved.

Leipzig were level again at 2-2, and City, for all their experience on the European stage, were finding this particular trip to Saxony anything but straightforward.

But for all their efforts, Leipzig found themselves undone once again, as Erling Haaland slid home to restore City's lead with just over 10 minutes remaining.

The Norwegian had been unusually quiet on the night, but if there is one lesson to be learned from City's aforementioned European exploits, it is that their towering frontman can never truly be written off.

Elliot Anderson, who had tried his luck only moments earlier, powered down the right before fizzing a dangerous ball towards the back post, where Haaland slid in to guide it into a virtually empty net for his fifth in three Champions League outings this season.

Leipzig skipper Raum and City midfielder Rayan Cherki both rattled the crossbar as the chances began to flow in the final quarter of an hour, and the hosts were not out of the clash just yet.

Four minutes of additional time were signalled, and Leipzig were handed one final opportunity when substitute Allan needlessly conceded a free-kick out on the left. But despite teasing deliveries from Raum and Gruda, City stood firm, before Jeremy Doku lifted an effort over the bar at the other end.

In the end, this was hardly a vintage Manchester City performance on the European stage. For long spells, Leipzig had been the better side, and twice they had fought their way back into the contest.

Yet, as has so often been the case on the continent, City found a way to win, condemning Demichelis to his first home defeat in charge of the club.

#2976. Outside groups urge congressional Dems to target corporate interests over Trump

As Democrats prepare to take back the gavels in key investigative committees in the next Congress, outside groups are urging them to use their oversight resources to focus more on the cost of living and corporate actors than on President Donald Trump.

Such an approach could resonate more deeply with voters, according to several groups and individuals who have been drafting oversight plans and advising members of Congress. Democrats are expected to launch myriad investigations into companies that have done business with the Trump administration in the technology, cryptocurrency and prediction market industries when they assume power in January.

"We were pretty worried that we were going to repeat the same mistake of 2019, where Democrats came with a big new majority ... and they spent a year-plus on impeachment," Alex Jacquez, a senior vice president of policy, advocacy and research at Groundwork Collaborative, a progressive think tank, told CNBC on Thursday.

"The issue never really resonated for people. Donald Trump was more popular the day that impeachment ended in the Senate than he was when it started in the House," he said.

Several Democratic-aligned groups and strategists expressed similar sentiments to CNBC. They're urging Democrats — who won sizable majorities in both chambers and have an axe to grind against the Trump administration — to show restraint. They should avoid the mistake of spending too much time trying to remove the president from office and stay focused on affordability, Jacquez and others said.

"Voters don't want us to investigate just for the sake of political vendettas. It's not healthy for us as a democracy. It plays into a lot of what Trump has done himself," said Lucas Holtz, a senior political advisor at Third Way, a center-left think tank, which he said has been in close contact with Democratic leaders on oversight and other aspects of their congressional agenda. "Democrats hopefully have learned lessons from Trump and will responsibly investigate in a way that connects back to cost of living."

That strategy is supported by recent polling conducted by Groundwork and polling firm Hart Research that was shared exclusively with CNBC.

It found that 2 in 3 voters think investigations of corporate greed should be a high priority, more than the 58% who believe Congress should prioritize Trump's personal corruption.

And in focus groups conducted by the think tank, Jacquez said there was palpable anger aimed at CEOs and corporate leaders who voters feel have ripped them off.

"A lot of people were pessimistic … that oversight would result in anything, and so they want restitution. They want CEOs to go to jail. They want people to resign. They want prices to be lowered," Jacquez said.

"In the focus groups in particular, I was pretty shocked at how much jail time came up for CEOs," he added.

Whether Democrats heed that advice remains to be seen.

House and Senate leaders are rolling out the foundations of their legislative and investigative agendas, and some presumptive chairs of key committees have flicked at who and what they'll target. They've so far laid out a sprawling hit list, which includes Trump, his family, Cabinet officials, big banks and tech companies.

For one, Sen. Ron Wyden of Oregon is expected to lead the Senate Finance Committee and has already set the table for an ambitious oversight agenda.

A person familiar with the matter told CNBC on Thursday that the committee would probe JPMorgan Chase's banking relationship with the late sex offender Jeffrey Epstein, and in particular decisions made by Mary Callahan Erdoes, the CEO of JPMorgan's asset and wealth management division. The person spoke on condition of anonymity because the probe of the nation's biggest bank has not been announced.

The news comes a day after Wyden said in a statement that the panel's top priorities "will be affordability for us, accountability for them."

Wyden said the committee would double down on its efforts to make sure billionaires pay their fair share in taxes, address the "corporate chokehold on our nation's health care system" and reassert congressional authority over trade. He also vowed to target the "special interests" that had cozied up to Trump in the past two years.

Presumptive House Judiciary Committee Chair Jamie Raskin, D-Md., also listed Epstein as a potential source for probes, specifically the Department of Justice's alleged efforts to cover up his human trafficking operation. Raskin also said the committee could probe Trump's $1.8 billion "lawfare fund"; the president's attempt to pardon himself, his family and their businesses; and violations of the emoluments clause by Trump and his Cabinet, which prohibits federal officials from accepting gifts from foreign states.

Another person familiar with Democrats' oversight plans, who was not authorized to speak publicly, told CNBC the strategy is to aggressively target business while also taking a more traditional watchdog role of the executive branch.

Any business that has affiliations with the Trump administration could come into the crosshairs, the person said.

Democrats believe they have a mandate after the election to conduct aggressive investigations into industries like tech, prediction markets and cryptocurrency — all of which have kowtowed to or benefited from the Trump administration over the past two years, the person said. Indeed, the CNBC Voter Poll showed voters did respond resoundingly to Democrats' anti-corruption and affordability message that put tech leaders in the spotlight. The poll was a collaborative effort across several news organizations that drew on about 100,000 interviews conducted by phone, online and in person at polling locations with a margin of error of 0.6%.

Tech companies have publicly embraced the Trump administration, attending and donating to the president's inauguration and, in some cases, giving gifts to the president. The White House has been hands off with artificial intelligence while encouraging the buildout of data centers — a massive infrastructure gambit that is now in jeopardy after data centers proved a loser at the ballot box.

Cryptocurrency and prediction markets, two relatively new players, are also going to be under the microscope, the person said. The crypto investigations will be led by Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., the incoming chair of the Senate Banking Committee and a known critic of the industry that has dumped millions of dollars into electoral politics.

Another wrinkle for crypto is the return of Sen.-elect Sherrod Brown, D-Ohio, who lost his seat in 2024 to Sen. Bernie Moreno, R-Ohio, after crypto spent millions to have him defeated. Brown was elected Tuesday to Ohio's other Senate seat.

The Trump family has also been intertwined with prediction market companies while making billions on crypto ventures.

The second person said the oversight strategy will both address the perception that companies hold undue influence with the government and benefit from it and will deter the companies from pursuing corrupt behavior or rolling over for the administration.

Democrats expect companies will try to do everything in their power to cooperate, given that the election results were so heavily in their favor, the person said. Helping their cause is that companies cannot avoid congressional subpoenas, and a company itself cannot be pardoned.

The oversight itself will also serve as a means to an end, the person said, publicly surfacing information that could later materially impact a company.

Former Federal Trade Commission Chair Lina Khan echoed that point in a post to X on Thursday.

"Why should Congressional Democrats investigate corporate America if their mandate is to deliver real economic change? Because investigations are a key catalyst for securing reform and legislation," Khan said.

She highlighted the work of the House Judiciary Committee during Trump's first term, which she said conducted an "18-month investigation into Amazon, Apple, Facebook, and Google, securing key evidence of how their business practices were undermining competition and harming people, setting the basis for both lawsuits and proposed legislation."
