The Jacksonville Jaguars weathered the storm in Baltimore, and the Ravens had no answer for the elements or Anthony Campanile's defense. In a rainy Thursday night showdown, Jacksonville completely smothered Baltimore, turning a hostile road environment into a blowout victory.

Here's everything you need to know from Jacksonville's Week 9 33-10 road win over the Baltimore Ravens.

Mr. Consensus Boards, tight end Nate Boerkircher, adjusting on the fly to Trevor Lawrence's extended scramble, was exactly what Jacksonville needed to get the game started early.

After three home blowout victories in the 2026 season, the Jacksonville Jaguars put together their first multi-score victory away from Duval County. A looming question heading into this matchup was whether Liam Coen's squad could replicate their clear home dominance (+85-point win differential) when on the road. On Thursday, they answered this call, and then some.

Notably, a passer rating for simply hiking and spiking the ball earns a 39.6. To put this performance in context: Baltimore wide receiver Zay Flowers caught just one more Fagnano pass attempt than the Jacksonville defense. Nate Boerkircher – 2 catches for 61 yards

However, the offensive limitations were clear. Anthony Campanile's defense held Baltimore to 2 of 6 on third down (30%) with a 3.3 yard average per play.

Time of possession: 17:31 to 12:29 Baltimore

Redzone Offense: 1/2 (50%) Jacksonville to 0% (0 attempts for Baltimore)

Linebacker Foye Oluokun secured the game's only sack, while wide receiver Travis Hunter surrendered Jacksonville's lone turnover, a third-quarter fumble. However, the defense held Baltimore without points following the turnover, turning it over on downs.

#2974. The midterm election could reshape the muni bond market. Here’s what investors need to know

The outcome of Tuesday's election is expected to have broad implications across the market, including municipal bonds.

The spotlight has been on the battle for congressional control and whether Democrats will wrest the House of Representatives, the Senate or both from Republicans. But for the muni market, what happens at the state level could have bigger repercussions — at least in the near term.

There is an array of initiatives on ballots around the nation that could change tax laws and add to bond supply. Taxes matter to muni investors since the income received is free of federal tax, as well as state tax if the holder lives in the state where the bond is issued.

Depending on the location, ballot measures are up for approval that would either provide tax breaks, or raise taxes on the wealthy.

"It's a complete bifurcation between red and blue states," said Dan Close, head of municipals at Nuveen. "Blue states are trying to put in place graduated income tax structures, trying to capture more revenue from high-income earners. Red states are promoting affordability by trying to cut property taxes."

Red states are those that predominantly vote Republican, while blue states swing toward Democrats.

For instance, California has a proposition known as the "billionaire tax," which would impose a one-time levy equal to 5% of the wealth of certain taxpayers with at least $1 billion in assets. The state also has a measure on the ballot that would make a temporary tax hike permanent on individuals earning over $371,000, adjusted annually for inflation. The tax is currently set to expire in 2031.

"The impact to the muni bond market will be to make the exemption more valuable," Close said of the possible passage of tax increases. Investors will likely seek shelter in the muni market, which would send bond prices higher, he noted.

It is a different story in Florida, where voters are set to decide whether to increase the Sunshine State's homestead exemption for non-school district property taxes. Qualified homeowners currently have a $25,000 property tax exemption, as well as another for $25,000 that doesn't apply to school property taxes. The new proposal raises the latter exemption to $150,000 in 2027 and $250,000 by 2028. New residents have a five-year waiting period before receiving the benefit.

If passed, some local governments will have to find other revenue sources or increase the overall tax rate to replace what they've lost, said Tom Kozlik, head of public policy and municipal strategy at HilltopSecurities.

"It's not necessarily going to result in downgrades for sure, but there would have to be some balancing with regard to overall revenues," he said.

Not all municipalities will be hurt equally, which means credit research is important, said Close.

"Like everything in municipal finance, it is really on a case-by-case basis that you could have some credit deterioration from [a] rollback of property taxes," he said.

Other voters on Tuesday will be asked to approve more state or local government debt. That could weigh on a market that has already seen heightened supply.

Some $473.8 billion has already been issued year to date, through September, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association. That's a 7.8% increase over the same period a year ago. In 2025, total muni issuance reached $587.3 billion, 14.3% higher than 2024, Sifma said.

Cooper Howard, director of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research, believes supply is the biggest issue coming out of the election.

"That amount of issuance has been one of the reasons why you have to see that strong amount of demand continue to keep pace with it for total returns to keep up," Howard said.

Fortunately, new supply doesn't necessarily come quickly into the market. "There's a window over a couple years that they can issue," he noted.

The ultimate makeup of the next Congress could also have consequences for muni investors as lawmakers sort out their priorities. Most notable is what lawmakers will do to tackle the country's affordability problem, Kozlik at Hilltop said.

"There could be some tax policy initiatives and even affordability-related initiatives that manifest themselves in the first quarter of next year," he said. "That could impact housing. It could impact higher education. It could impact public power financing."

Investors considering moves before the election may want to look at those states with tax increases on the ballot, said Close at Nuveen.

For instance, Nuveen recently began offering a separately managed account dedicated to Washington after getting an enormous number of inquiries from financial advisors. The state recently enacted a 9.9% tax on individuals with taxable annual income above $1 million, set to go into effect in 2028. But the issue is now being put to voters, who can decide whether to repeal the new law.

"At the margin, if you do think that some of these measures are going to pass, knowing that bonds usually move up with an increase in taxes, there might be some opportunities to buy ahead of the election for blue states," Close said.

Howard believes munis are attractive right now, particularly for those who are in a tax bracket of 24% or above. Fundamentals are good and the bonds largely have good credit quality, he said. "About 70% of the broad muni index is either triple-A or double-A rated."

Kozlik is also bullish, calling yields "really attractive." He suggests investors concentrate on AA-rated or higher bonds.

"The muni space is part of the portfolio that most investors want," he said. "It helps them sleep well at night."

#2973. Justin Jefferson says his frustration came from "wanting to win"

Vikings receiver Justin Jefferson engaged in a rare display of frustration late in Sunday's 19-15 loss to the Lions. He also didn't talk to reporters after the game, possibly to avoid saying things that would underscore what he was feeling in the moment.

Four days later, Jefferson spoke to the media — and the No. 1 topic was his apparent mood during Minnesota's first loss of the season.

"It was wanting to win," Jefferson said, via Kevin Seifert of ESPN, "and I felt like we had plenty of opportunities to win that game and we only hurt ourselves in multiple ways. It's just wanting better from the whole entire offense especially. And I feel like the potential and the guys that we have in that room is something very, very special. I feel like we're wasting that if not going out there executing the plays that we have called and just not living to that standard that we feel like we live up to every single day."

Jefferson, who always says all the right things about his quarterbacks, said all the right things about Kyler Murray, whose performance against Detroit largely contributed to the failure to score more than 15 points.

"I feel like Kyler has continued to grow," Jefferson said. "I feel like Kyler is continuing to get comfortable with this offense. And I feel like the plays that are there are plays that he's well capable of making. And those are plays that we've seen him make multiple times throughout the season, throughout practice and in training camp as well. So we definitely have faith in him. We have confidence in him to go out there and be that guy that we need him and want him to be.

"But all in all, it's just executing our plays and just coming ready to play. And I feel like once he's back out there and he gets more comfortable with the system, more comfortable with just throwing to these different type of receivers, then I feel like he's going to look a lot better in [reporters'] eyes. But we see that every single day. We see that type of Kyler that we need out there on Sundays every single day. So just bringing that on Sundays. We're behind him. We're confident in him, so we just going to keep riding with him."

For now, the Vikings will ride with Carson Wentz, while Murray is out with a hamstring injury. And Wentz has proven that, of all the quarterbacks who have played for the Vikings since Sam Darnold left for Seattle after the 2024 season, Wentz knows how to get the ball to Jefferson.

Soft bench or not, Wentz has an opportunity to stay on the field if he makes the plays that Murray didn't on Sunday. Plays that, by the time the game was on the line, coach Kevin O'Connell didn't allow Murray to try to make.

The one moment that resonates from Sunday's game wasn't anything Murray did or failed to do. It came from O'Connell's decision to punt from the Minnesota 33-yard line on fourth and five with 3:01 to play. Given the manner in which the Lions were running the ball, the likelihood of getting another opportunity to score a touchdown was slim.

Put simply, if O'Connell didn't trust Murray to gain five yards with the game on the line, O'Connell shouldn't entrust the starting job to Murray.

For now, Murray technically can't play due to injury. It's hard not to wonder whether, if Murray had made the plays needed to outscore the Lions on Sunday, he'd be playing on Monday night despite the injured hamstring.

#2971. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2970. 'The whole landscape changed': Gaming CEOs see new opportunities post-midterms

Midterm election results have created new openings for U.S. gambling expansion, while raising the prospect of greater political and legal pressure on prediction markets, according to gaming industry executives.

"The whole landscape changed," said Craig Billings, CEO of Wynn Resorts, during the company's earnings call Thursday.

Wynn has been closely watchng developments in the Middle East as it looks to open the United Arab Emirates' first legal casino in 2027. Billings said he suspects the election will have an impact on the "likely trajectory of the conflict in the region."

Closer to home, Billings sees opportunities in Texas. And he's not the only one. On the heels of reporting record third-quarter revenue and raising guidance for its brick-and-mortar business, Penn Entertainment CEO Jay Snowden called out specific opportunities made possible by state leadership changes.

Election Day was "a very good day overall for the industry, and certainly for Penn," Snowden said on Thursday's earnings call.

Texas, in particular, could be a jackpot for the casino industry. In a massive upset, Democrat Vikki Goodwin defeated longtime Lt. Gov. Dan Patrick, who has been persistent in blocking gambling expansion in the Lone Star State.

"Texas is a fascinating market," Billings said, explaining how many consumers travel to Oklahoma to gamble at tribal casions. "So it's kind of a stealth market that is giant."

Penn has been building a position in Texas for years, Snowden said. It operates two of the state's three Class 1 thoroughbred racetracks and has established relationships with local leaders.

Penn plans to spend more time in Texas and, if lawmakers move toward legalization, Snowden said he expects the company is “positioned as well, if not better than really anybody in the sector.”

Las Vegas Sands also has invested heavily for many years to get casino gambling legalized in Texas.

"When Las Vegas Sands thinks about Texas and the Metroplex, it's really about an asset that will be both for business and future tourism," Sands Chairman and CEO Patrick Dumont told CNBC in an interview in October in Macao.

Dumont recently acquired the controlling stake in the Dallas Mavericks from his mother-in-law Miriam Adelson. He said there is the potential for a new Mavericks arena to incorporate a future casino.

"Whatever Las Vegas Sands can do in the future, that will be its own location, its own site and its own timeline," he said.

Houston Rockets owner Tilman Fertitta, currently serving as U.S. ambassador to Italy, owns Golden Nugget casinos, is Wynn Resorts' largest investor and is buying Caesars to take it private. Following a CNBC interview in Italy, he said, "Of course I want gaming in Texas!"

A new market would draw national sportsbook operators that would likely include DraftKings, Flutter-owned FanDuel, Caesars, Fanatics, and BetMGM, which is co-owned by MGM and Entain, among others.

Though the Texas' state election removed a major political obstacle, it didn't produce a deal. Legalizing casino gambling or sports betting would still require a constitutional amendment approved by two-thirds of both chambers and then voters.

Texas state control is now divided. While Democrats flipped the state House, Republicans kept the Senate. Democrat Gina Hinojosa won the governorship.

Las Vegas Sands spokesman Ron Reese told CNBC it's too early to speculate about what might happen with Texas gaming, but that the company will monitor developments closely.

Truist gaming analyst Barry Jonas wrote in a Wednesday note that he sees mixed implications from the midterm results.

"The Ohio governor-elect Amy Acton's support for regulated online casinos could improve the chances of iGaming legalization," Jonas said. On the other hand, Nebraska voters rejected ballot measures to legalize online betting.

Georgia, where Democrat Keisha Lance Bottoms won the governorship, could also provide an opportunity. She campaigned on letting voters decide whether to legalize casino gambling, with revenue expected to be earmarked to help fund education. Sports betting and casinos remain illegal in the state, and earlier sports-betting legislation failed in the House.

Penn could also benefit if lawmakers seek tighter regulations for so-called skill-based gaming machines, which compete with casinos and licensed video gaming terminals, according to Snowden. He said candidates who support skill-based gaming machines lost seats or influence in several states.

Penn operates four casinos in Pennsylvania as well as video gaming terminals in that state and other jurisdictions, and has three casinos in Missouri.

Snowden linked recent strong year-over-year gaming revenue growth to the shutdown of skill games in Pennsylvania in mid-October. Although he cautioned that it was too early to know whether Pennsylvania’s growth would last, he noted Missouri also saw strong revenue growth as its state attorney general pursued the machines there.

In Indiana, voters in three counties rejected a proposed casino license, which Penn sees as a benefit as it limits new competition for existing casinos in Indiana and neighboring Illinois and Ohio. Some gaming insiders speculate that a wave of left-leaning progressives in power could work to limit gambling businesses.

"There's no shortage of noise about where this industry is headed, and the election certainly turns up the volume," said Joe Maloney, president of Sports Betting Alliance. "What hasn't changed with the election is what works, and that's state-regulated sports betting and iGaming, built on accountability, consumer protection, and tax revenue for states. That's our lane, and we'll make that case to every new lawmaker willing to listen."

Democratic victories are likely to increase the pressure on prediction markets, even as industry supporters call out their predictive value in forecasting the election results.

"Every eligible customer should have access to these markets, which are federally regulated through our CFTC-registered Futures Commission Merchant," a Robinhood spokesperson said.

Democrats’ control of Congress gives them subpoena power and committee resources to investigate the president’s family and business dealings. That could put Donald Trump Jr. back in the spotlight as he is a paid strategic adviser to Kalshi and sits on Polymarket’s advisory board.

Congress could also consider legislation limiting the markets’ scope. The bipartisan Prediction Markets Are Gambling Act would bar Commodity Futures Trading Commission-regulated exchanges from offering contracts based on sports or casino-style games. But the bill had only been referred to committee as of the election. A veto-proof law would require two-thirds support in both chambers; no such vote is currently scheduled.

The American Gaming Association has been fighting prediction markets as unlicensed gambling.

"The prediction markets are facing a tsunami of criminal charges, major civil investigations and significant defeats in court," Bill Miller, the trade group's CEO, told CNBC. "In the aftermath of the election, we expect the bipartisan scrutiny of these platforms to further intensify."

"Our platform is an institutional-grade exchange with strong market-integrity and customer-protection programs," Polymarket told CNBC. A spokesman said the company welcomes the opportunity to work with lawmakers on both sides of the aisle.

Repeated requests to Kalshi for comment went unanswered.

Jeff LeRiche, a partner at law firm Husch Blackwell, said he expects more scrutiny.

"Entities with robust compliance and surveillance infrastructure will be well positioned to navigate the increased attention," said LeRiche, who spent more than 20 years in the CFTC's enforcement division.

The state-level legal fight is already underway. Utah has filed criminal charges against Kalshi and its founders, and the U.S. Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit rejected Kalshi’s request for a rehearing in its Nevada case, leaving the company exposed to state enforcement there.

New Democratic attorneys general in Ohio, Michigan and Nevada will also decide how to handle inherited disputes.

Ohio’s outgoing AG Andy Wilson helped lead a multistate effort to defend state gambling laws, Michigan’s Dana Nessel sued Kalshi and won a preliminary injunction, and Nevada’s Aaron Ford secured injunctions against prediction-market operators.

New AGs could also decide to follow Utah's lead in filing criminal charges, along with Arizona where criminal charges are pending.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2968. Silva and Karetsas ensure Dortmund defeat Real Betis as Guirassy limps off

Borussia Dortmund held out for a slender 2-1 victory over Champions League opponents Real Betis tonight. Despite losing Serhou Guirassy to an early injury, goals from Fábio Silva and Kostas Karetsas sufficiently outgunned their visitors from the Andalusian capital.

The hosts probably should have taken the lead with a pair of chances inside eight minutes. Twice, Serhou Guirassy failed to steer home right flank deliveries from Julian Ryerson and Jobe Bellingham. Barring an offside goal for Betis star Antony, BVB were largely on top.

Following a couple sighters from Felix Nmecha outside the box, Alvaro Vallés produced a point-blank save to keep Daniel Svensson out. Betis also appeared in the final third at the other end, notably Abdessamad Ezzalzouli testing Gregor Kobel with a curling strike.

Having knocked away on the door for most of the first-half, it took just 4 minutes for BVB to open the scoring after the break. Ryerson put a low cross into the box for Bellingham to strike. The rebound off Vallés fell nicely to Silva, thrashing in from six yards out.

It could have gotten better soon after as Karetsas failed to beat Vallés. For all the hosts' dominance, Betis grabbed a lifeline through substitute Aitor Ruibal. A costly Anton mistake surrendered the ball, enabling Ezzalzouli to find the winger at the back post who converted.

#2967. Dynasties or Parity? How MLB, the NFL, and the NBA try to find competitive balance

MLB owners and players are preparing for a likely lockout, and potentially missed games in 2027, over the contentious issue of adopting a salary cap. Owners are fighting to keep payrolls in check, and players want to get paid what they think they deserve.

But don't be confused — this isn't a referendum on curbing sports dynasties.

"We don't think dynasties are bad," said Morgan Sword, MLB's executive vice president of baseball operations, in an interview with CNBC Sport. "Dynasties that are achieved on well-run organizations can be wonderful for a league and engage people outside of that city."

Earlier this week, MLB proposed a cap of $258.2 million and a floor of $180.2 million to start in 2028. Limiting the spending ranges of teams should make the league inherently more fair, according to MLB executives. Currently, teams can spend an unlimited amount on players without penalty. The New York Mets and L.A. Dodgers spent more than $335 million on their 2026 rosters while the Miami Marlins and Cleveland Guardians spent less than $90 million, according to USA Today data.

Still, reasonable people can argue both sides of whether there's actually a competitive balance problem in baseball. MLBPA head Bruce Meyer has repeatedly pointed to metrics, such as the varied array of recent World Series winners — 10 different teams in the last 13 years — and the success of mid- or small-market, low-spending teams such as the Guardians, Tampa Bay Rays and Brewers, that prove the point that a cap is simply not needed.

Whether a league should value dynasties over parity, or vice versa, to improve fan engagement is a topic for debate. NBA Commissioner Adam Silver has hedged on this issue. In a CNBC Sport interview in 2024, he suggested parity was a superior outcome to dynasties.

"The data is absolutely crystal clear that the more competition you have, the more it drives interest in the league," Silver said in 2024. "If you have a 30-team league and you only have four teams competing for championships, of course the fans of those teams love that, and the fans of those teams that have been historic dynasties have disproportionate amounts of fans. But, we think competition is great. That's ultimately what we're providing to our fans."

Still, in an interview with "The Dan Patrick Show" in June, Silver noted dynasties can be "great" as long as fans agree that league rules are fair for all competitors.

"I don't think fans are as enthusiastic about them if one team is spending drastically more than another team," Silver said.

The NBA changed its salary cap before the 2024-25 season to include a second apron that disincentivizes spending above a certain threshold with harsh penalties that restrict free agency and trades. Only one NBA team — the Denver Nuggets — spent over the second apron this season, set at $221.7 million for 2026-27.

Silver credits the change with a move toward emphasizing parity over dynasties — a topic he discussed in 2024 with CNBC Sport after the second apron's introduction.

The NBA has had eight different champions in the past eight seasons. Even the losing teams in the most recent NBA Finals have been varied. The last nine runners-up: the San Antonio Spurs, Indiana Pacers, Dallas Mavericks, Miami Heat, Boston Celtics, Phoenix Suns, Heat, Golden State Warriors, and the Cleveland Cavaliers.

Both MLB and NBA league executives have said they view the NFL's hard cap setup as the gold standard in sports competitive balance.

"The goal over time has been, and I've said it, to be more NFL-like, where you believe your team has a shot … regardless of the market size, " Silver said in June. "Fans want a sense that [the league construction] is not top heavy, as it was historically in the NBA. Remember, three teams won 60% of the championships over the first 65 years of this league."

There's some irony in both leagues pointing to the NFL as the prototype for competitive balance. The New England Patriots had a 20-year dynasty from 2001 to 2021 with quarterback Tom Brady and head coach Bill Belichick. Barring significant injuries, teams like the Kansas City Chiefs, Buffalo Bills and San Francisco 49ers have recently been Super Bowl contenders every season while bottom dwellers such as the Arizona Cardinals and New York Jets seem to struggle year after year.

There's an argument the hard cap is actually a detriment to competitive balance.

A cap "takes competition out of the hands of clubs and prevents teams from getting better," NFLPA chief Bruce Meyer said to The Athletic last month. "It certainly doesn't guarantee better balance, and we know that from the dynasties in the other sports."

But Sword argued the existence of dynasties in the NFL is actually a selling point, not a flaw.

"It's frustrating for fans where the Kansas City Royals don't have the same opportunity to build a dynasty as the Kansas City Chiefs because of the economic system of the leagues," Sword said.

MLB's point of view on dynasties is they can be "wonderful" for a league, he said.

"I believe fans love excellence," said Sword. "If fans think the game is being played on a fair set of rules, fans love seeing groups of people excel under that set of rules. I think fans enjoy the whole arch of a dynasty — the construction of one, the reign of one, and the fall of one."

It's that last point that's key to MLB's argument for a cap — the fall of one. MLB fears its uncapped rules allow for well-run teams to achieve sustained successes without ever having to worry about the fall of a dynasty.

Much of the argument in the coming months for or against a cap will have to do with whether or not the players feel they are advantaged or disadvantaged by a cap. But the issue of whether caps and floors lead to a better fan experience is perhaps even more important for a league's health.

#2966. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

#2965. 13 young Eagles worth watching in what's left of a lost season

13 young Eagles worth watching in what's left of a lost season originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Eagles aren't even at the halfway point yet.

There are still nine games to go in this miserable 2026 season. And now that the Eagles are sitting at 2-6 with a point differential of -65 it probably feels a little daunting to have to watch this team play nine more times.

Maybe some Eagles fans really will do something else on Sundays — not to mention Thanksgiving and Christmas Eve — but let's be honest: Most of you are still going to watch when the Eagles play.

So other than rooting for a good draft pick, how do you find enjoyment out of a lost season like this? The answer is pretty easy. You get to monitor the development of the young players on the team. And, hopefully, you'll be able to see their progress in the second half of this season.

With that in mind, here are 13 young Eagles who are worth watching the rest of the way:

QB Joe Milton: Maybe the Eagles really do just ride it out with backup quarterback Tanner McKee in place of Jalen Hurts. But there's some definite intrigue with Milton. The Eagles signed him off the Cowboys' practice squad on Oct. 16 and it didn't take long for Milton to supplant Cole Payton as the new backup. The 26-year-old Milton (6-5, 236) has played in just five career games but the former sixth-round pick has plenty of arm talent. His highlight reel from his 2023 season at Tennessee is littered with highlight plays. Milton has a big arm and can elude pressure. He just wasn't very consistent. But in this lost season, if McKee continues to struggle, it would be fun to see Milton get a chance.

QB Cole Payton: The Eagles on Sunday night made an interesting decision. They kept Cole Payton active over offensive lineman Jake Majors but Payton didn't play. My first thought was that Payton would have a role on special teams but he didn't. So perhaps there was an offensive role in mind for Payton that the Eagles didn't get to in their 27-10 loss — or maybe he was just an emergency RB option. Payton is a heck of an athlete and while he's developing as a QB behind the scenes, maybe there's a chance to get him on the field in some other roles.

RB Jaydon Blue: In the loss to the Commanders, Blue played just 8 snaps on offense and 7 snaps on special teams, despite Saquon Barkley being banged up and others missing. Blue 22, was a fifth-round pick out of Texas last year but played in just five games with the Cowboys as a rookie. Blue has an intriguing skillset. He ran a 4.38 at the Combine last year and also showed an ability as a receiving threat at Texas. He's a different kind of back and it would be fun to see him get some burn.

WR Makai Lemon: The first-round pick from USC got off to a slow start in his rookie season but has been getting better and better once the Eagles scrapped the portion of the playbook that had him going backwards. Over the last couple of weeks without DeVonta Smith and Dontayvion Wicks, Lemon has played more and has had his two biggest games of the season. Overall, Lemon has 31 catches on 45 targets for 251 yards and a touchdown. Among rookies, Lemon is sixth in receiving yards behind Carnell Tate, Denzel Boston, KC Concepcion, Germie Bernard and Antonio Williams. But Lemon has been coming on stronger recently.

WR Darius Cooper: The UDFA from Tarleton State is still just in his second NFL season. He made the roster again in 2026 and has 11 catches for 175 yards and 4 touchdowns this year. But he has caught just 45.8% of his targets, which is a very low number. Cooper hasn't been a star but he has made some big-time catches and should continue to get some run the rest of the way.

TE Eli Stowers: The second-round pick from Vanderbilt missed the first six games of the season on Injured Reserve with a quad injury. But Stowers made his NFL debut in Week 7 and then made his first NFL catches in Week 8. A couple of those catches came in garbage time but it was still good to see him finish with 3 for 53. When the Eagles drafted Stowers, we knew he was a developmental tight end. The best way to develop is to get him more playing time this season. Stowers is far from a finished product but he has the type of athleticism that's worth developing.

OL Willie Lampkin: The Eagles have had Landon Dickerson back in the starting lineup at left guard the last couple of weeks, but it'll be interesting to see if Dickerson remains with the team through the trade deadline. If not, maybe we see a bit more of Lampkin. The 5-foot-11 offensive lineman started a couple of games at left guard in between Cam Jurgens and Dickerson. Even if Lampkin can't get back on the field as a starter, the Eagles could find some unique ways to use him. He lined up as a fullback on the Saquon Barkley direct-snap touchdown on Sunday night.

OG Micah Morris: The sixth-round pick from Georgia has played just one snap all season so he probably won't get many chances to play this season. But if more injuries hit, Morris could get some chances. He's a really athletic rookie who is worth developing.

C Drew Kendall: The Eagles drafted Kendall out of Boston College in the fifth round last year and have been high on him for a while. We saw that this year when they got him into the starting lineup from Week 2. Kendall has now started five games at center this season and could be the Eagles' long-term fix at the position. The Eagles traded away his predecessor Cam Jurgens earlier this season, so if Kendall plays well the rest of the way, he could keep that job for a while.

OT Markel Bell: The third-round pick from Miami has had some ups and downs this season. Nick Sirianni seemed impressed by Bell's performance against the Cowboys, but Bell didn't have his best game against the Commanders. Lane Johnson started the first three games of the season before he abruptly retired and Bell has now started the last five games. According to PFF, Bell has given up 17 pressures and 5 sacks this season. He has had some good moments in the run game too. Johnson seemed really impressed by Bell in training camp; we'll see if Bell ends up being his long-term replacement.

DT TY Robinson: Robinson played 104 defensive snaps as a rookie fourth-round pick last year. Through eight games this season, Robinson has played just 52 snaps and is firmly behind Jalen Carter, Jordan Davis, Moro Ojomo and Byron Young on the depth chart. Robinson has played just 9% of the snaps, while Young has been on the field for 27%. Robinson had a solid training camp and preseason so it might make sense to start having him eat into Young's snaps.

LB Jihaad Campbell: This is a tricky one because Campbell began the season as a full-time starter next to Zack Baun and then the Eagles started up a rotation between Campbell and Jeremiah Trotter Jr. While it looks like Trotter might simply be the best option right now, Campbell was a first-round pick and has a ton of athletic ability. Campbell hasn't lived up to expectations but he's in Year 2 as a first-round pick. The only way for him to get better is to continue to play.

S Drew Mukuba: Mukuba is in a similar position as Campbell but there's no one like Trotter pushing him. The second-year, second-round pick from Texas has had a mostly disappointing season as a full-time starter in 2026. But he did have a good game against the Commanders with a forced fumble and an interception that was negated by a penalty. Through eight games, Mukuba has played 556 of 558 snaps. We might learn a lot about him over the final nine games of the season.

#2963. Mississippi Senate: Colom, Hyde-Smith gird for ballot legal fight in razor-thin race

An ultra-tight Mississippi Senate race between Republican incumbent Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith and Democratic challenger Scott Colom could end with one of the biggest shocks in a midterm election cycle already chock full of stunning defeats of GOP candidates.

With Colom in the lead by just 1,813 votes on Thursday, Hyde-Smith's campaign set the stage for a potential legal battle over ballots by demanding in a letter to county commissioners and circuit clerks in all 82 Mississippi counties that they immediately separate all provisional ballots cast in the election from regular and absentee ballots.

"These measures are necessary to ensure that each affidavit ballot's eligibility for counting andultimate disposition can be verified," the letter said.

Lawyers for Colom's campaign responded with a letter of their own to the counties, calling the Republican's demands "unlawful and extraordinary measures."

"Those demands have no basis in Mississippi law, and if you depart from ordinary election procedures as you have been asked to do, you will violate Mississippi voters' rights and Mississippi election law," they said.

Provisional ballots, which are also known as affidavit ballots, are cast by a voter whose name does not appear on the poll book, who does not present an acceptable photo ID, or in several other situations.

The ballot is then placed in a sealed envelope and later examined by election officials to see if it can be cast, or the voter can return with a photo ID or evidence within five business days to show they are entitled to cast the ballot.

When the letters were sent, Colom had 49.04% of the counted ballots, compared to 48.85% for Hyde-Smith, with an estimated more than 95% of the votes in, according to the Associated Press.

Colom's lead ticked up slightly late Thursday afternoon to 1,813 votes out of a total of 951,744 counted as of 6:17 p.m. ET, the AP said. Ty Pinkins, an independent candidate, had 20,007 votes, or 2.1% of the total count.

A final result may not be available until next week because more ballots can still arrive and be counted.

Mississippi's election procedures do not have an automatic recount provision.

"Absolutely, I didn't think it would be this close," said Brian Anderson, a political science professor at the Mississippi University for Women in Columbus.

Anderson said that before the election, he thought Colom, a 43-year-old district attorney, might get into position to lose by just 3 or 4 percentage points, which would have been a very solid showing in a state that hasn't elected a Democrat to the Senate since 1982.

"I'm much more surprised" now that Colom has a chance to win, Anderson said.

If Colom defeats Hyde-Smith, 67, he would be the first Black senator from Mississippi since the post-Civil War Reconstruction Era.

"The Democrats have struggled here to create a pipeline of candidates," Anderson said. A Colom win "would galvanize the Democratic Party in a way it has not been," he said.

Neither candidate's campaign immediately responded to requests for comment from CNBC.

Hyde-Smith's X account, in a post Thursday afternoon, said, "Cindy Hyde-Smith is going to represent Mississippi in the United States Senate for the next six years."

"We believe thousands of votes remain to be counted from dozens of Republican counties, and we are confident those votes will reinforce her path to victory," the post said. "Election officials are continuing the normal process of counting outstanding ballots, including absentee ballots. They must have the time to complete that work and ensure every lawful ballot is accurately counted and every Mississippian's voice is heard."

The post added, "We expect Democratic lawyers to pull out every stop, and we are prepared. Our focus is on completing the count and returning Cindy Hyde-Smith to the Senate to keep fighting for Mississippi values."

"Mississippi is the firewall, and this race is our line in the sand," the message said.

In a pair of ironic twists, if Colom wins, his victory could be attributed in some ways to actions taken by two Republicans: President Donald Trump and Hyde-Smith herself.

Supreme Court Justice Amy Coney Barrett, whom Trump appointed, wrote the court's 5-4 majority ruling in June that upheld a Mississippi law allowing mail-in absentee ballots postmarked by Election Day to be counted if they are received within five business days.

Those ballots could provide Colom a margin of victory. There are also an estimated 10,000 to 15,000 provisional ballots that require verification of eligibility to vote.

A lawyer for the Republican National Committee, whose lawsuit argued federal law barred counting votes received after Election Day, noted in a brief to the Supreme Court, "Democratic voters tend to mail their ballots later on average than Republican voters, which results in late-arriving ballots favoring Democratic candidates."

Trump at the time of the Supreme Court ruling called it "a tremendous loss" for voters' rights.

Hyde-Smith was appointed to her Senate seat in 2018 after the resignation of Thad Cochran and later that year won a special election for the seat. She was elected to a full term in 2020.

In 2023, she effectively blocked Colom's nomination by then-President Joe Biden to a post as a federal judge for the Northern District of Mississippi. Colom's nomination had the support of Mississippi's other Republican senator, Roger Wicker, and two former GOP governors.

Hyde-Smith said she opposed Colom's nomination for reasons that included his purported support for transgender students participating in girls' sports and because his campaign for district attorney had allegedly received money from billionaire George Soros.

Colom, in an April 10, 2023, letter to Hyde-Smith, said he was unaware before his hometown newspaper reported it that Soros had donated to a political action committee that had supported his campaign. He also wrote that he was not sure why Hyde-Smith had suggested he "opposed legislation to protect female athletes."

Colom concluded the letter by promising to be guided by the law, not his personal views on public policy, if he were confirmed as a judge.

"I hope you will reconsider your views about my nomination," he wrote.

Hyde-Smith did not reconsider her views and maintained her block on the nomination.

Colom announced his candidacy for the Democratic nomination for Senate in September 2025 and recently told CNN, "Me not having a federal judgeship is not a motivating factor for me at all" in running.

Anderson said Hyde-Smith ran "a low-visibility campaign," at least compared to Colom's, and that she focused on voters who traditionally would be expected to support her.

Hyde-Smith refused to debate Colom several times.

Colom "knows how to speak to people's interests, and Hyde-Smith was all about values, which is great for a lot of people, but it's all about the interests right now," Anderson said.

Those interests include concerns about affordability, the Iran war and corruption.

Colom is "bringing up all those," Anderson said.

Anderson noted that Colom's campaign had pounced on a remark Hyde-Smith made during a Feb. 22 interview with a local television outlet.

In the interview, Hyde-Smith answered a question about rising beef prices by saying, "You know, there so many proteins to choose from."

Colom's campaign featured a video clip of that remark in an ad featuring people reacting incredulously to Hyde-Smith's suggestion.

"Pick another protein?" one woman said in the ad. "She done lost her d--- mind. I'd rather pick another senator."

#2960. AI debt concerns grow as ING reportedly seeks buyers for $500 million of data center loans

ING is reportedly seeking buyers for a portfolio of 12 data center loans worth around $500 million, joining a broader push by banks to distribute credit risk tied to the artificial intelligence infrastructure build-out.

The auction is said to include a $25 million piece linked to the Stargate project in New Mexico, which involves OpenAI, SoftBank and Oracle, as well as certain other loan assets involving Vantage Data Centers and CyrusOne, Bloomberg reported Friday, citing people familiar with the matter.

ING will hold a bids-wanted-in-competition, or BWIC, auction process, with bids sized at $10 million or more due by Nov. 19, according to Bloomberg. A BWIC is a competitive process for investors to bid on pieces of a loan portfolio.

The sale highlights a growing market for banks to move data center loans to private credit firms, asset managers and insurers as financing for the AI boom expands.

Investment banks typically use a lend-to-distribute model for these types of loans, said Hank Calenti, global markets strategist at SMBC.

"The buyers can range from private credit investors through asset managers to insurers. They would buy these assets for their expected higher returns," he said.

The ING sale comes as fears grow over soaring debt tied to the AI build-out — and potentially disappointing returns among investors.

Those concerns have accelerated following the Democrats' sweeping win in the U.S. midterms, which could see moratoriums or tighter restrictions on data center construction in certain states.

The perceived risk of a construction slowdown sent AI-related stocks into free fall on Thursday, with the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index closing the session down 5.5% — its worst day since April 2025 — and into correction territory.

"Whether banks sell more based on AI data centers moratorium fears should be driven by the cost (discount received) versus the benefit of exiting to free up cash for further lending, where the cost includes explaining the exposure to your board," Calenti told CNBC via email.

"This could be business as usual, or financiers looking to exit before the door shuts."

Pruksa Iamthongthong, head of APAC equities at Aberdeen Investments, said the Democrats' gains have increased concerns around greater scrutiny of data center development, at a time when investors were already questioning the financing and returns behind the scale of AI capital spending.

"We think near-term volatility could continue as the market assesses potential delays to U.S. data center construction and constraints around compute capacity."

Earlier this year, Wall Street lenders including JPMorgan Chase and Morgan Stanley were said to be exploring a variety of approaches to reduce their lending exposures to data center construction.

According to a Financial Times report on May 3, these include offloading portions of debt via private sales to non-bank lenders, as well as through so-called significant risk transfers, which transfer part of a bank's credit risk to investors to free up balance sheet capacity.

#2959. Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac rally as Ackman presses for conservatorship exit

The midterm elections could be a turning point for the release of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac from government control, according to billionaire investor Bill Ackman.

Investors may agree with him, if the rally in both stocks is any sign.

The Federal National Mortgage Association, known as Fannie Mae, and Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, or Freddie Mac, have each jumped more than 60% from Oct. 14 to Monday's close. The stocks were both up roughly 3% on Tuesday.

Ackman, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management, has long pushed for the two entities to be released from federal conservatorship.

"The reason why it's not being touched right now is, you know, midterms," he said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk Box."

Ackman, who owns both names through Pershing Square, believes Freddie and Fannie have the potential to increase "10-fold from current share prices," he said in his quarterly letter to shareholders, issued on Monday.

Fannie and Freddie buy mortgages from banks and other lenders, and then package them into mortgage-backed securities, known as MBS. Their operations are critical to the housing market, providing liquidity to banks and creating a secondary market for MBS.

The entities were placed into conservatorship in September 2008, during the financial crisis. The stocks were delisted from the New York Stock Exchange in 2010 and have traded over the counter since then.

Ackman wants the conservatorship to end, arguing the entities would be worth multiples of their current valuation if they were freed from government control.

President Donald Trump has supported ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie. But it remains politically and economically contentious.

The entities operate under strict capital threshold requirements and would need to be recapitalized, which could happen through issuing common equity. However, the Treasury Department would need to figure out an exit plan for its roughly $388 billion ownership stake in the entities through preferred securities and warrants.

Congress may also need to play a role, passing legislation to restructure the government's role in mortgage financing and home-loan guarantees.

Nonetheless, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent recently said it could happen before Trump's term ends, telling 2Way's "The Morning Meeting" podcast that the administration will tackle the ownership structure "next year, the year after." He said affordability will be guiding the decision.

One point of controversy is whether ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie would increase the spreads on MBS over Treasuries, effectively making mortgages costlier. Bessent has sought to reassure the market that wouldn't happen.

"Whatever we do is going to be guided by making sure that the spread on [agency] mortgages … over the Treasury does not increase," Bessent said.

Ackman argues that privatizing the GSEs would help insulate them from political meddling. "Fannie and Freddie are these massive, enormously profitable institutions where whichever political party controls them…if they're still in conservatorship, can influence the outcome of elections," he told CNBC.

In one scenario, he explained, Democrats win the White House, conservatorship is still in place, and Democrats push Fannie and Freddie to lower credit standards in order to expand home ownership and affordability.

"If they're going to move more aggressively into borrowers with lower credit quality, for example, to achieve a political objective, that's going to have an impact on trillions of dollars outstanding mortgage securities," Ackman said. "It's going to have an impact on overall credit spreads."

MBS investors want certainty about how the entities will be run, he noted.

"That only gets resolved in a world in which they exit conservatorship, they become publicly traded companies that are owned by institutional and retail investors," Ackman said. "We set the rules. In conservatorship, the president can do anything."

#2956. Montana votes to ban corporate political money. Next stop? Probably the Supreme Court

Imagine a corporation wants to spend $10 million to defeat a political candidate. Under the Supreme Court's landmark 2010 Citizens United ruling, it can spend as much as it wants provided it doesn't coordinate with the candidate's campaign.

On Tuesday, Montana voted to change that.

In a state President Donald Trump won by nearly 20 points in 2024, roughly 65% of Montanans voted to approve Initiative 194 – the "Montana Plan" – embracing a novel legal strategy to curb corporate political spending. If states cannot restrict political speech under Citizens United, can they instead strip corporations of the power to spend money on elections?

The vote follows a record $11.87 billion in political advertising nationwide this election cycle that was split about evenly between the two parties, according to media tracking firm AdImpact.

The voter-approved measure is set to become state law on Jan. 1 in a state where voters can make law via ballot initiatives. Montana's Republican-controlled legislature could amend or repeal it, though if that happens, proponents have pledged to take the fight back to voters in 2028.

Now, Montana is poised for a constitutional showdown that could reach the nation's highest court. If the strategy survives, it could provide a replicable blueprint for restricting corporate political spending across the country. Similar measures are being explored in more than 30 states.

"If a single state can successfully deny corporate power over elections, it proves that Citizens United wasn't the final word on democracy, and we will continue battling," said Tom Moore, a former Federal Election Commission official and current senior fellow at the Center for American Progress who helped develop the Montana Plan.

The Montana Plan hinges on a legal distinction: Corporations may have constitutional rights, but their powers come from the states.

Under Initiative 194, Montana would strip corporations, trade associations, labor unions and nonprofits, including those headquartered elsewhere, of the legal power to spend money influencing elections in the state.

In practice, that should mean no corporate-funded attack ads, no trade associations bankrolling referendum campaigns and no politically active nonprofits spending their own funds to sway Montana voters.

Violators risk losing state-granted corporate privileges like limited liability, which generally protects shareholders from personal responsibility for corporate debts, until all improperly spent funds are surrendered and legal conditions are met.

"The argument of the people supporting the initiative is, in theory, trying to be pretty simple. Keep your campaign cash out of Montana, or you run the risk of putting your shareholders' personal balance sheets on the line," said Kal Munis, a politics professor at Montana State University who is an expert in the state's politics.

The financial stakes extend well beyond Montana. Known corporate contributions to outside groups in federal elections surged from $13 million in 2010 to more than $600 million by September 2026, according to OpenSecrets, a nonpartisan nonprofit that tracks such spending. That excludes money funneled through nonprofits that conceal their donors.

But there's an irony to the Montana Plan. It had high-dollar backers from corporate America.

State campaign finance data shows Samantha Walton, wife of Walmart heir Lukas Walton, gave $722,300 to groups backing the plan, while LinkedIn co-founder and Democratic megadonor Reid Hoffman contributed $150,000. The campaign's organizing nonprofit, Transparent Election Initiative, is itself a type of dark-money group that generally isn't required to disclose its donors.

And the new law wouldn't exactly take billionaires out of politics. It exempts individuals, political committees, government entities and bona fide news, commentary and editorial content.

Consider Tesla and SpaceX CEO Elon Musk, the 2026 election's largest known individual donor, with at least $474 million in political giving, according to a CNBC analysis of campaign finance records. Musk could still spend millions of his personal fortune influencing Montana elections. His companies, however, would face the new restrictions.

Moore argued that because corporations derive their legal existence from states, those states should have the power to restrict their political spending.

Opponents call the Montana Plan an unconstitutional end run around Citizens United. If states cannot directly prohibit protected political speech, they argue, they cannot accomplish the same thing by threatening companies' legal privileges.

And critics warn the restrictions would reach beyond corporate giants, silencing local trade groups, labor unions and community organizations on school bonds, tax measures and other local ballot issues.

"Why allow a media corporation to speak out, while denying a non-profit association of citizens the right to organize around clean water or lower taxes?" said Brad Smith, who leads the Institute for Free Speech and is a former FEC Chairman. "Billionaires will find loopholes. All this really does is silence Montana's small- and medium-sized businesses."

The first legal test is already underway in Hawaii, where lawmakers enacted a similar law in May. Montana is the first state to embrace the strategy through a statewide vote.

The Hawaii law faces formidable legal opposition. The U.S. Chamber of Commerce has sued to block it, while Smith's Institute for Free Speech is representing the Grassroot Institute of Hawaii in a separate challenge. The national ACLU has also weighed in, arguing in a court brief that the law violates the First Amendment.

Legal and campaign finance experts told CNBC the dispute could eventually reach the Supreme Court, whose 6-3 conservative majority has repeatedly loosened campaign finance restrictions. Just in June, the justices struck down limits on political parties' spending in coordination with candidates.

"Shifting the prohibition into the corporate code is a distinction without a difference," said Michael Kang, a Northwestern Pritzker School of Law professor who studies campaign finance. "The courts will see right through it because the clear intent remains to bypass Citizens United. It seems like a legal sleight of hand."

Montana has lost this fight before. In 1912, voters banned corporate campaign spending after copper barons bribed lawmakers and dominated state politics. The law stood for a century before the Supreme Court struck it down in 2012 under Citizens United. This time, the threat could also come from within: Montana's Republican-controlled legislature could weaken or repeal the new law.

"The way we look at it, this is states' rights vs Citizens United," Moore said. "That's the pitch once and if it makes it to the Supreme Court they're going to need to decide between one of their two favorite legal theories and things to fall back on."

#2953. Binance breach puts custody at center of crypto infrastructure trade

The $6.3 billion Binance hack could accelerate a shift in how investors value cryptocurrency companies, putting a greater premium on custody providers and the infrastructure needed to securely hold and protect digital assets.

Much of crypto's evolution this year has involved companies moving past their reliance on trading volumes and transaction fees, to focus instead on building the infrastructure connecting digital assets with traditional finance. Last Thursday's breach adds a new dimension, bringing cybersecurity, custody, compliance and balance-sheet resilience into sharper focus.

Importantly, the Binance breach was not an indictment of crypto itself, or a case of leadership-driven fraud, as with FTX in late 2022. Instead, Binance's leadership moved quickly to contain the fallout, protect customers and commit the exchange's own resources to cover losses.

On Tuesday, Binance CEO Richard Teng told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that the company has replenished 98% of $6.34 billion in affected assets, and that it is still in the process of purchasing outstanding, more illiquid assets.

Management's response, alongside efforts elsewhere in the industry to boost confidence, helped prevent a security breach from escalating into a broader crisis of confidence. For example, Tether, Circle, Ripple and others moved quickly to freeze or restrict access to about $3 billion in stolen assets; Coinbase blocked transfers to compromised wallets and offered Binance direct assistance.

The hack could even help shape the next phase of the crypto infrastructure trade. The new winners may not just be the businesses with the most users, volume or assets under custody, but the ones that can also demonstrate they're able to protect those assets under extreme duress.

"Which company has the resources to invest in cybersecurity and protect customer funds? For a smaller company, it could be much harder because once these crypto companies become infrastructure plays, providing the well for tokenization … they need to have the compliance and control in place so that a similar hacking will not happen or can be minimized," Owen Lau, senior analyst at ClearStreet, told CNBC. "Larger companies have more resources that could give them some advantage."

While institutional investors have increasingly embraced digital assets, broader adoption depends on confidence that those assets can be securely held, accessed and transferred. The Binance breach highlights the importance of that infrastructure, and could increase demand for specialized custodians and platforms with established asset-protection capabilities.

Coinbase has operated a dedicated institutional custody business since 2018. Circle entered the custody business this year, after the U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency granted it approval to operate as a trust bank. In the past few years, Ripple and Kraken have also launched custody businesses.

Still, smaller players compete in the space as well. Small cap crypto exchange stock Gemini Space Station has offered custody services since 2019. BitGo Holdings, founded in 2013, is one of the earliest players in institutional crypto custody and infrastructure, with a reputation for security and serving major institutions.

The shift could also drive consolidation as companies look to buy capabilities, deepen their infrastructure offerings and achieve the scale needed to compete, Lau said.

The more consequential structural shift may be the separation of custody from execution, according to Morgan Stanley.

"Crypto exchanges have historically combined trading, custody, and settlement within vertically integrated platforms, concentrating operational risk. Repeated security breaches could encourage investors to move assets toward independent custody while continuing to trade on CEXs," the firm's analyst Felix Stratmann said in a note Monday," referring to centralized cryptocurrency exchanges.

"This would preserve the role of centralized exchanges as liquidity and execution venues, while potentially benefiting regulated custodians, including banks, as institutional investors place greater emphasis on asset segregation, operational resilience, and investor protection," Stratmann said.

#2950. Market sell-off hits hard: Advisors say 'stay the course with an eye on buying'

Top financial advisors aren't urging clients to change their portfolios after Thursday's sharp market sell-off, even as retail investors were heavy sellers of individual stocks.

In a Thursday flash poll of advisors from CNBC's Financial Advisor 100 and Financial Advisor Council, more than three-quarters of respondents said investors' first steps should be to check their allocation against their goals or stay the course. The online poll, conducted from 2:30 p.m. to 6 p.m. ET on Thursday, was sent to 121 firms and 38 responded.

Retail investors sold individual stocks Thursday as technology stocks tumbled, and the Dow gave back its post-midterm election gains. The S&P 500 suffered its worst one-day drop since April 2025 on Thursday. Analysts linked the sell-off to fears of a data center construction slowdown following major Democratic election wins.

The Nasdaq Composite tumbled 5.5%, closing more than 10% below its most recent high and putting it in what Wall Street regards as a correction. The broad-market S&P 500 lost 4.4%, while the Dow fell more than 1,800 points, or 3.4%.

Yet, financial advisors who answered the CNBC poll overwhelmingly responded that they are not recommending changes to their clients' portfolios. The broader message from interviews with several advisors: review whether your portfolio still matches your goals, but don't let a sudden market move make the decision for you.

For investors who continue contributing to a 401(k), a market decline can mean buying investments at lower prices over time, said Andy Pratt, director of investment strategy at Burney Wealth Management in Reston, Virginia.

"If you have a 401(k), money is being allocated there biweekly, monthly, you know, whatever your pay period is, and so you're able to dollar-cost average in," Pratt said. "So when the market does sell off, you're entering at a much better spot."

Pratt said a well-designed portfolio may not need changes during a downturn. John Mullen, managing director, president and CEO of Parsons Capital Management in Providence, Rhode Island, put it this way: "Stay the course with an eye on buying."

Yet, if investors are panicking, that could be a sign to reassess whether their investments still fit their goals and risk tolerance, financial advisors say.

Carolyn McClanahan, a certified financial planner, physician and founder of Life Planning Partners in Jacksonville, Florida, says she tells clients to follow this strategy, which "works beautifully."

McClanahan said investors should stick to an investment policy that reflects their needs and "tune out the rest of the noise."

Tra Sullivan, a certified financial planner, wealth manager, and chief investment officer at Dallas-based Beaird Harris Wealth Management, said historically the U.S. stock market has often risen in the 12 months after midterm elections, but he views that pattern as context rather than a promise.

He added that when markets fall, "there's always a recovery, and a lot of times, in most cases, more quickly than we anticipate", although past rebounds don't guarantee future recoveries.

Nasdaq futures rebounded in pre-market trading on Friday with a bounce in chip stocks following a sell-off that sent the Nasdaq into correction territory the day before.

Still, if market weakness continues, Sullivan said he brings the discussion back to a client's financial plan.

For long-term investors, Dan Moisand, a CFP and financial advisor at Orlando, Florida-based Moisand Fitzgerald Tamayo, said resilience can matter more than trying to time each market swing.

"Being resilient has proven to be more profitable than trying to be nimble," he said.

Correction: An earlier version of this article misstated John Mullen's certifications. He is managing director, president and CEO of Parsons Capital Management.

#2949. Nasdaq drops into correction territory — here’s what typically happens next

The Nasdaq Composite plunged into correction territory on Thursday. History shows more selling can follow in the near term, but a recovery eventually takes hold three months later.

The tech-heavy index slid more than 5% on Thursday, closing more than 11% below its record reached last month. On Wall Street, any decline greater than 10% is a correction. A 20% pullback qualifies as a bear market.

Across 10 previous correction episodes over the last decade, selloffs in 2025, 2022, 2020 and 2018 were followed by further rough selling over the next one month. But the Nasdaq was back to rally mode again after six months for all those occasions except one: 2022.

Overall, the Nasdaq gained an average of 9.41% from the correction bottom over the following three months and 17.03% over six months.

The January 2022 correction was the one that offered a harsher outcome. The Nasdaq lost another 18.3% over the next six months and was down 24.3% a year later.

The catalyst for this correction was Democrats sweeping the House and Senate in Tuesday's midterm elections. The party also made significant gains in Texas, where Gina Hinojosa won the race for governor after campaigning on an immediate moratorium for data center development.

Investors are now weighing whether tighter state-level restrictions could slow data center construction, putting pressure on companies tied to the artificial intelligence trade.

Frank Cappelleri, founder of CappThesis, pointed to past midterm election cycles for guidance and found that points to more volatility in the near term as well.

The chart analyst said the Nasdaq was higher 12 months after the election in all but one of 13 midterm cycles since 1974.

"The caveat is that the path can be a volatile one," he said.

Cappelleri pointed to 2018, when the Nasdaq rallied the day after Democrats won the House. A sharp December sell-off followed, but the index was ultimately about 15% higher a year after the election, he said.

Chip stocks tumbled Thursday, with the iShares Semiconductor ETF (SOXX) falling 12% and for its worst day since the Covid-19 breakout of March 2020.

— CNBC's Fred Imbert contributed to the report
