#2948. Democratic sweep gives markets a reason to rethink path of interest rates

The Democrats' resounding victory in the U.S. midterms has the potential to curb interest rate hikes globally next year, according to market watchers — but the uncertain path of oil prices, U.S. economic growth and fiscal policies will be key.

Central banks around the world are back in monetary tightening mode, with the Federal Reserve, Bank of England, European Central Bank and Bank of Japan all raising rates in recent months.

The shift has in part stemmed from the U.S. and Israel's war with Iran driving up prices in oil and gas markets. The war has also exacerbated a squeeze in global refining capacity which has sent the cost of products like diesel, gasoline and jet fuel soaring.

Traders largely expected big Democratic gains this week after polls pointed in their favor, but there was still uncertainty leading up to the vote about whether the party would capture both chambers of Congress, increasing the ability for lawmakers to challenge President Donald Trump's policymaking.

Crude oil prices tumbled following the clear-cut Democratic victory in both the House and Senate, with international benchmark ICE Brent crude falling 8.7% on Wednesday, closing below $100 a barrel for the first time since September.

That was largely attributed to raised expectations that the result may mean Trump is more inclined to strike a deal to end the war in Iran, helping restore Gulf refinery capacity and improve export conditions. Vice President JD Vance's lengthy statement blaming Republican losses on the Iran war boosted that view.

Dan Coatsworth, head of markets at AJ Bell, said there had been "an admission from the Republican camp that the Iran war cost them a clean sweep in the election, which the market has taken to indicate that a swift resolution to the Middle East conflict could help them save face."

"Trump might relish the opportunity to take all the credit if an end to the war leads to a big drop in energy costs."

If the election result "pushes for a resolution of the conflict in the Middle East, that might bring oil down, and that's been an issue for the Fed and inflation, and part of the reason why rates are higher," said Andrew Slimmon, senior portfolio manager and head of the Applied Equity Advisors at Morgan Stanley.

Historic data shows a tight correlation between oil prices and global rates, Ralph Axel, rates strategist at Bank of America, said in a research note published Thursday. Higher oil prices over the next three to six months would likely mean "significantly higher interest rates" in the U.S., but especially so in the U.K. and Germany, he said.

"If oil prices rise, we think rates will too, especially at the front end where long-end term premium is less of a factor and monetary policy expectations play a larger role," he added.

As the uptick in crude prices on Thursday and choppiness on Friday showed, a resolution to the nearly nine-month-long conflict is far from locked in.

"For now, oil has begun to climb back up again, and interest rate expectations have not radically changed from pre-election levels," AJ Bell's Dan Coatsworth noted.

Money markets on Thursday indicated a moderate change in expectations from a week ago, both in the U.S. and Europe, with traders pricing in a slightly higher chance of monetary easing on a one-year horizon.

The effect on a two-year timeframe was more pronounced in the euro area than the U.S., according to CNBC analysis of LSEG data, with rate pricing down 8.7 basis points on the week for the ECB versus 4.6 basis points for the Fed.

"Republicans don't really have an off-ramp here at this stage," said Laurel Rapp, director for the U.S. and North America Program at Chatham House. "We know the Democrats will seek to take votes in the House and the Senate to constrain the Republicans, to restrain the administration on Iran war powers. But whether that will bind the administration is another matter."

Eurasia Group's Clayton Allen meanwhile told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" that while the war with Iran is unpopular at home, leaving the Middle East region without tangible wins would also be unpopular and that he expected an escalation within weeks.

Andrew Bailey, governor of the Bank of England — which hiked rates by 25 basis points on Thursday — told CNBC that energy price pressures stemmed from a combination of obstructions on the Strait of Hormuz, a shortage in refining capacity, and low European gas storage.

"A peace process in the Middle East will not shift all of those elements," he told CNBC's Steve Sedgwick on Thursday.

"Let's wait and see, because we have seen that fall in energy prices over the last 48 hours, but it is a very volatile situation, as we've learnt over the last seven, eight, nine months."

Ken Egan, head of European Sovereign Credit at KBRA, said the interest rate outlook would be primarily impacted by whether economies have enough underlying momentum for existing inflation pressures to become more persistent.

Europe is "heading into winter after months of elevated energy prices, and the full impact is still feeding through to the wider economy," he said by email.

"The concern for central banks is whether these pressures start to push up wages and prices more broadly, or whether weaker demand limits that pass-through."

A key question for the Federal Reserve will be whether the blue sweep "could lead to more fiscal prudence in the U.S. and thus less inflation," Jane Foley, head of FX strategy at Rabobank, said by email.

"It is very early, however, to be sure of the fiscal outlook, not least because many Democrats were voted in to soften cost of living pressures ... so it is very difficult to come to a firm conclusion at this point as to how the outlook for rates will change next year," she said.

The high level of U.S. fiscal stimulus and whether it is about to change is the "million dollar question" right now, James Sullivan, managing director and head of Asia Pacific equity research at J.P. Morgan, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia."

"The reasons why you're seeing inflation running high is a combination of a range of factors, from the war in Iran, to tariffs, to significant tightness in particular areas of the energy complex," he said.

"But it's also important to recognize the U.S. government has continued to spend at very high levels, into an economic expansion with low unemployment, and that's driving very specific macroeconomic implications. If that changes, you potentially take the pressure off the rate environment in a way that gives the Fed significantly more forward flexibility."

Daniel Clifton, head of policy research at Baird Strategas, said the impact on the data center buildout in the U.S. was another big factor to watch.

"In 2025, we had major fiscal headwinds, and it really didn't matter because we had such a big capex boom in the AI build-out last year. This year was driven a lot by stimulus," he told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe."

"If you have an environment where you have tighter fiscal policy — the Fed is raising rates right now — and you have a slowdown in the AI capex build out, you're probably going to reach a policy mistake at some point in mid-2027," he said.

"That's what happened in 2000 during the Y2K tech boom, the Fed was raising rates, we had higher oil prices, and they just went too far, and eventually it had a recession. Our forecast is not for a recession in the United States. Our forecast is that we'll have a mid-cycle slowdown in 2027."

#2947. U.S. economy added 165,000 jobs in October, way more than expected; unemployment rate slips to 4.1%

The U.S. economy added jobs at a much stronger than expected pace in October, while the unemployment rate moved lower as the labor market showed surprising strength.

Nonfarm payrolls increased by a seasonally adjusted 165,000 for the month, compared with the Dow Jones estimate for 59,000, the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported Friday.

The unemployment rate nudged down to 4.1%, compared with the forecast for 4.2%. A more encompassing measure that includes discouraged workers and those holding part-time jobs for economic reasons rose to 7.9%, up 0.3 percentage point, amid an increase of 421,000 in the civilian labor force.

In addition to the strong payroll gains for October, prior months were revised up substantially: The August figure moved up to 186,000, an upward revision of 53,000, while the September count moved to 60,000 following an upward revision of 31,000.

Payroll gains also came in better than forecast. Average hourly earnings increased 0.4% on the month and 3.1% annually, compared to respective estimates for 0.3% and 2.9%. The average work week was unchanged at 34.4 hours.

The breadth of job growth also was strong, contrary to a trend in which health care had accounted for the bulk of gains.

Professional and business services saw an increase of 70,000 to lead all sectors, while health care was next at 42,000. Other areas seeing notable growth included transportation and warehousing (25,000) and manufacturing (19,000).

The report comes the same week as an election in which the economy and in particular inflation was the center of attention. Democrats staged a nationwide rout that will give the party control of both houses of Congress after campaigning heavily on addressing affordability issues.

For the Federal Reserve, the report also comes at a pivotal time. Central bankers are weighing whether to resume raising interest rates in December as inflation remains stubbornly above the central bank's 2% target.

Traders increased the probability of another quarter percentage point hike when the Federal Open Market Committee meets Dec. 8-9. Odds rose to 76.8%, according to the CME Group's FedWatch tool, though the indicator pointed to only one more hike after. The odds of further hikes in 2027 have fallen somewhat in recent days, after the midterm election rout and Thursday's sell-off in tech stocks.

Market reaction otherwise was muted, with a mixed picture on stock market futures heading into the open. Treasury yields were higher across the board.

#2946. How Tanner McKee found out he was benched and what he's doing about it

How Tanner McKee found out he was benched and what he's doing about it originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Nick Sirianni was asked point-blank on Monday if a quarterback change was on the table and he shot it down emphatically. The Eagles were sticking with Tanner McKee, Sirianni insisted.

"Tanner's our guy," Sirianni said on Monday afternoon. "We're not making any changes there.

The next day McKee got the call from Sirianni.

Despite what the head coach said to the world the day before, he was making a quarterback switch. McKee has been benched and Joe Milton, who arrived less than three weeks ago, will start in Week 9 against the Giants. The news broke on Wednesday.

"That's one thing I appreciate about (the team)," McKee said on Thursday. "They've been very upfront about things. I know what he said in the media on Monday, the day before. But he said he wanted to communicate to the guys before he says anything to the media. He doesn't want us finding out in the media, which I appreciate."

McKee, 26, said he isn't on social media, so he didn't know about Sirianni's declaration on Monday until after he found out he was demoted. So at least there's that.

Jalen Hurts began the year as the Eagles' starting quarterback but has been out since the Panthers game with blood clots that are claiming his season. McKee made the last three starts and the Eagles lost all of them. While McKee isn't the only reason they lost, he also wasn't able to do enough to win.

Over the last three weeks, McKee completed 63.2% of his passes for 638 yards with 2 touchdowns and 2 interceptions. He had a passer rating of 76.6 as the Eagles lost to the Panthers, Cowboys and Commanders. And there's no doubt that McKee struggled on Sunday in Washington, especially with his pocket awareness.

"I think, watching the film, different decisions, different throws," McKee said. "Obviously, as a quarterback, have to take accountability for things like that. I think you have to go back and review everything that was done even throughout the week. I thought I had a great process. Thought I felt prepared. I don't have any regrets there. I think you have to go back and be like, 'What could I have done there?' And take that approach as well."

The Eagles initially drafted McKee in the sixth round out of Stanford back in 2023 and groomed him to be their backup quarterback. That was the job he held all of last season coming off a Super Bowl parade. This offseason, though, the Eagles brought in veteran Andy Dalton to compete for that back job and Dalton won. But Dalton was demoted after throwing an interception against the Bears in Week 3 and was eventually released.

It's definitely fair to question how the Eagles have handled the quarterback position all year. McKee went from competing for the backup job to being the 3, then the 2, then the 1 and is now the 2 again.

And he's not resigned to staying in that spot.

"This is the NFL. Competition is always open," McKee said. "I'll keep our conversation private. I don't know how much [Sirianni has] been open to the media about our conversation. But there's always competition. This is the NFL. So you gotta go out, you've got to perform every week. I'm going to approach it as a competition and be ready in case my number is called. Whether that's an injury or my number is just called."

It's not like McKee got to play with a full deck over the last three weeks. He was without DeVonta Smith and Dontavyion Wicks for the last two of those losses and Saquon Barkley hasn't been healthy either. Not to mention, the offensive line hasn't played up to its standard all season.

But when McKee was asked on Thursday why he wasn't given the benefit of the doubt with all the injuries, he made no excuses.

"We have great players and we had great players on the field," McKee said. "The expectation is to go out and win and unfortunately, I fell short doing that. And I gotta be better and make better plays."

Because Milton arrived less than three weeks ago, McKee has been helping him as much as possible this week. Milton missed all of the install during training camp and has had to catch up quickly before his first NFL start on Sunday.

Even though Milton took his job, McKee has been more than willing to help him this week.

"In sports, you gotta have a short memory," McKee said. "I'm still a Philadelphia Eagle. I want the team to perform so anything I can do to help the team, whether that's helping Joe or Jalen or whoever it is, that's my job, that's my role and that's what I'm going to do."

As a 2023 draft pick, McKee is in the final year of his rookie contract with the Eagles. Based on the way this season has gone, there seems to be a pretty good chance he won't be back with the team in 2027.

But McKee isn't trying to rush that fate. McKee was asked on Thursday if he wants to still be here the rest of this season or if he'd prefer a clean break.

"I love it here," he said. "It's a great place. I have a lot of confidence in the coaches, in our scheme, Sean (Mannion). And so I'm happy to be here. I love being here. And I love to compete. Like we said, in the NFL, things are going to go up and down. It's going to be a roller coaster. But you have to love that as a competitor and you have to embrace that. That's my approach. That's what I'm going to do."

#2945. Democratic sweep puts crypto on defensive in Washington

Democrats' sweep of Congress likely resets the political calculus for crypto, ushering in a more adversarial approach in Washington and pushing industry-friendly legislation like the Clarity Act to the back burner.

With crypto critics like Sherrod Brown, D-Ohio, returning to the Senate and Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., retaining significant influence over financial policy, analysts say the new Congress is likely to focus on scrutinizing President Donald Trump's personal crypto dealings over supportive industry legislation.

For years, crypto fought an uphill battle in Washington, asking for clear operating rules, but often landing on the receiving end of regulation by enforcement. That shifted after Trump's 2024 election victory, giving the industry a reprieve and seat at the policy table.

"We see Sherrod Brown winning in Ohio as damaging the crypto industry's political clout as the industry worked to defeat him," Jaret Seiberg, TD Cowen's Washington analyst, said in a note Wednesday. "We did not think Clarity could advance over the next two years. This makes us more confident in our prediction."

The crypto industry spent more than $200 million to sway the midterms, hoping to get leaders elected that could push a comprehensive crypto market structure bill over the finish line after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate.

Seiberg emphasized that Democrats "will investigate Trump's crypto entanglements including World Liberty Financial" and said "the resulting political environment may make it impossible to advance a version of Clarity that the crypto sector could back."

Mike Townsend, Charles Schwab's managing director of legislative and regulatory affairs, said he expects "Democrats to push next year for a version of the bill that is much tougher on cryptocurrency companies."

The stakes extend beyond market structure legislation via Clarity. Stablecoin innovation; decentralized finance, or DeFi, development; banking access; tokenization; and sensible tax policy are priorities the industry sees as important to ensuring the U.S. remains competitive in the global crypto economy.

The industry will need continued bipartisan engagement to ensure these priorities aren't sidelined by changing political dynamics. And Democrats have historically been less supportive of crypto than Republicans, though that divide has narrowed as more Democrats embrace the industry.

The implications of Democrats' big win aren't all bad. Crypto has reached a level of financial integration that could be difficult to reverse, with its future increasingly centered on establishing crypto as financial infrastructure. Even with the Clarity Act potentially stalled until 2028, key financial regulators can help advance the industry through rule-making.

Coinbase's vice president of U.S. policy, Kara Calvert, points out that the election results point to a historic shift in the makeup of the incoming Congress, with a record number of pro-crypto voices expected to join its ranks.

"With last night's results, the 120th Congress is on track to have more new pro-crypto members than we've ever seen in any freshman class," she said. "Crypto is 100% bipartisan, and that's why the future of finance does not rest on either political party or a single election night. Democrats and Republicans alike know that clear rules protect consumers and keep innovation in the U.S."

As of Thursday morning, 19 candidates characterized as pro crypto by the advocacy nonprofit Stand With Crypto have been elected to the Senate versus eight Senate candidates it characterizes as anti crypto. In the House, 228 pro-crypto candidates have been elected versus 106 the group deems anti crypto.

Several races have not been called, but Fairshake — the super PAC launched in 2023 to support pro-crypto candidates regardless of party — has so far won 29 of the 34 races it spent on, per Reuters, including three of the six House candidates it prioritized by spending a baseline of $1 million on their campaigns.

Dante Disparte, chief strategy officer and head of global policy and operations at Circle, emphasized Circle's bipartisan approach and readiness for new Senate and House leadership, but said it "may take longer to get Clarity tabled again."

That bipartisan support may offer a blueprint for what comes next, but even if crypto legislation stalls, progress can continue, said Joseph Chalom, CEO of ether treasury company Sharplink.

"We expect the Clarity Act to remain on hold through 2028. Even so, there is a clear path forward for stablecoins … and for the tokenization of public equities under the SEC's five-year innovation exemption," he said. "Technological progress is rarely rolled back. Once the benefits of faster settlement, lower costs and markets that never close become clear to investors and institutions, this shift will be very difficult to reverse, regardless of who wins the White House in 2028."

Near-term, investors should expect more volatility as the market tries to figure out how crypto regulation may shift under a Democratic Congress.

The impact of the blue wave "should vary considerably across assets," said James Butterfill, head of research at CoinShares. "We would expect U.S. crypto equities to be more sensitive than bitcoin, particularly businesses reliant on new regulatory permissions. Established, compliant operators could even benefit from greater scrutiny of competitors. So miners who already have data centers set up are likely to remain in high demand by AI hyperscalers."

— CNBC's Talia Kaplan contributed reporting.

#2943. LeBron James' lawyers send cease and desist to AI sports-betting app that used his name and likeness

Attorneys for Philadelphia 76ers star LeBron James sent a cease and desist letter to an AI sports-betting app that used a popular nickname for James and released an ad featuring his likeness, according to Front Office Sports.

Bron AI, a sports-gambling app, put out the ad Wednesday. In it, an AI version of James can be seen placing bets on NBA outcomes.

The ad, which has already been taken down on X, never showed James' face in AI, but showed a basketball player who looked a lot like James from behind. The player in the ad was wearing a No. 23 jersey that read "Bron" on the back, making it an exceptionally obvious reference to James.

In the cease and desist letter, which was obtained by Front Office Sports, James' attorneys make it clear James "neither approved any use and/or exploitation of, nor authorized or granted permission" to his name or likeness being used in the ad.

Additionally, James' attorneys pointed out that the 76ers star already has an "exclusive endorsement arrangement" with Polymarket and that the Bron AI ad appeared to show James' character placing bets on NBA games, which is "a very serious violation of NBA rules and regulations."

James is, apparently, not the only NBA star to issue a cease and desist letter to the company. James' former teammate, Los Angeles Lakers star Luka Dončić, reportedly also asked the company to stop using his likeness. Dončić's picture was used in the ad. Pictures of other athletes, such as Giannis Antetokounmpo, Jayden Daniels, Christian McCaffrey and Donovan Mitchell also appeared in the ad.

Given the egregious nature of the violation, Bron AI quickly pulled the ad from social media. It's unclear whether the company will face further backlash for the stunt.

#2942. CNBC's experts on whether the AI trade is ending and what investors should do next

Amid a powerful rout in tech stocks, investors told CNBC on Thursday that something may be fundamentally breaking in the AI trade following the big success of Democrats in Tuesday's midterm elections and the threat they try to slow the data center buildout.

Democrats are more aggressively anti-data center than Republicans, and Wall Street is quickly redoing its math what any restrictions they implement could mean for the related companies' future growth.

"Right now, investors are questioning that $1.2 trillion of capex that we forecast for next year," Peter Boockvar, chief investment officer at One Point BFG Wealth Partners, told CNBC. "Well, maybe that's not going to happen."

Boockvar believes this could be the start of the end of the AI trade as we know it.

Whether it's completely over or not, investors need to be reassessing how they're planning to play the AI trade going forward, the analysts said.

The Nasdaq Composite dropped 5% on Thursday and fell into correction territory, off by more than 11% from its record high set last month. The iShares Semiconductor ETF dropped 11% on Thursday, its biggest decline since during the Covid breakout in March 2020. The Global X Artificial Intelligence & Technology ETF (AIQ) is down 6% on the day and down 15% over the past month.

"There has been a huge change in the outlook for AI infrastructure buildout," Eric Johnston, head of equities at Cantor Fitzgerald, told CNBC. "The entire economy and the consumer and earnings growth are all levered to that buildout."

The selling Thursday spilled over to non-AI stocks with the Dow Jones Industrial average down more than 1,600 points.

Josh Brown pointed to the 4.8% yield for the 10-year Treasury yield, saying a compelling risk-free rate is causing investors to reconsider taking the risk in these volatile AI stocks.

"You don't need to be at 80% U.S. stocks and have 60% of that exposure be tech," Brown said.

Many investors believe the AI trade isn't over completely, investors just need to get more selective.

"The AI buildout just got harder," said Rob Sechan, Newedge Capital Group Founder & Managing Partner:

"Nobody voted to make compute less scarce," Sechan said, citing Microsoft's Azure cloud business growing 48% year over year in its most recent third quarter. That level of growth suggests its capacity constrained, not demand constrained, he said.

"When new capacity gets harder to build, existing capacity becomes more valuable," Sechan said.

Richard Clode, portfolio manager at Janus Henderson, noted a few bright spots in tech on Thursday despite the highly correlated downward movements in the big tech indices.

"Motorola Solutions had some good results," he said. "We're seeing a little bit of rotation into some of the … internet names...Sony reported some good results, and again they were up strongly overnight."

"So there are some brief pockets of respite from this, but obviously pretty painful across the board today," he said.

Some experts believe the selling was overdone and investors could start nibbling.

This "indiscriminate selling" is a "buying opportunity," said Larry Adam, Raymond James chief investment officer. If investors "take a longer term perspective, which is what we do, I think the valuations at today's levels will tend to prove to be a good buying opportunity," he said, urging investors to look at the fundamentals.

Victoria Greene, chief investment officer of G Squared Private Wealth, said she saw some strong supports around the S&P 500's 200-day moving average and was on the lookout for a market bounce following Thursday's rout.

"Typically, the average return is plus about 180 basis points the next day," she said. "The question we have to ask ourselves: Is this a pullback in an uptrend, which is what I believe? Or is this the end-all, be-all [and] the wheels are coming off and everybody just needs to buy T-bills? I don't think we're there yet."

#2940. In Roob's Eagles Stats: A big pile of misery broken up by tiny encouraging blips

In Roob's Eagles Stats: A big pile of misery broken up by tiny encouraging blips originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Thank God for Makai Lemon and Eli Stowers. Because without those two rookies, I wouldn't have much of a "Positive Section" in this week's Roob's Eagles Stats.

Even with them there aren't a whole lot of encouraging numbers surrounding this football team.

So with that in mind, here's this week's Roob's Eagles Stats. When you lose six in a row, the stats aren't pretty.

1. With Makai Lemon catching six passes for 57 yards and Eli Stowers three for 53 yards, this was only the second time in the last 36 years the Eagles have had two rookies over 50 receiving yards in the same game. The last time it happened was in 2021, when DeVonta Smith had 122 yards and Kenny Gainwell 58 in a loss to the Chiefs at the Linc. Before that in 1990 Fred Barnett had 55 yards and Calvin Williams 72 in a win over the Patriots at the Vet. Two rookie replacement players did it in a 1987 strike replacement game against the Cowboys, including Haverford High's Mike Siano, but last time it happened in a real road game was in 1973 with Charlie Young (112) and Don Zimmerman (51) in a loss to the 49ers at Candlestick Park.

2. Lemon now has 31 catches, 3rd-most among NFL rookies and only two fewer than rookie leader K.C. Concepcion of the Browns, who has 33. Germie Bernard of the Steelers has 32. Lemon is on pace for 65 catches, which would be 3rd-most in Eagles history by a rookie, behind Keith Jackson (81 in 1988) and Jordan Matthews (67 in 2014). With seven catches against Dallas and six against Washington, Lemon tied the franchise record of consecutive games by a rookie with six or more receptions. Seven others have done it, most recently Jeremy Maclin in 2009 and DeVonta Smith in 2021.

3. With his fourth sack on Sunday night, Jalen Carter now has 11 ½ in his career and moved into a tie for 7th-most career sacks by an Eagles interior lineman. He's just 1 ½ behind Javon Hargrave for sixth. The top five are Andy Harmon and Fletcher Cox (20 ½), Jerome Brown (19), Darwin Walker (17) and Kenny Clarke (16 ½). With his fourth sack Nolan Smith surpassed his total of 3.0 from all of last year and is now just 2 ½ sacks shy of his career high of 6 ½ in 2024, not including four more from the postseason.

OK, thus begins the reality section. Brace yourself. This is bad. Really bad.

4. The Eagles have had fewer than 350 yards of offense in all eight games so far this year, with a high of 341 at Tennessee. This is the first time in more than half a century they've opened a season with eight games under 350 net yards. Last time it happened was the first 11 games of the 1971 season, when their yardage high was 315 yards in a 37-14 loss to the Bengals in the season opener at Riverfront Stadium. Going back to last year, the Eagles have gone 10 straight games with fewer than 350 yards, their longest streak since an 11-gamer over the last nine games of 1977 and the first games of 1978. This is not good.

5. The Eagles have been outscored by 65 points through eight games, the most points they've been outscored by in their first eight games since 1998, when they were outscored by 117 points. You have to go back to 1972 to find the last time before 1998 they were outscored by this many points eight games into a season. The only NFL team outscored by a larger margin this year is the 0-8 Chargers, who have been outscored by 76 points. The Eagles have been outscored by 11 points by the 3-4 Panthers and by 17 points by the 2-4-1 Commanders and 3-5 Cowboys.

6. This is the first time since 1970 the Eagles have lost three games in a season by 11 or more points to teams with losing records. It's only the third season in franchise history they've lost two games by 17 or more points to teams with losing records. It also happened in 1972 and 2015. The only other time they lost back-to-back games by at least 17 points to losing teams came in 2015, when they lost 45-17 to the Bucs, who finished 6-10, and 45-14 to a Lions team that finished 7-9. Chip Kelly was fired four weeks later.

7. The Eagles led 10-0 Sunday before losing 27-10. Last time the Eagles led a game by 10 points in the first quarter and lost by at least 17 points was in 2011, when they took a 10-0 lead on the Patriots at the Linc on a LeSean McCoy touchdown and Alex Henery field goal before getting outscored 38-10 the rest of the game and losing 38-20, Tom Brady over Vince Young. This was the first game in franchise history where the Eagles led by 10 points in the first quarter and didn't score again.

8. With a 25-14 loss to the Panthers, 38-21 to the Cowboys and 27-10 to the Commanders, this is only the third time in the last 50 years the Eagles have lost three straight games by 11 or more points, allowing at least 25 points in all three. It also happened in 2012 and 2016. Last time the Eagles were outscored by 71 points in a six-game span was 2012.

9. Rasul Douglas's interception near the end of the game was the 23rd of his 10-year career with the Eagles, Panthers, Packers, Cards, Bills, Dolphins and Commanders. Douglas is one of only three Eagles drafted in the last 30 years with at least 23 career interceptions. The others are Sheldon Brown (26) and Jordan Poyer (25). Douglas, the Eagles' 3rd-round pick in 2017, is the first cornerback drafted by the Eagles to record an interception against the Eagles since Mark McMillian of the Chiefs picked off Rodney Peete in a 24-21 Chiefs win at the Vet in 1998.

10. The Eagles netted just 268 yards Sunday night, their fifth game during the six-game losing streak with fewer than 270 yards. They're averaging 244 yards per game during these last six games, their worst six-game span in 27 years, since they averaged 241 during a six-game span in 1999, Andy Reid's first season as head coach. This is the first time they've had five games under 270 yards in a six-game span since early in the 2003 season. No other NFL team has had five games under 270 yards this year. Their overall average of 265 yards per game is their worst through eight games since 1999, when they were averaging 254 through their eighth game.

#2936. Lakers’ Walker Kessler surprised where he ranks in key statistical category

SACRAMENTO — Walker Kessler has been every bit of the rim protector the Lakers hoped he would be when they made the investment they did to acquire him from the Jazz.

Lakers center Walker Kessler (14) has once again been one of the league's best rim protectors. AP Photo/Bethany Baker

Opponents shoot significantly worse and less near the rim when the 7-foot-2 center is on the floor.

He's top seven in the league in defensive field-goal percentage near the rim.

And after blocking four shots during the Lakers' 116-103 win over the Kings on Wednesday, Kessler ranked third in blocked shots per game entering Thursday — a stat that surprised him.

Walker Kessler was surprised when he found out he didn't rank second in blocked shots per game. Getty Images

"Third? I know [Victor Wembanyama's] ahead of me," Kessler said. "Who else is ahead of me?"

Kessler added: "But it's crazy. I thought I was at least No. 2 That's crazy."

Wembanyama, the reigning Defensive Player of the Year, was an easy guess considering he leads the league at 4.5 blocked shots per game after blocking eight shots in the Spurs' win over the Mavericks on Wednesday.

The only other player averaging more blocked shots than Kessler? Bucks big man Myles Turner, who entered Thursday averaging 2.8 blocked shots per game compared with Kessler's 2.7.

Walker Kessler has blocked the second-most shots so far this season. AP Photo/Mark J. Terrill

"Really?" Kessler responded when this information was relayed to him.

Technically, Kessler has blocked more shots this season. He's recorded 24 blocked shots, while Turner has 22.

But Kessler's nine games played compared with Turner's eight gives the Bucks center the edge when it comes to the season-long average.

It's unlikely either will threaten Wembanyama when it comes to the most blocked shots per game this season. The Spurs 7-4 phenom has led the NBA in the statistical category each season since being the top draft pick in 2023. But the race for No. 2 will be a fun one to monitor.

#2935. Eagles-Giants matchups to watch in Week 9

Eagles-Giants matchups to watch in Week 9 originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Eagles (2-6) host the Giants (4-2-1) on Sunday afternoon at Lincoln Financial Field.

While the Eagles are on a six-game losing streak, the Giants are in first place in the NFC East and coming off a bye week.

The Eagles have been having a tough time with blitzes all year and they're probably going to see a lot of them this week against the Giants and defensive coordinator Dennard Wilson. Maybe Joe Milton will be able to avoid them better than Tanner McKee.

Milton, 26, will make his first-career start on Sunday. While he isn't exactly a dual-threat QB, Milton has more ability to avoid pressure.

This season, the Eagles have faced 109 blitzes this season and have given up 66 pressures — a pressure rate of 60.6%, which is the highest in the NFL. They have also allowed a free rusher more than any other team in the league. So it would be crazy for teams to not blitz them at this point. And the Giants already blitz at the second highest rate in the NFL, per NextGen Stats. And the Vikings barely count because Brian Flores is playing a different sport right now:

1. Vikings: 71.0%2. Giants: 40.3%3. Buccaneers: 40.2%4. Patriots: 40.1%5. Panthers: 39.6%

Wilson was with the Eagles in 2021 and 2022 and was passed over for the defensive coordinator job in 2023, when the Eagles went with Sean Desai. He has since served as DC with the Titans and now Giants.

We've watched Saquon Barkley (hamstring/back) try to gut it out for the last two games and with the bye week coming up, he'll probably try to do it again against his former team on Sunday afternoon. Barkley managed to play 22 snaps against the Commanders in Week 8 and wasn't very productive. He had 13 carries for just 30 yards and a touchdown, as well as a 24-yard catch. But Barkley is still obviously the Eagles' best option. Even playing through these injuries, Barkley has shown how good he still is. And the Eagles have already told him they're not moving him ahead of the trade deadline.

The Giants this season have the No. 26-ranked run defense, giving up 128.1 yards per game. Before their bye week, the Giants held the Texans to just 84 rushing yards in an overtime game but gave up 175 to the Saints and 202 to the Commanders. So they might still be susceptible on the ground. The Giants are giving up 1.66 yards before contact per attempt, which ranks 27th in the league. The Eagles will have the same OL again this week for the third straight game.

The Giants have some very good edge rushers but the team hasn't gotten many sacks this season. They are tied with the Chiefs and Dolphins at the bottom of the league with 9.0 in seven games. (The Eagles have just 10 in eight games.)

But the Giants still have Abdul Carter, Kayvon Thibodeaux and rookie Arvell Reese to bring the pressure. And then they have a DC who is always willing to add more pressure by blitzing. This won't be an easy game for Jordan Mailata and Markel Bell. Bell, the rookie from Miami, had a rough game against the Commanders in Week 8 and has had some ups and downs since taking over for Lane Johnson. But the Eagles need to really figure out what they have in him over the final nine games of the season. This will be a good test on Sunday.

The Giants are coming off the bye week but it's unclear if that time off was enough to get Cam Skattebo back from a hamstring injury. The Giants' starting running back hurt his hammy in a run late in the Giants' overtime win over the Texans before the bye. If Skattebo is unavailable, the Giants would have Devin Singletary and Gregory Desroisiers as their top backs.

Skattebo this season leads the Giants in rushing with 418 yards on 98 carries (4.3) and 2 touchdowns. Skattebo is a battering ram, who is averaging 3.6 yards after contact per rush. The Eagles have had some tackling issues this season, so they'll need to get him to the ground.

Much has been made about the Eagles' inability to rush the passer this year, and that's true. But it starts because they're unable to stop the run. Jordan Davis is supposed to be their run-stuffing anchor but he has been dealing with a hand injury that has slowed him down. The Eagles are 28th in run defense this season, allowing 131.9 yards per game. The Eagles have given up 100+ rushing yards in every single game this season. That's their longest such streak to start a season since 1968.

The Giants used the No. 6 overall pick to draft Nabers out of LSU in the 2024 season and he's on pace to have a career year in 2026. As a rookie, Nabers went for over 1,200 yards but tore his ACL in Year 2 and missed most of the season. Nabers has come back from that ACL tear better than ever this season. Through seven games, he has 48 catches on 70 targets for 605 yards and 2 touchdowns. He's averaging 86.4 yards per game and has had over 100 yards in each of his last four games. Nabers lines up a bit in the slot but is mostly outside. The Eagles haven't used Quinyon Mitchell to travel with top receivers yet this season but it might be a good week to try it.

Mitchell has been excellent this season but proved he's human when he gave up a 53-yard pass to Treylon Burks in the third quarter. But Mitchell has been excellent all season. He has given up 27 catches on 50 targets for 293 yards. So 18% of the yards he has given up this season have come on one play. He has an opponent passer rating of 69.8 this year.

Through eight games this season, Jalen Carter already has four sacks and is on pace to have a career-high in 2026. But that obviously doesn't tell the whole story. At times, Carter looks dominant, but he hasn't been consistent enough. Even Vic Fangio has admitted that this season. But if the Eagles' defense is going to figure things out in 2026, then Carter has to be the motor behind it.

In this game, Carter will get some matchups against Giants first-round pick Francis Mauigoa. The Giants used the No. 10 overall pick on the offensive lineman out of Miami. But because they didn't have a spot at offensive tackle, the Giants have moved him inside the right guard for his rookie season. While Mauigoa has had some ups and downs this season, he has flashed some real potential.

#2929. How new Eagles QB Joe Milton is preparing for unlikely first career start

How new Eagles QB Joe Milton is preparing for unlikely first career start originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

A few weeks ago, Joe Milton was in meetings and film study with Dak Prescott and former Eagle Sam Howell in the Cowboys' quarterback room at the team facility in Frisco, Texas.

Now he's the Eagles' starting quarterback.

"You never know what can happen in the NFL," Milton said after practice Thursday. "Yes, I was on the practice squad and I was just preparing for any moment that presents itself. Still, when I was at Dallas on the practice squad, I was just pretty much going through the plays, going through the week like I was a starter and just being ready.

"And that's what the NFL is about. Just being ready when your name is called and just preparing for those moments."

The Eagles signed Milton off the Cowboys' practice squad on Oct. 16, and on Sunday – 23 days later and after just 10 practices – he'll make his first NFL start against the Giants at the Linc.

"My coaches here, Parks (Frazier) and Sean (Mannion), did a great job of getting me caught up early," Milton said. "And I spent a lot of time inside the building just trying to learn this offense and the communication aspect of it.

"It's a lot of new terminology for me, so being in my third offense, just trying to plug and play and go from there. … Just executing whatever the play call is that comes through my helmet. Just call that play and execute. Know my job. Know my 1/11th. Do my 1/11th, and make a play."

Milton spent 2024 with the Patriots playing in Alex Van Pelt's offense then joined the Cowboys last year playing under Klayton Adams.

A lot of learning, a lot of new schemes, new coaches, new teammates. Just constant change for the 26-year-old Milton.

"It's just a whole new offense," Milton said. "I just came from my previous team, and a lot of stuff was just different. So coming here was just pretty much learning a whole new system of the West Coast and just going with that.

"Kind of go back to my rookie year, rookie year offense."

The Eagles were 2-3 this year under Jalen Hurts, now out for the season with blood clots, and 0-3 under Tanner McKee, benched this week for Milton.

This will be the first season since 2002 where the Eagles will start three different quarterbacks in meaningful games. In 2002, Donovan McNabb started the first 10 games, before breaking his right leg vs. the Cards at the Linc, Koy Detmer started vs. the 49ers at Candlestick but suffered a dislocated elbow and A.J. Feeley started the next five games before McNabb returned for the final two regular-season games and the playoffs.

Asked what people who haven't seen him play – which is pretty much everybody – should expect, Milton didn't talk about his mobility or his rocket-cannon arm, he just spoke about playing disciplined football within the scheme.

"Doing what the coach asks, going out there and whatever play is called, just going out there and executing that play, and nothing else matters in the world except for that play," he said. "So whatever it takes, whatever the team needs me to do, that's what I'm willing to do. If I have to run, if I have to throw or hand it off, it's perfect."

Milton hasn't started a game although he played most of a win against the Bills for the Patriots on the final day of the 2024 season, throwing for one touchdown and running for another. He got into four games with the Cowboys last year – all in mop-up duty late in blowout losses – and threw a touchdown and two INTs.

In parts of five games with the Patriots and Cowboys, he's completed 37 of 53 passes (70 percent) for 424 yards with two TDs and two INTs with a 90.4 passer rating.

Milton is the first quarterback who'll start for the Eagles after not being here on opening day since Ken O'Brien in 1993. He's the first former Cowboy to start at quarterback for the Eagles since Rodney Peete in 1995. He's the third NFL QB to make his first career start this year, following Jalon Daniels of the Buccaneers and Joe Fagnano of the Ravens.

"I approach the week the same," he said. "Came in here, worked hard and stayed late, got the plays like I always do for every week since I've been here, and it's been going good so far."

Milton said his 1-year-old daughter, Maleia, helps keep him grounded when things are kind of nutty.

"I mean, I always (want to) be where my feet are," he said. "My daughter does a great job of reminding me of that. Yes, she can't talk yet, but at the same time, when I see her face, it just allows me to be thankful. So when I see her every night on FaceTime, I'm just thankful. It allows me to take a deep breath and remember where I'm at. And being where my feet are is probably the best thing because you think too far and you never know what (can) happen."

#2924. Nick Sirianni emphatically backs OL coach Chris Kuper amid losing streak

Nick Sirianni emphatically backs OL coach Chris Kuper amid losing streak originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Chris Kuper was dealt a tough hand, but this is bad.

When the Eagles hired Kuper as their offensive line coach this offseason to replace the legendary Jeff Stoutland, the hope was that the Eagles would be healthy up front and return to dominance. That hasn't gone to plan.

And through eight games, the Eagles' struggling offensive line is one of the big reasons they are 2-6 and losers of six straight. But head coach Nick Sirianni still believes in Kuper. He gave an impassioned defense of his OL coach on Monday afternoon.

"Chris is coaching his [butt] off," Sirianni said on Monday. "He's fighting his [butt] off, he's coaching his [butt] off on a daily basis. You guys don't get to see that. I get it on a daily basis.

"You get to see that for three hours on Sunday, but he's coaching his [butt] off and he's fighting his [butt] off and I know he's giving the players good tools to use fundamentally and helping them be the best players that they possibly can be. But we all have to be better and we know that."

The next morning on 94WIP, Sirianni said more of the same about Kuper when asked about the way Stoutland departed the organization. He also said the Eagles' players have faith in Kuper and you can ask them. That might be true, but that can't matter more than results; and the results aren't good.

Through eight games, the Eagles are 30th in points and 32nd in total yards.

It's impossible to say if having Stoutland this season would have helped the Eagles greatly. But it's also impossible not to wonder, especially because his departure came after his role was diminished by Sirianni during the 2025 season. Stoutland is one of the greatest coaches in franchise history but even his line struggled last year.

It's also fair to point out that the line the Eagles have been using for much of the season is very different than what they expected entering the year. At the start of the season, the Eagles were banking on Lane Johnson, Landon Dickerson and Cam Jurgens. Well, Johnson retired after playing in three games, Jurgens was traded to the Ravens and Dickerson has played just 3 of 8 games.

Here's a look at the Eagles' OL throughout this season:

Week 1: Jordan Mailata, Landon Dickerson, Cam Jurgens, Tyler Steen, Lane JohnsonWeek 2: Jordan Mailata, Cam Jurgens, Drew Kendall, Tyler Steen, Lane JohnsonWeek 3: Jordan Mailata, Cam Jurgens, Drew Kendall, Tyler Steen, Lane JohnsonWeek 4: Jordan Mailata, Cam Jurgens, Drew Kendall, Tyler Steen, Markel BellWeek 5: Jordan Mailata, Willie Lampkin, Jake Majors, Tyler Steen, Markel BellWeek 6: Jordan Mailata, Willie Lampkin, Jake Majors, Tyler Steen, Markel BellWeek 7: Jordan Mailata, Landon Dickerson, Drew Kendall, Tyler Steen Markel BellWeek 8: Jordan Mailata, Landon Dickerson, Drew Kendall, Tyler Steen Markel Bell

In addition to the constant state of flux on the line, the Eagles have dealt with a ton of injuries to other players, including Jalen Hurts, DeVonta Smith, Saquon Barkley and more.

So it wasn't reasonable to expect the Eagles' offensive line to completely save them. But it's not unreasonable to expect better execution when it comes to things like assignments and communication. The Eagles have struggled with simple things up front at times this year. The Eagles have the 27th-ranked rushing offense in the NFL in 2026 and have given up a pressure rate of 42.65, which ranks 29th.

And the Eagles have been absolutely flummoxed by blitzes all year. They entered Week 8 allowing the highest pressure rate on blitzes in the NFL this season (57.7%), per NextGen Stats, and were even worse against the Commanders on Sunday Night Football. The Commanders generated 10 pressures on 12 blitzes (83.3%), which is the second-highest pressure rate on blitzes by a team in the 2026 season.

The Eagles have now faced 109 blitzes this season and have given up 66 pressures — an outrageous rate of 60.6% The Eagles have also allowed a free rusher on 18.2% of their passing plays this season, the most of any team, according to Ryan Paganetti.

A big focus for the offense this week is to fix the communication errors that have plagued them through eight weeks. Sean Mannion also expressed his confidence in Kuper this week.

"I think Koop's been doing an outstanding job," Mannion said. "I know, like I said, everything ultimately starts with me and making sure I'm charting the right course for us in terms of our protection plan, in terms of how we're getting it taught, in terms of our install meetings and everything like that. But Chris is doing a great job. He's a tremendous teacher of the fundamentals. He's great with those guys in the room."

Kuper was not retained by the Vikings after the 2025 season and the Eagles hired him with the hope that his knowledge in this system would help the transition to Mannion. Nothing on offense has worked this year.

So there's no doubt that the pressure on Kuper has started to get ratcheted up in recent weeks as the Eagles' struggles continue. But the Eagles seem like they're sticking with him for now.

There are a lot of problems with the Eagles' offense this season, as Sirianni is happy to share.

"Again, we all have to do better," he said. "I think, again, we want to zero in on one thing. We're not coaching well enough right now and we're not playing well enough right now. I know we're speaking offensively and right now we're not doing all the things we need to do to win football games, and that falls on everyone's shoulders. It's not just one thing. We have to dig deep as coaches, as players, and again, find solutions, find answers."

#2923. Joe Brady on cutting S C.J. Gardner-Johnson: "It was the best interests of the football team"

On the day the NFL suspended former Bills safety C.J. Gardner-Johnson for spitting on an opponent, Buffalo coach Joe Brady said the team would not be cutting Gardner-Johnson. After the game that Gardner-Johnson missed, the Bills cut him.

Meeting with reporters on Thursday, Brady explained the reversal.

“There was a lot to evaluate,” Brady said. “You know, we got together and figured out, ‘Hey, this is what we felt like this was the best thing to do for the Buffalo Bills.’ I have so much love for [C.J.]. Like, I really do. I loved getting to coach him. I loved being around him. I loved — I did love the edge, and his personality allowing him to be him, right?, and as a person. So that didn’t make the decision easy at all.

“But we also have to be an organization that, you know, it’s unfortunate obviously what happened, you know? But it also opened a door and an opportunity for guys the other day, and that was a part of evaluation. And seeing how those guys kind of played, and you know with a lot of the roster moves. And, again, this wasn’t — we didn’t come out of the game post Vegas and say, ‘This is what we were going to do. We’re just gonna do this the following Monday.’ We were, you know, the plan was him being here and we were going to evaluate it after the game in Baltimore. And we felt like with some of the moving parts we had to do it was the best interests of the football team.”

Gardner-Johnson’s suspension opened the door for Damar Hamlin to start the game against the Ravens. And Hamlin apparently did well enough to help the Bills realize that, all things considered, the “best interests” of the Bills meant the Bills were better off without the “high maintenance” Gardner-Johnson.

#2921. Walker Kessler admitted Domantas Sabonis outplayed him before strong fourth quarter

Prior to Wednesday's fourth quarter, Walker Kessler was sitting on five points, three rebounds and two steals — far lesser production than he'd proven capable of thus far in this season. Then, he exploded for a 10-point, five-rebound, four-block, one-steal final frame to finish with 15 points, eight rebounds, four blocks and one steal. Those are numbers much more in line with the 25-year-old's early season signature.

Kessler's counterpart in the middle was Sacramento Kings center Domantas Sabonis. He's a rugged, brawny big man who's unafraid to launch elbows and hips into defenders for space down low and also threads passes to teammates for easy scores. Before that fourth-quarter renaissance, Kessler recognized the Lithuanian veteran — who logged 16 points, 17 rebounds (six on the offensive glass), one assist and one steal — was getting the best of him.

"He's a very, very talented player. Really physical player," Kessler told reporters after the Los Angeles Lakers' 116-103 win on Wednesday. "Definitely got my tail kicked a little bit in the first three quarters. But I relied on my teammates and they did a good job of encouraging me … At the end of the day, just trying to win and started to roll my way a little bit."

While likely past his All-NBA apex at this stage of his career, the 30-year-old Sabonis remains a good player and tough cover. For Kessler to struggle through three quarters but still stay in the fight mentally to bounce back and help fuel a third straight Lakers victory is encouraging. Of course, the white-hot exploits of Luka Dončić, who notched his fourth consecutive 40-point outing, didn't ding Los Angeles' win probability either.

Kessler will face another big, strong body Friday night when Donovan Clingan and the West-leading, 6-1 Portland Trail Blazers come to town. It'll mark both the Lakers' second meeting of the year with Portland and their second NBA Cup Group Play game. After blowing a big lead to the Golden State Warriors last Friday in their opening NBA Cup matchup of 2026-27, they must win this one to maintain any hope of advancing to the Quarterfinals. Tipoff is schedule for 7 p.m.

#2920. Eagles snap counts: Time to see more of Eli Stowers

Eagles snap counts: Time to see more of Eli Stowers  originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Eagles have lost six games in a row and you're probably tired of watching Johnny Mundt and Grant Calcaterra play football.

That's probably a big harsh but the Eagles have now lost six games in a row and they're simply not going to recover this season. It's time to see what some younger players can do and Eli Stowers is right at the top of that list.

Here's how the Eagles' tight end snaps broke down in Sunday night's 27-10 loss to the Commanders:

Dallas Goedert: 55 snaps (80%)Johnny Mundt: 13 snaps (19%)Eli Stowers: 13 snaps (19%)Grant Calcaterra: 12 snaps (19%)

The Eagles signed Mundt to a one-year deal this offseason with the promise that he would be an ideal blocking tight end. Howie Roseman talked about needing to find that element for the tight end room and Mundt was supposed to be that guy. It hasn't worked. So even though Stowers doesn't fit that mold — he's more of an athletic pass catcher — it's time to see more of him.

In his second NFL game, Stowers caught his first NFL pass. It was a 7-yarder in the third quarter to move the chains on a 2nd-6. He also caught a couple passes in the final moments of the fourth quarter to give him 3 for 53 against the Commanders. In a season like this it becomes important to develop young talent. Stowers had 12 snaps in his debut and 13 in his second game; the Eagles should continue to increase his playing time.

• Tanner McKee and his entire offensive line played all 69 snaps against the Commanders. McKee was under pressure all night and was sacked five times. The blitz kept getting home.

• Without DeVonta Smith and Dontayvion Wicks, the Eagles were obviously short-handed at receiver. Here's how the snaps broke down: Darius Cooper – 66 snaps (96%), Makai Lemon – 65 snaps (94%), Hollywood Brown – 32 snaps (46%), Elijah Moore – 18 snaps (26%). Lemon did some good things with 6 catches on 9 targets for 57 yards.

• Saquon Barkley played through hamstring and back injuries and got a total of 22 snaps (32%). He was bottled up way more in this game than he was a week ago against Dallas. Elijah Mitchell led the running back room with 39 snaps (57%) and Jaydon Blue got on the field for 8 snaps (12%).

• Willie Lampkin and Fred Johnson were on the field for that Barkley direct-snap touchdown in the first quarter. Lampkin lined up as a lead blocker.

• Cooper DeJean, Zack Baun and Quinyon Mitchell each played all 60 defensive snaps. Drew Mukuba (59) and Riq Woolen (58) were right behind them. It was another forgettable performance by the defense but at least Mukuba made a couple plays. He forced a fumble and had an interception that was negated because of a penalty.

• Without Jihaad Campbell (hamstring/quad), Jeremiah Trotter Jr. played 54 of 60 snaps (90%) and led the Eagles in tackles with 10.

• The DT rotation: Jalen Carter – 49 snaps (82%), Jordan Davis – 37 snaps (62%), Moro Ojomo – 36 snaps (60%), Byron Young – 15 snaps (25%), Ty Robinson – 9 snaps (15%).

• The edge rotation: Nolan Smith – 47 snaps (78%), Jonathan Greenard – 28 snaps (47%), Arnold Ebiketie – 24 snaps (40%), Jalyx Hunt – 21 snaps (35%). Smith returned from a concussion and at least provided some juice.

• Kenny Moore played 29 snaps but got banged up in the game. Marcus Epps ended up playing 13 snaps.

#2915. ‘There's no magic potion:' Vic Fangio pinpoints the issue with Jonathan Greenard

'There's no magic potion:' Vic Fangio pinpoints the issue with Jonathan Greenard originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Jonathan Greenard, the Eagles' $98 million free agent edge rusher, doesn't have much to show for his first two months as an Eagle.

Two tackles, one quarterback hit and one forced fumble (that the Eagles didn't recover) to show in 118 snaps over parts of five games.

"What I see is a guy that has had very, very little practice," Fangio said Tuesday. "I mean, when I say very little, minuscule.

"He missed all of training camp, missed the first two weeks of the season, no practice, started practicing, was on a pitch count, played 19 plays, was going to increase the next week, got hurt early in that game, didn't finish that game, didn't play the next game, and he's been limited in practice."

Greenard hasn't had a full week of practice yet. The Eagles, 2-6 with a six-game losing streak, open the practice week on Wednesday for a home game Sunday against the Giants.

Greenard has had exactly six days of full practice so far this year – the Friday before the Bears game, all three days leading up to the Rams, the Friday leading up to the Panthers and the Friday before the game Sunday night in Washington.

"You know, contrary to some people's popular belief, practice is important," Fangio said. "And he has had next to none."

Greenard missed all of training camp and the first two games with a pectoral injury, played 18 snaps against the Bears and 19 against the Rams, then missed the Jaguars game with an ankle injury. He's played 24, 29 and 28 snaps the last three weeks with modest results.

Prior to the Cowboys game, Greenard was limited on Thursday and missed practice Friday and Saturday. Last week, he was limited at practice on Wednesday and Thursday and full on Friday.

When he has played, Greenard hasn't been close to the player the Eagles envisioned. This is a guy who had 24 ½ sacks in 2023 and 2024 and made a Pro Bowl in 2024. His $24.5 million average annual salary is 12th-highest among all NFL edge rushers.

"Practice," Fangio said. "You know, there's no pill, there's no magic potion. You can watch all the tape you want, you can sit in meetings all you want, you can do walkthroughs all you want. The guy's got to practice. Practice is important for everybody."

The Eagles could sure use some pass pressure.

Nolan Smith has four sacks but he's the Eagles' only edge rusher with any sacks. Overall, the Eagles have just 10 sacks, 4th-fewest in the league and the fewest in franchise history through eight games.

You always take Pro Football Focus grades with a grain of salt, but out of 118 edge rushers who've played at least 100 snaps his 57.1 overall grade ranks 93rdand his 65.1 pressure grade ranks 77th.

According to Stathead analytics, Greenard has no quarterback hurries, and he's played the 6th-most snaps among all edge rushers who have no sacks or hurries.

"Yeah, he's just not into a groove," Fangio said. "He's not comfortable in our system totally and what we want him to do. And it's not because he doesn't know or doesn't want to. It's just he hasn't executed it. Walk-throughs are an assignment check. It's not execution.

"I don't know that I've had a guy that's had so little practice."

#2913. 10 things you need to know about new Eagles quarterback Joe Milton

10 things you need to know about new Eagles quarterback Joe Milton originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Joe Milton on Sunday will become the Eagles' third starting quarterback this year and the first former Cowboy to start at quarterback for the Eagles in more than 30 years.

What fruit is Milton famous for throwing the length of a football field? What long-time Eagles safety went to the same high school as Milton? Who's the only other quarterback drafted by the Patriots to play for the Eagles?

You'll find answers to these questions and several other fascinating nuggets in our 10 Things you Need to Know about Joe Milton.

Guaranteed it's more interesting than 10 Things you Need to Know about Andy Dalton.

1. Milton's cousin is Anquan Bolden, the long-time Arizona Cardinals wide receiver, who caught 1,076 passes for 13,779 yards in his 14-year career. Both went to Pahokee, Fla., High School, also the alma mater of long-time Eagles safety Andre Waters. Another cousin of Milton's is Kevin Bouie, the Eagles' 7th-round pick out of Mississippi State. Bouie, who also attended Pahokee High School, never got into a regular-season game for the Eagles but played for the Chargers in 1996 and Cards in 1997.

2. Milton spent the first two years of his college career at Michigan, backing up Shea Patterson. During the 2020 COVID season, Milton started three games but gradually lost playing time to Cade McNamara and then transferred to Tennessee before the 2021 season. Milton was hurt most of 2021 and backed up Hendon Hooker for most of 2022, although he did win two late-season starts after Hooker suffered a torn ACL in a game against South Carolina. Milton went 8-4 in 2023 with 20 touchdown passes and five interceptions, passing for 2,769 yards and rushing for 292 with six more TDs.

3. Milton was originally the Patriots' 6th-round pick in 2024. He played in one game in New England, going 22-for-29 for 241 yards and a 48-yard touchdown pass to Kayshon Boutte in a 23-16 win over the Bills on the final day of the 2024 season after Drake Maye played one series. Milton also ran for a touchdown. Patriots head coach Jerod Mayo was fired soon after the game ended. Milton's passer rating of 111.4 was highest by a Patriots rookie since Bailey Zappe had a 118.4 in a game against the Browns in 2022

4. Although Milton has thrown about a quarter as many career passes as Tanner McKee – 202 to 53 – the strong-armed Milton has two more completions of at least 35 yards than McKee. Milton threw a 48-yard TD to Boutte in 2024 and a 35-yarder to Jalen Tolbert for the Cowboys in a 44-23 loss to the Broncos last October. McKee's longest career completion is the 34-yarder to Eli Stowers on the final play of the loss to the Commanders Sunday. In his career, Milton has averaged 8.0 yards per attempt and 11.5 yards per completion, compared to McKee's 6.1 yards per attempt and 9.8 yards per completion. Among 79 active quarterbacks who've thrown at least 50 passes, Milton's 8.0 yards per attempt is 3rd-highest behind Brock Purdy and Maye and his 11.5 yards per completion ranks 22nd.

5. Milton will become the Eagles' first quarterback to start a game after joining the team after opening day in 33 years. In 1993, the Eagles signed Ken O'Brien on Oct. 4, and he started four games, going 0-4, the last four starts of his career. O'Brien, who had made two Pro Bowls in nine seasons with the Jets, made his first start as an Eagle against the Cowboys on Oct. 31, 27 days after he signed. Milton will top that, starting against the Giants on Nov. 8, only 23 days after the Eagles acquired him on Oct. 16.

6. Milton will become the first quarterback from the University of Tennessee to throw a pass for the Eagles since Pat Ryan played in four games at the end of 1991. Ryan threw no touchdowns and four interceptions as an Eagle and had a passer rating of 10.3, worst by an Eagles quarterback who threw at least 25 passes since 1943. The Eagles' only other Tennessee quarterback was Johnny Butler, who threw 13 passes in 1943 and lost his only start, 13-7, to the Brooklyn Dodgers at Ebbets Field in 1943. Previous quarterbacks who've played for both the Eagles and Cowboys include Cunningham, Rodney Peete and Mark Sanchez. Only Peete played for the Eagles after the Cowboys.

7. Milton will be the second quarterback (and third player) drafted by the Patriots to throw a regular-season pass for the Eagles. Matt Cavanaugh, the Patriots' 2nd-round pick out of Pitt in 1978, spent 1986 through 1989 with the Eagles backing up Ron Jaworski and Randall Cunningham. He went 1-1 in two starts for the Eagles, both in 1986. Cavanaugh spent 13 years in the NFL – four with the Patriots, three with the 49ers, four with the Eagles and two with the Giants. He was 8-11 in 19 career starts with 28 touchdowns and 30 interceptions. After retiring from the NFL, Cavanaugh coached with the Cards under Buddy Ryan, the 49ers under George Seifert, the Bears under Dave Wannstedt, the Ravens under Brian Billick (where he was Cunningham's final NFL offensive coordinator), the Jets under Rex Ryan, the Bears again under Marc Trestman, Washington under Jay Gruden, the Jets again under Robert Saleh and he's currently a senior offensive with the Commanders under Dan Quinn. The only other player drafted by the Patriots to throw a pass for the Eagles was safety Joe Scarpati, the Patriots' 13th-round pick in 1964. He was 1-for-2 with a three-yard completion to Sam Baker in a 1968 win over the Lions at Tiger Stadium.

8. Following Jalen Hurts, Andy Dalton and McKee, Milton will become the fourth Eagles quarterback to throw a pass this year. Last time four quarterbacks threw a pass for the Eagles in the same season was 1991 when five did – Randall Cunningham, Jim McMahon, Brad Goebel, Jeff Kemp and Pat Ryan.

9. How strong is Milton's arm? You can find the video online, but while he was at Tennessee he threw an orange an estimated 100 yards. Milton, during a practice at the Vols' 80-yard practice facility, hurled the orange from his own end zone across the field and into the facility's back wall. He is said to have thrown a football 100 yards as well and a baseball 95 miles-per-hour.

10. Tradition in Pahokee is for kids to chase and catch rabbits that scatter when local farmers burn the sugarcane fields in the fall. According to local tradition, the faster you are and the more rabbits you catch, the better football career you'll have. Milton even has a tattoo of a rabbit on his arm to honor the tradition. Another Pahokee native, Janoris Jenkins, a Pro Bowl cornerback with the Giants, actually went by Jackrabbit Jenkins during his 11-year NFL career to commemorate his hometown tradition.

#2910. Yaxel Lendeborg has changed everything for Steph Curry, Warriors this season

This was supposed to be a wasted season for Steph Curry.

The Warriors were injury-riddled and coming off a lackluster summer in which they made no major moves.

It meant the greatest shooter of all time was destined to tread water as he turned 39 years old.

He has resuscitated Curry's career. He has breathed new life into a flailing dynasty. He has given the Warriors their future franchise star.

Lendeborg was a question mark when the Warriors selected him as the No. 11 pick in the 2026 draft.

Can a jack-of-all-trades turn into a star? Can a 24-year-old surpass expectations or has he already peaked? Can a rookie really be NBA ready?

Lendeborg has emphatically answered those questions.

He's averaging 21.5 points (50% shooting from the field, 40% from 3), seven rebounds and 3.9 assists for the Warriors, who have won five games in a row.

Golden State Warriors' Yaxel Lendeborg dribbles against the Phoenix Suns during an NBA game at Chase Center in San Francisco, Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

The Warriors are good. Curry has a wingman. This is thrilling for anyone who loves basketball.

No one seems to be savoring this more than Curry, whose joy has been on full display amid the Warriors' unexpected rise.

During his 44-point deluge against the Timberwolves on Oct. 28, Curry flashed a lunge pose after sinking a shot from beyond the arc.

After his jab sent Luka Doncic onto his butt and he swished a 3-pointer against the Lakers on Oct. 30, he did the Edvard Munch celebration, putting his hands on his cheeks and opening his mouth in shock.

And after scoring 34 points against the Suns on Monday, he didn't hesitate when asked what has changed for the Warriors this season.

"I mean, Yax," Curry said, flashing a smile.

Golden State Warriors guard Steph Curry warms up before a game against the Los Angeles Lakers in Los Angeles, Wednesday, Oct. 21, 2026. London Entertainment

Curry and Lendeborg are the NBA's second-highest scoring duo (51.5 points per game), behind Luka Dončić and Austin Reaves (61.8). Lendeborg's success has been a life raft for the Warriors, who are without Jimmy Butler and Moses Moody for the first few months of the season and without Kristaps Porzingis indefinitely.

In many ways, Lendeborg has unlocked Curry. He has made his brilliance count. He has lit a new fire under him.

Curry, who missed 27 straight games because of runner's knee last season, is averaging 30 points (46.8% shooting from the field, 42.9% from beyond the arc), 4.2 rebounds and 4.5 assists per game.

He just surpassed Michael Jordan (44) for the most 40-point games in NBA history since turning 30, notching his 45th such performance last week.

Curry is playing as though he were a decade younger. And that's because a guy 14 years younger than him is making all of this meaningful.

It's funny because this really couldn't have worked out better for both parties.

Curry, a two-time MVP and four-time champion, is doing what he loves most: winning.

And the outspoken Lendeborg has found a team that needs him to contribute immediately and encourages his bravado, two things that could've been problematic with other franchises.

Yaxel Lendeborg #1 of the Golden State Warriors drives to the basket against Jalen Green #4 of the Phoenix Suns in San Francisco on Nov. 2, 2026. Getty Images

And neither is being politically correct.

The rookie recently raised eyebrows when he stunningly said he lost all respect for Dončić following their trash talk in the season opener. The Warriors didn't wince.

Instead, Curry, Draymond Green and coach Steve Kerr unapologetically had his back.

Lendeborg landed in the perfect situation. He has a mentor in Green, who's famous for unabashedly speaking his mind. And he has a franchise behind him with a win-now mentality that needs him to shine.

Lendeborg has found his home. He can flourish here. He needs to flourish here.

It's in the form of a rookie who has made it so that instead of treading water he's soaring.

#2909. If you stayed up late enough, you saw why the Eagles are so high on Eli Stowers

If you stayed up late enough, you saw why the Eagles are so high on Eli Stowers originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Eli Stowers said two weeks ago that whenever he did finally get the opportunity to make plays, he was confident he'd be ready.

Then he went out Sunday night and showed he was.

It was largely garbage time and the game was all but over, but Stowers – the Eagles' rookie 2nd-round pick – caught three passes for 53 yards in the second half of the Eagles' sixth straight loss. They were the first three receptions of his career in the second game of his career.

Stowers caught a seven-yarder from Tanner McKee for a first down in the third quarter and then caught passes on the final two snaps of the game, a 12-yarder that gave the Eagles a 4th-and-7 on the Washington 42-yard-line with two seconds left and then a 34-yarder down to the 8-yard-line as time expired.

"It's always good to get the first one out of the way and to be able to feel that in a game," Stowers said at his locker after practice Wednesday. "Anytime you go out there and you get a first catch, that's a blessing to be able to even say that, 'I got my first catch in the NFL game,' so I think it did help the confidence.

"My body's been feeling better and been able to move a little better so it felt good."

After a middling training camp, Stowers spent the first six weeks of the season on Injured Reserve, and it was fair to wonder what exactly the Eagles had in this converted quarterback from Vanderbilt. He made his NFL debut in the loss to the Cowboys and had one target but no catches.

But anyone who watched till the end of the game Sunday night got to see the soft hands, athleticism and speed the Eagles like enough about Stowers to make him the 54th player and second tight end taken in this year's draft.

He actually almost scored on that final snap of the game before finally getting tackled at the 8-yard-line after the longest pass play of McKee's career.

"I caught the ball and it was a lot of space," he said. "And so anytime you catch the ball, you've got to go try to score. That's the mindset that any ball carrier should have. So that was my mindset. I was hoping I could drag them in there. They ended up getting me down, but it's all right."

What's really important is that Stowers is healthy after a lost summer. The Eagles didn't rush him back, and it paid off.

"I had hurt my hamstring and then I hurt my quad and then it was a span of like seven weeks where I just had to get my body back healthy from camp," he said. "I don't think it was really a matter of me being behind physically. It was just I had to get back healthy, get on the field and perform well."

Stowers became the Eagles' first 2nd-round pick to not play the first six weeks of a season since guard Bobbie Williams in 2001. Williams wound up playing 12 years in the NFL and starting 136 games, mainly for the Bengals.

"Sometimes injuries like that, you can't do anything about it," he said. "Take care of your body the best way you can. Get good sleep, eat right, drink, do all the stuff. But sometimes you just can't do anything about it. So if you sit there and you stay on it, kind of sulk about it, nothing's going to get any better. So I just attacked it every single day of getting it back right and be prepared for when I get back."

It's no coincidence that the Eagles put Stowers' locker next to Zach Ertz, whose 828 career catches are 5th-most in NFL history by a tight end.

Ertz is currently on the practice squad and out of elevations, but that doesn't mean he can't be a sounding board every day for the 23-year-old Stowers.

"He's been awesome," Stowers said. "Everybody in that room has been awesome. We got all vets in there and I really appreciate how much they've kind of poured into me.

"One of the first things Zach told me when he got here, he was like, 'Yo, if you have anything, any questions, anything,' let me know. Don't be scared to ask. Sometimes he'll even, when I'm sitting here being quiet, he'll encourage me to ask him a question. Like, 'Have you got something?'

"So, I appreciate that, man. He's helping me get better."
