#2842. What we learned from the Spurs win over the Mavericks

At some point between the end of the '21 season and the start of the '22 season, I made a pact with myself: no more first quarters.

You have to understand, these were dark times to be a Spurs writer.

And yes, I know, as a fan myself, that those years were no more of picnic for the average fan than they were for me, but still. There's just something about watching a beating and then having to write about it that feels vaguely abusive, especially when some of your favorite people are still involved.

Having to watch Gregg Popovich lose like that…I felt like a journalist covering Muhammad Ali's last fights.

It didn't matter how valiant he was, how much he tried to make something out of nothing — it just seemed kind of dirty to watch at all. We all knew what he was doing, of course.

But watching a worthy sacrifice doesn't make it any easier. I still get misty-eyed when the Iron Giant tells Hogarth Hughes not to follow him (as if he could) before rocketing from the ground into space, and meeting the falling arc of a nuclear warhead.

There's a reason I don't watch the movie all the time. It would be fairly masochistic of me to do so.

But there are 82 games in a season, and someone has to cover them.

As a writing staff, you take some solace in the knowledge that you're all in this together. You're all having to write about it. You're all having to dig for silver linings. And that (for this site) you're all doing it out of some (possibly misplaced) sense of love for the team, and the sport, and the readers who are just about as miserable as you are.

But I just could not watch first quarters anymore. Not in real time.

It's one thing to know that the Titanic is sinking; to be thrown into the middle of the chaos and do your best to make sense of it. It's another thing altogether to watch the ship headed towards the ice-burg in the darkness. To hear the godawful scraping and know what it means. To have been watching the journey for years, and completely run out of ways to spin it.

It had to have been a very conscious decision on James Cameron's part to make that movie a romance. How else could you get people to watch it? The wreck just cannot be the main course.

I couldn't convince myself to watch the game at all if I started with the first quarter. The Spurs would either start taking their beating from the onset, or worse, soar to an early lead that we all knew wouldn't last.

So I decided that I would start watching during the second quarter, finish the game, and then watch the beginning.

It was so much easier to bear when you already knew how it would end. Especially when the Spurs did miraculously pull off the win.

You could go back and break down how the positive momentum started, with the empty but understandable hope that there might be something algorithmic in those 12 minutes that could be reverse-engineered and employed more regularly.

And even the sting of defeat didn't smart as much when you were able to initially avoid the fool's gold of productive opening minutes.

Hell, sometimes it was even a little funny when you encountered the Spurs finding a way to lose the opening frame that you'd never even considered.

(Even though the Spurs won the game, I don't think I'll ever forget Jakob Poeltl picking up 2 fouls in the first 96 seconds of the Lakers game in 2022 that tied Pop with Don Nelson.)

Lately, though, I've been forced to reconsider my first-quarter policy, and the reason is pretty obvious: Victor Wembanyama is just too fun to be put on the back-burner in any capacity.

It was a sensible policy when the losses were piling up. Sometimes it still is.

But frustration with a contender-caliber team starting slowly is simply not enough to deprive myself of the immediacy of the joy provided by the most insane fusion of Hakeem Olajuwon, Kareem Abdul-Jabbar, and Wilt Chamberlain imaginable.

Wemby just had the first *official* 50 point, 20 rebound, 8 block game in NBA history (and the first 50/20/10 in the non-assist category if you combine his steals and blocks), in his 8th game of his 4th season, and he's the same age that Wilt was as a rookie!

Chamberlain didn't even peak until he was 25!

That's the territory we're in. That's the conversation.

And that's in spite of existing in the era of injury and rest management. In spite of the fact that Wemby's doing it on a team that's good enough to have already made the Finals.

In spite of playing in an era where blocks are harder to come by, and strategic scouting and game-plans are another level, and players are (pretty successfully) bombing away from long range. In spite of an out-of-whack jumper that's converting at about 28% outside of 10 feet.

Having chugged a 16oz energy drink during my late dinner before the game in an attempt to battle the somnolent rigidities of middle-age, I found myself doing the same 'fighting the urge to pee' dance that my child still breaks out every now and then, as I tried to hold out til the end of the half.

Smelling weakness through the television screen like a shark loose in a blood bank, Wemby proceeded to block three shots in roughly 13 seconds, as if to say: are you sure that you want to miss this?

I can't remember the last time I almost wet myself over basketball, or a more satisfying pee at the half.

There's plenty of negative things I could say about the game. I don't know that I've ever seen this team shoot that badly as a whole. Even Julian Champagnie and Devin Vassell couldn't buy a shot.

There's no reason it should have been that close with such a performance out of Wembanyama.

The bench's defense looked like it had been coordinated by Wile E. Coyote and the Acme Corporation, and they put up a total of ten points in their relief of the starters. Carter Bryant and Tarris Reed somehow got no minutes on a night in which it couldn't have been more clear that a shakeup was needed.

Even De'Aaron Fox's previously next-level show of efficiency took the wrong turn at Albuquerque.

This was a bad game, if we're being honest. A real stinker. And yet I can't find the animosity within me to make this the all-around feel-bad roast that it should be, because, as I've said so many times over the last year, it just did not matter.

This should have been one of those games where I had to go back and break down the first quarter in a state of rage.

It should have been. But I just don't have it in me to despair the undespairing.

Through it all, Wemby was completely unshaken. Understandably, so were his teammates. In their shoes, I think I'd feel the same way.

I don't love that they frittered away lead after lead. I don't like it as a trend, and I don't like it as reminder.

But I think it's easy to forget that we don't live in that place were I can't stand to watch the first quarter anymore, and that by resigning myself to that policy of the past, I'd be getting in the way of my own happiness.

You can't protect yourself from pain without also protecting yourself from rapture. I think sometimes, in both life and basketball, we have a way of forgetting that.

I can tell you everything they did wrong. I can rant and rage with the best of them, and sound my barbaric yawp over the roofs of the world.

I can tell you that they were saved anyway, undeservingly.

Give me a better story than that, I dare you.

I don't have a lot of nice things to say about this game, but man has Wemby really taken to the responsibility of attacking the rim this season. Right now he and Giannis are the only players in the NBA averaging 7+ Rim FGs and 8+ Paint FGs made per game. And Wemby's actually doing it more ways than Giannis is, as the only player in the league averaging 5+ points as the P&R Roll Man, 4+ Points off of Cuts, and 4+ points In Transition, per game. If his jumper starts heating up, we're going to see even crazier numbers than we did last night. Which is insane when you consider that he's 4th in the league in scoring with a jumper finds the bottom of the bucket about 1/4th of time right now.

It's been a bit of an uncomfortable fit for Stephon Castle when all three guards are on the court at the same time, and he seems to have been asked to serve as more of an off-ball shooter because of that, but when the need arises, he always seems to find an extra gear. I know many are hoping that the addition of Tobias Harris forces some rotation adjustments, but I will say, Castle is getting the open shots, and he's not shrinking from the challenge. He's shooting 32% from three now, but if he starts hitting those, that lineup might really morph into something scary, because everything will open up on the inside, and Wemby's already mutilating the rim as it is (and Harper's been slouch, either). And as much as I think Harris will be a boon to the team, I'd secretly prefer to see that happen instead.

Though Fox had probably his most inefficient night of the season on the offensive end, he's still the only player in the NBA creating 15+ Assist Points Per Game, with a 2-1 Assist-to-Turnover Ratio, while also sporting a 65+ True Shooting Percentage, which is crazy when you see all the names that miss the mark. But, just as importantly, he's been outstanding on the defensive end, and it showed in the clutch last night when he kept the still-lethal (and long-time Spurs killer) Kyrie Irving off-balance just enough to that his attempt at a game-winning bucket. To have lost a game with Victor playing like that would have felt catastrophic, so he'd be next in line for the game ball, in my mind.

Playing You Out – The Theme Song of the Evening:

#2841. Derrick Henry's production has plummeted without Lamar Jackson this season

Without quarterback Lamar Jackson, the Ravens want to be able to rely on running back Derrick Henry. That plan isn't working.

In Thursday night's 33-10 loss to the Jaguars, Henry managed just 57 yards on 17 carries. That continues what has been happening all season, with Henry struggling to get anything going without Jackson on the field.

It was a bad omen in Week 4 against the Titans, when Jackson left at halftime with an ankle injury and Henry was ineffective in the second half. In the first half of that game, with Jackson on the field, Henry had seven carries for 38 yards, an average of 5.4 yards per carry, and a touchdown. In the second half with Tyler Huntley, Henry had 16 carries for 35 yards, an average of 2.2 yards per carry, and no touchdowns.

Jackson has played one snap since that Titans game, and the story has been the same, with Henry not able to do much of anything.

Overall this season, in three and a half games playing with Jackson, Henry has 73 carries for 339 yards, an average of 4.6 yards per carry and 97 yards per game, and seven touchdowns. In five and a half games without Jackson, Henry has 102 carries for 350 yards, an average of 3.4 yards per carry and 64 yards per game, and one touchdown.

At 4-5 and in last place in the AFC North, the Ravens, who entered the year with Super Bowl aspirations, are on the verge of their season collapsing. They need to turn things around, and they need Henry to turn things around.

#2840. Eddie Hearn denies signing Arman Tsarukyan to deal: ‘Just some breakfast’

Arman Tsarukyan and Eddie Hearn are not getting into business with each other.

That's despite a picture that Tsarukyan posted last week showing what appeared to be a meeting between the longtime lightweight No. 1 contender and the Matchroom Boxing head. But take your tinfoil hats off, people. According to Eddie Hearn, this was nothing but a chance encounter at a hotel that turned into the opportunity for brunch.

"Everyone went crazy for this," Hearn wrote on Instagram. "We were just staying in the same hotel randomly and arranged to have some breakfast together."

Hearn has taken it upon himself to help UFC fighters in his stable get more media attention and lucrative sponsorships. For a promotion company, the UFC doesn't actually promote its fighters very much, so Hearn has stepped in to fill that role. And it doesn't hurt that the optics aren't great for UFC and White, whom Hearn has accused of being checked out.

When Hearn and Garry linked up, they kept the deal a secret until the UFC announced Garry vs. Makhachev. Hearn wasn't so secretive with his Tsarukyan breakfast, which he posted on his socials for all to see. With Tsarukyan waiting to see if he'll finally get another shot at the lightweight title, could this whole thing affect his chances? Even now that we're learning it was an innocent breakfast?

#2839. What are the best NFL trade deadline deals ever? These 9 top the list

As the 2026 NFL trade deadline approaches, many top contenders hope to make a midseason acquisition that pushes them toward a Super Bowl title. Historically, only a few trades have turned out as impactful as a team hoped. Fewer still have aged particularly well in the years since the moves were made.

Given the brevity of the season, the time necessary to get players acclimated to a new team's schemes, and a salary cap to deal with, the NFL trade deadline is not quite as active as that of other sports. Only in the last decade or so has it become really significant, with more teams hitting the gas on mid-season deals since 2016.

As such, most of the best trade deadline deals in history have come since the turn of the century. These nine moves in particular stand out for the impact each player made with the team that acquired them:

NFL TRADE DEADLINE: Will the Jets spring another November surprise?

Week 3: Denver Broncos cheerleaders perform during the "Sunday Night Football" game against the Los Angeles Rams at Empower Field at Mile High.

Week 3: The Maracanã Stadium gets set to host the International Series game between the Baltimore Ravens and Dallas Cowboys in Rio de Janeiro, Brazil.

Week 3: Corgi dog competitions take place during halftime of the game between the Detroit Lions and New York Jets at Ford Field.

Week 2: Singer Taylor Swift watches the game between the Indianapolis Colts and Kansas City Chiefs at Arrowhead Stadium.

Week 2: The Canadian and U.S. anthems are sung during a pregame ceremony before the "Thursday Night Football" game between Detroit Lions and Buffalo Bills at new Highmark Stadium.

Week 1: Singer Taylor Swift and actor Tom Cruise attend the "Monday Night Football" game between the Kansas City Chiefs and Denver Broncos at Arrowhead Stadium.

Week 2: The Canadian and U.S. anthems are sung during a pregame ceremony before the "Thursday Night Football" game between Detroit Lions and Buffalo Bills at new Highmark Stadium.

Week 1: Singer Taylor Swift and actor Tom Cruise attend the "Monday Night Football" game between the Kansas City Chiefs and Denver Broncos at Arrowhead Stadium.

Week 1: Fans arrive ahead of the NFL game between the Los Angeles Rams and San Francisco 49ers at the Melbourne Cricket Ground (in Melbourne, Australia. The 49ers won the first NFL game played in Australia, 27-7.

Week 1: Kim Thayil of Soundgarden and Mike McCready of Pearl Jam perform during halftime of the 2026 NFL Kickoff Game between the Seattle Seahawks and New England Patriots at Lumen Field.

When Kyle Shanahan took over as the San Francisco 49ers' head coach before the 2017 season, he came with plans to overhaul the team's offensive scheme. Then-starter Colin Kaepernick was not an apparent fit for his offense, so Shanahan allowed Kaepernick to walk, hoping to bring in a new starting quarterback to slot into his offense.

The 49ers head coach found his man in a trade deadline deal to acquire Jimmy Garoppolo from the New England Patriots. It cost the 49ers their 2018 second-round pick, but the trade ended up being the spark the team needed that year.

Garoppolo made his debut for his new team in Week 12 following a lower-body injury to starter C.J. Beathard, and he became the team's starter the following week. Garoppolo led the 49ers to five straight wins to close the season after starting the year 1-10.

That February, San Francisco extended Garoppolo on a five-year deal.

"Jimmy G" had his ups and downs with the Niners, but the team made the playoffs in three of his six seasons – including its first Super Bowl appearance since 2012 in 2019. He also has the fourth-best win percentage (.691) by a San Francisco quarterback in franchise history, behind Steve Young, Joe Montana and current starter Brock Purdy.

The 2018 first-round pick led the Chicago Bears in tackles in the four full years he played for the team. In 2020 and 2021, he earned second-team All-Pro nods after finishing in the top six in combined tackles each year.

The writing was on the wall for Smith's time in Chicago when the team couldn't reach an agreement with the linebacker ahead of his fifth season. He requested a trade in August 2022 and told the media he didn't feel properly valued by the new front office regime that took over that January.

After eight games, the Baltimore Ravens traded 2023 second- and fifth-round picks to the Bears for Smith. He finished the year with a career-high 169 tackles across two teams, 4.5 sacks, 11 tackles for a loss, and six passes defended. That production earned him first-team All-Pro honors for the first time in his career.

Smith got a first-team All-Pro nod for a second straight time in 2023 with his 158 tackles – including 21 in a single game – plus 1.5 sacks, five tackles for a loss, eight passes defended, and a forced fumble.

This trade has continued to age even better as Smith re-affirms year after year that he's one of the league's best all-around inside linebackers. He earned his third first-team All-Pro nod in a row in 2024 with 154 tackles, 1.5 sacks, four tackles for a loss, four passes defended, and a forced fumble.

Even though Smith did not earn All-Pro honors again in 2025, he was named a Pro Bowler for the fourth time in his career. Four years later, the trade has still been a big win for the Ravens, who signed him to a five-year, $100 million extension in January 2023.

NFL TRADE DEADLINE: From the Bills to 49ers, 1 trade target for each division leader

The Pittsburgh Steelers' acquisition of Fitzpatrick was similar to the Ravens' trade for Smith. The main difference was that this mid-season trade was less of a trade deadline move; it happened six weeks before the cut-off in 2019.

Fitzpatrick requested a trade one week into the safety's second season with the Miami Dolphins. The 2018 first-round pick felt the coaching staff in Miami was not utilizing his skill set correctly. He made 12 tackles across two games before the Dolphins and Steelers completed their trade.

In September 2019, Pittsburgh sent its 2020 first- and fifth-round picks and a 2021 sixth-round pick in exchange for Fitzpatrick, Miami's 2020 fourth-round pick and 2021 seventh-round pick. Fitzpatrick made his Steelers debut in Week 3 and established himself as one of the league's best safeties by the end of the year.

He was a first-team All-Pro safety in 2019 and again in 2020. Fitzpatrick earned a third such nod in four years with more first-team All-Pro honors in 2022. It was a year in which he tallied six interceptions that tied for the league lead. He has five Pro Bowl appearances in 2019, 2020, 2022, 2023 and 2024.

The Steelers traded Fitzpatrick back to Miami before the 2025 season, but his years in Pittsburgh remain the most decorated of his career.

When Jon Gruden took over as head coach of the Raiders in 2018, it didn't take him long to make some big moves that flipped productive players for draft picks. Just ahead of that 2018 season, the then-Oakland Raiders traded star edge rusher Khalil Mack and two draft picks to the Chicago Bears for a return of four picks - including two first-rounders.

After a 1-5 start to the 2018 season, Gruden denied rumors that he was seeking trade partners for Amari Cooper, who was the Raiders' No. 1 receiver in each of his first three pro seasons. Nevertheless, the Raiders traded the wideout for the Dallas Cowboys' 2019 first-round pick in late October.

When he arrived in Dallas, Cooper didn't take long to re-establish his reliability as a top receiving option. Despite playing just nine games for the Cowboys in 2018, the Alabama product's 725 receiving yards, including a 180-yard performance against Washington and a 217-yard day against Philadelphia and six touchdowns, led the entire team that year.

Cooper's three and a half years with the Cowboys were the most productive of his career. Before Dallas traded him in 2022, he had accumulated 3,893 receiving yards and tallied 27 touchdowns on 292 catches – his best numbers for a single team across the four he's played for.

Although the Cowboys never made a deep playoff run while Cooper was on the team, he was a crucial part of the Dallas offense in all four of his seasons.

Spoiler alert: this is the only trade before 2000 to make this list. Herschel Walker will make the worst trade list; Vikings fans, don't worry.

Dickerson entered his fifth season with the Rams having led the NFL in rushing yards in three of his first four seasons. One of those seasons was his record-setting 2,105-yard 1984, which also included a league-leading 14 touchdowns. Shortly after setting the single-season rushing record – one that still stands – negotiations on an extension broke down.

Three games into the strike-shortened 1987 season, Los Angeles shipped Dickerson to Indianapolis as part of a three-team, 10-player trade. The move was somewhat ironic – four years earlier, Dickerson had made it known before the draft he would refuse to play for the then-Baltimore-based franchise.

In just nine games with the Colts that year, Dickerson tallied 1,011 yards and five touchdowns. He earned his fourth Pro Bowl nod and was named to the All-Pro first team for the fourth time. Indianapolis made the playoffs in 1987, going 9-6 and winning the AFC East one year after going 3-13.

The next year, Dickerson once again led the NFL with 1,659 rushing yards and 2,036 yards from scrimmage. He earned a fifth career Pro Bowl and All-Pro first team nod, but the Colts missed the playoffs.

After a 1989 season that included 1,311 rushing yards and a sixth career Pro Bowl nod (but no playoff berth), Dickerson's final two seasons in Indy were less smooth. The Colts traded him to the Raiders in 1992, ending his tenure.

Jalen Ramsey quickly established himself as a star NFL cornerback. He was the Defensive Rookie of the Year runner-up to Joey Bosa in 2016 and earned his initial first-team All-Pro recognition in his second season in 2017.

If winning cures everything, losing exposes issues. The Jacksonville Jaguars' AFC championship game run in 2017 was followed by a 5-11 season in 2018. Then, Jacksonville began the 2019 season 0-2.

During the Jaguars' Week 2 loss, Ramsey had a verbal altercation with then-head coach Doug Marrone that escalated to the point that both had to be physically restrained. The next week, the cornerback played his final game with Jacksonville.

In mid-October, the Jaguars traded Ramsey to the Los Angeles Rams for two first-round picks and a 2021 fourth-rounder.

The cornerback was a Pro Bowler in his four seasons in Los Angeles and earned two more first-team All-Pro nods in 2020 and 2021. Ramsey helped the Rams win a Super Bowl in the 2021 season, accumulating 13 total tackles and four passes defended throughout the playoff run, including one pass defense in the Super Bowl.

The Broncos scuffled for years after Miller won Super Bowl 50 MVP. Their only winning season after that magical 2015 was in the year after – a 9-7 record with no playoff berth in 2016.

In 2021, Denver started 3-0 before losing four straight. As part of their efforts to escape the multi-year slide, new general manager George Paton sent their former Super Bowl MVP to the Rams for second- and third-round picks.

In eight games with Los Angeles, Miller recorded five sacks, one pass defense, a forced fumble and 12 tackles for a loss. The Rams lost three straight games after the trade deadline, but their five-game win streak that followed had them in position to win the NFC West with a win or a Cardinals loss in Week 18. Arizona obliged.

Miller played in all four of Los Angeles's playoff games and led all defenders in that postseason with four sacks and six tackles for a loss.

The Rams went on to win the Super Bowl over the Bengals in February 2022. Miller had two sacks in the game, getting him to 4.5 Super Bowl sacks in his career. That tied him with Charles Haley for most sacks by a single player in Super Bowl history – a record he and Haley still share.

When healthy, Christian McCaffrey was one of the best offensive players in the NFL with the Carolina Panthers.

In 2019, his third season in the NFL, the former Stanford Cardinal led the league with 403 touches, 2,392 scrimmage yards, and 19 total touchdowns. He earned first-team All-Pro recognition for the first time, his first Pro Bowl nod, and a third-place finish in Offensive Player of the Year voting.

Unfortunately, for the running back and the Panthers, McCaffrey couldn't stay healthy for the two years after. He played just ten games in the 2020 and 2021 seasons, accumulating 212 touches, 1,159 yards from scrimmage, and eight touchdowns.

With Carolina's window of contention closing, the Panthers decided to flip the star tailback for future draft picks halfway through the 2022 season. The 49ers, looking to make a postseason push, gave up their second, third, and fourth-round picks in 2023 and a 2024 fifth-round pick.

In 11 games for San Francisco, McCaffrey led the team with 746 rushing yards and six rushing touchdowns. He was also the fourth-most productive pass-catcher on the 49ers that year, with 52 catches for 464 receiving yards.

The 2023 season was even better. He led the league with 1,459 rushing yards, 2,023 scrimmage yards, and 21 total touchdowns. In 2023, McCaffrey earned his third Pro Bowl nod, second first-team All-Pro honors, an Offensive Player of the Year win, and third-place finish in MVP voting.

The running back's stellar year was a big reason the 49ers made it to Super Bowl 58. He became the first player in Super Bowl history with 75 rushing and 75 receiving yards in the game.

More injuries minimized McCaffrey's impact in 2024, but his 2025 season was a return to form. He finished with 2,126 scrimmage yards on a league-leading 413 touchdowns and earned first-team All-Pro honors for the third time in his career. He also won Comeback Player of the Year, finished second in Offensive Player of the Year voting and was fourth in MVP voting.

Lynch had a strong first two seasons as a pro in 2007 and 2008. He put up back-to-back 1,000-yard seasons with 15 total touchdowns across those two years and a Pro Bowl nod in 2008.

Before the 2009 season, Lynch was arrested on misdemeanor weapons charges that led to a three-game suspension to start the year. That season was also the year that Fred Jackson supplanted Lynch as the Buffalo Bills' starting back en route to his first 1,000-yard season.

Lynch played just four more games for the Bills in the 2010 season before the Seattle Seahawks traded a 2011 fourth-round pick and a 2012 conditional fifth-round pick for the running back. The rest is history.

Seattle's new acquisition established an early legacy in the Seahawks franchise, with a 67-yard touchdown run featuring nine broken tackles against the New Orleans Saints - dubbed the "Beast Quake" - in the 2010 playoffs.

#2838. Lakers assign Bronny James, Cameron Carr to G League team

Third-year guard Bronny James and rookie guard Cameron Carr have been assigned to G League Coachella Valley, the Lakers announced Thursday.

James, who is the third year of a four-year contract worth $7.9 million ($4.4 million guaranteed), played only 12 minutes this season across five games, scoring four points on two-of-three shooting while missing his only three-pointer.

Carr, who was the 24th overall draft pick this summer, has played only four minutes in three games, missing the only shot he took.

James has played in 74 games during his young career, including 42 last season when he averaged 2.9 points and 1.2 assists in 8.9 minutes.

The Lakers moved their G League affiliate to Coachella Valley, where they will open the season Friday at Acrisure Arena in Palm Desert against Salt Lake City.

#2837. Colchester fans are with us, says boss Cowley

Colchester United manager Danny Cowley says he believes "the supporters are right with us" despite their long winless run.

The U's have not won any of their last 12 matches in all competitions since a 1-0 victory over Rochdale on 29 August.

It is a run of form that has seen Colchester slip to 23rd in League Two ahead of Saturday's FA Cup first round meeting with Worcester City who play in the seventh-tier Southern Football League Premier Division Central

They lost 3-1 to Walsall last Saturday but Cowley told BBC Essex: "We conceded two late goals, but they are still singing our names, they're right behind us."

He said: "Once we got over the initial disappointment of losing, I felt really inspired by the way that they [the fans] stuck with them.

"Maybe it gave us all a lesson, if things haven't gone your way, it's easy to feed your misery... the supporters maybe made me look at myself and all week we've been really positive."

Cowley's side have not reached the second round of the FA Cup since 2021, and were knocked out by MK Dons in round one last season.

"For us the challenge is to still be in the hat come 17:00 on Saturday, still with 99 points to play for in the league," Cowley added.

"If we can achieve that, then it will be a pretty good weekend for us."

#2836. Lakers’ Luka Doncic leads NBA in defensive stat

Entering Wednesday's game against the Kings, Luka Doncic was leading the NBA in defensive two-point field-goal percentage as the primary defender (21.9%) with a minimum 30 attempts defended.

It's an eye-popping stat for Doncic, who is known for his wizardry on offense but who has been a lighting rod for criticism on defense over his eight-season career.

Los Angeles Lakers guard Luka Doncic, right, looks on while guarded by Sacramento Kings forward Leaky Black during an NBA basketball game Wednesday, Nov. 4, 2026. AP Photo/Scott Marshall

Doncic is having an early MVP campaign, averaging a league-leading 38.5 points, 8.1 rebounds and eight assists for the Lakers (7-2).

He has been making notable contributions on defense, too.

Doncic recently had five steals and three drawn charges in a 131-125 win over the Raptors on Nov. 1, becoming the first player to record those defensive marks in the same game since player tracking began in 2013-14, per ESPN Research.

Los Angeles Lakers guard Luka Doncic reacts during the first half of an NBA basketball game against the Sacramento Kings in Sacramento, Calif., Wednesday, Nov. 4, 2026. AP Photo/Scott Marshall

Luka Doncic (77) of the Los Angeles Lakers shoots against the Sacramento Kings on Wednesday, Nov. 4, 2026, at Golden 1 Center in Sacramento, Calif. NBAE via Getty Images

"Obviously, I'm not the best defender in the NBA, but I think this year I really took a step forward," Doncic said Nov. 1. "Obviously people are going to talk what they want to talk [about] me. But I know, and my team knows, that I'm better this year."

Doncic has scored 40 or more points in five of his last six games, including leading the Lakers to a 116-103 win over the Kings on Wednesday.

Doncic was recently asked in a tongue-and-cheek manner if he'd rather win the MVP award or Defensive Player of the Year.

#2835. Five Plays: Stanford Third Quarter

It took until the second half, but Louisville started to get more aggressive with their linebackers. On this play, Stanquan Clark times up the snap before shooting the gap to make a play in the backfield. He's just unlucky because the play goes to the other side, which leaves him grasping for air.

Stanford uses motion again on this play with a full shift of their tight ends across the field. Louisville tracks the motion, but you will notice that no one picks up the running back coming out of the backfield. So, on this play, Stanford takes advantage of two different issues UofL had in coverage earlier in the game. They don't identify their responsibility after the motion, and they don't account for the running back out of the backfield on a short-yardage pass.

This is a nice play design and call by Stanford, but it would be nice to be able to say that the play just beat Louisville as opposed to the defense not even giving itself a chance.

Lincoln Kienholz had his weekly instalment of "Man, it's nice to have a quarterback who can do this" in the second quarter with his arm, but he does it with his legs on this play. I especially love the spin move that wasn't really needed.

I wrote about Louisville needing to attack the edges of Stanford's defense in the What To Watch For post last week, so I was really happy to see them try to get outside a lot in this game. The first drive of the game was edge runs and swing passes, and they came back to it in the third quarter for this nice touchdown.

As good as Isaac Brown is in space, he should buy dinner for Jaleel Skinner and TJ McWilliams for their blocking on this play. McWilliams is the outside receiver to the top of the screen. He gets up to the outside linebacker, who is flowing towards him before passing him off to Skinner, who gets outside after going in motion. McWilliams then moves up to the cornerback to create a tunnel for Isaac to run through. This is teach tape.

I'm a bit of a broken record on this, but I don't really understand why they can run this defense as often as they run Cover 3. Situations will dictate a lot of things, but the only real difference is that they are a little more spread out. But if this were first down, you would not be blitzing Kaleb Perry, who loops around on a stunt on this play. He would be right in the middle of the field to play the run. Ben Perry would also not be in press coverage in the slot. He would be backed off a bit and could react to a run play.

If they were playing well in zone coverage, this wouldn't be a problem, but at this point in the season I can't understand why they haven't adjusted.

This was one of the dumbest calls I've seen in all the years I've been doing these film reviews. Stanford had just scored on the previous drive, and they had already shown that they could get explosive passing plays and chunk runs against this defense. A screen pass or a draw play to try to get a cheap first down makes much more sense here. Instead, they give the Cards an opportunity to get a true momentum-shifting play for a defense that had been gashed all day.

It was really great to see everyone on defense make the right play here. Perry closes on the initial receiver, while TJ Capers immediately reacts to the pitch and makes a big hit on the receiver even though he didn't make the catch. Tayon Holloway looks like he's running a scoop-and-score drill, too. It looks easy, but picking up a fumble on the run like this gets bungled a lot. He does well to bend his knees and drop his butt, which is where a lot of guys get it wrong. They keep running while trying to grab the ball in stride. Secure the ball, then take off.

Jabari Mack is good for one or two good plays a game, and this was a really good one. He is in man coverage on this play, but because he opens his hips at the beginning of the play, he is able to keep up with his man while also having an eye in the backfield. When Davis Warren commits to throwing the wheel route here, Mack breaks off of his man to get over the top and pick off the pass.

This was also a really nice catch for a defensive back. He high-points the ball over the receiver, who is in position to catch the ball. Mack snatches it away before it hits the receiver's hands.

Louisville attacks the edge again, and Isaac Brown just does what he does. There are maybe five guys in the country who could score on this play, and it's always nice to have one of those few.

#2834. NFL 2026 Fantasy Football Week 9: Studs, Duds And Could-Be With Stats

It is Week 9 of the NFL 2026 fantasy football season, and yes, chaos still reigns. So as we wander through it, it is time to assess, and remember the goal right now is to get into the fantasy football playoffs. That's the goal. That's the target. Embrace the chaos.

This week, two teams are on bye: the Pittsburgh Steelers and the Tennessee Titans. There is one International game: the Cincinnati Bengals versus the Atlanta Falcons. This game is filled with studs, including Joe Burrow, Ja'Marr Chase, Tee Higgins, Bijan Robinson, and Drake London, so get them out of your flex and into your lineups before the early start. There will be no less than five "back-up" quarterbacks starting this week, including Drew Lock and Gardner Minshew battling one another. Embrace the chaos.

And for your weekly reminder, studs are an accumulation of fantasy points from weeks 1-8 per fantasypros.com.

This is an International game, played in Madrid.

TAMPA, FLORIDA - DECEMBER 11: Kyle Pitts #8 of the Atlanta Falcons celebrates after scoring a touchdown during the fourth quarter(Photo by Kevin Sabitus/Getty Images)

Penix had a historically bad outing last week. He attempted 24 passes, completed eight for a 33.3% completion percentage and 125 passing yards. He also threw four interceptions. Now, for a caveat: this game was played in a torrential downpour, and there is the argument that when you have Bijan Robinson on your side, attempting any passes in a downpour is ill-advised. (What is it with Kevin Stefanski's insistence on passing when just…giving…it…to…Bijan…is optimal?)

Anyway, this isn't just about last week's fiasco; Penix is 32nd in productivity rate on pressured throws (3.8%) with a 26.1% completion percentage when pressured. The Bengals defense is 12th in pressure rate (32.5%) and has collected the third most sacks (17).

Penix, whose 2.35-second time to throw will have to find a quick out for his passes. This is where Pitts comes in. The Bengals' defense is giving up the second-most receiving yards to the tight end position (621) and the fourth-most receptions (49).

But the super-duper flashing red sign is that Pitts' fantasy points so far have been touchdown-dependent. And the Bengals' defense has a bend-don't-break philosophy against tight ends; they have not allowed a touchdown to the position this season.

ARLINGTON, TX - NOVEMBER 23: Quinnen Williams #92 of the Dallas Cowboys runs out of the tunnel prior to an NFL football game (Photo by Cooper Neill/Getty Images)

This is a game of unpredictability times a gazillion. On paper, the Cowboys should roll past the fire-sale Colts. But we have heard that narrative before, like last week when the Cowboys were a touchdown favorite against the Arizona Cardinals and ended up losing (the Gardner Minshew effect notwithstanding).

The Cardinals put up points against the Cowboys (as most teams have, as the Cowboys rank 27th in points allowed per game, 26.5).

The issue here is that the Colts have been unable to generate points. Last week they scored seven points; in Week 7 they scored 10, and in Week 6, in their loss against the Titans, they scored 20.

Jones has also thrown nine interceptions, with two last week, combined with 10 quarterback hits by the Jacksonville Jaguars before being replaced by Anthony Richardson. The Cowboys' defense is the gift that keeps on giving; the question is what do the Colts have left to give.

First and foremost, all the best healing energy is being sent to Jack Campbell.

FOXBOROUGH, MASSACHUSETTS - JANUARY 04: Greg Dulcich #85 of the Miami Dolphins in action during the game against the New England Patriots(Photo by Sarah Stier/Getty Images)

The Lions are giving up the most fantasy points (19.2), most touchdowns (11), and the most receptions (61) to the tight end position. Dulcich has a 12.5% first-read target share, runs 19.7 routes per game, and is on the field for a 66% snap share.

The Dolphins have managed to stay competitive in their last three games (v. the Bengals, the New York Jets, and the New England Patriots); this is a bet that they at least push the Lions in this one.

These Niners are coming off their bye week with their new acquisition, DeForest Buckner.

SANTA CLARA, CALIFORNIA - DECEMBER 21: Defensive tackle DeForest Buckner #99 of the San Francisco 49ers(Photo by Ezra Shaw/Getty Images)

Jeanty is a hard call this week. On the one hand, as a runner, he has been a disappointment, averaging 3.1 yards per carry. His carry volume has been a roller coaster, going from a Week 1 high of 23 carries to last week's low of two. He now gets the Raiders defense, which is fifth in rushing yards allowed per game (89) and second-best in yards per carry allowed (3.5).

But then there is his floor, which is elevated by his utilization in the passing game. Jeanty is number third among running backs with a 17.3% targets share is second in routes run averaging 21.4 per game and first in targets per game averaging 6.1.

Tucker is third on the team in snap distribution (67%) and third on the team in target share with 17.2%.

The Niners being a pass-funnel team gives Jeanty a solid floor despite his subpar rushing stats (that is, if he doesn't get sat), and Tucker is the safe out for Kirk Cousins when and if Fred Warner and the defense can bottle up Brock Bowers.

EAST RUTHERFORD, NEW JERSEY - NOVEMBER 01: Adonai Mitchell #15 of the New York Jets catches the ball against Treydan Stukes #31 of the Las Vegas Raiders (Photo by Al Bello/Getty Images)

Smith is having a good year. He has 12 passing touchdowns with only three interceptions. His 7.9 yards per attempt is seventh in the league, and his true completion percentage of 76% is third in the league.

His assortment of weapons is both good for Smith and not so great for fantasy managers. New York has a lot of mouths to feed, and the question is which ones will be fed on any given Sunday.

You can chase points and play Mitchell, or remember that Omar Cooper Jr. had double-digit fantasy points in Week 7 and zero targets in Week 8; or keep in mind that force-feeding Garrett Wilson to keep him happy is also a strategy. Then there are the two tight ends, Mason Taylor and Kenyon Sadiq.

Add to all that the Chiefs' defense has given up only two touchdowns to wide receivers and the fewest fantasy points to the wide receiver position (14.7). Also, the Chiefs are allowing the fewest fantasy points to the quarterback position (13.1), but Bo Nix just threw for 222 yards and two touchdowns on them last week.

The Rams have only allowed two rushing touchdowns by the running back position this season. And now their secondary is going to be without Jaylen Watson, with Trent McDuffie injured, making them a pass funnel (she asks). That makes a dual-threat quarterback an intriguing option.

The Commanders' run game is seventh, averaging 4.6 yards per carry. And the majority of that is because of Daniels. Among all quarterbacks, Daniels is first in yards per carry (8.74), first in scrambles per game (4.20), and third in carry share (19.6%). On his team, he is three yards behind the leading rusher Jacory Crosky-Merritt (239-236), and Daniels is the only Commander to have a 100-yard rushing game this season.

EAST RUTHERFORD, NEW JERSEY - DECEMBER 28: Rhamondre Stevenson #38 of the New England Patriots carries the ball against Brandon Stephens #21 of the New York Jets(Photo by Pamela Smith/Getty Images)

Stevenson has sole control of the Patriots backfield. Last week he had 19 carries for 82 yards. The Packers' defense is 15th in rushing yards allowed per game (110.9), 13th in rushing yards allowed per attempt (3.9), and they have given up the third most rushing touchdowns (10) while allowing the ninth most fantasy points per game to the running back position (20.7).

The Packers' offensive rushing woes still exist. Jacobs averaged 1.9 rushing yards per attempt last week on 21 carries.

This is the Monday Night Football contest. The Vikings will have Carson Wentz under center.

The Bills have surrendered 15 passing touchdowns. Prior to their bye week, in Week 6, Kirk Cousins torched them for 313 passing yards and two touchdowns; prior to that, Matthew Stafford threw for 383 yards and three touchdowns against them.

CLEVELAND, OHIO - NOVEMBER 16: Kyle Hamilton #14 of the Baltimore Ravens sacks Shedeur Sanders #12 of the Cleveland Browns(Photo by Nick Cammett/Getty Images)

Shedeur Sanders is the starting quarterback for the Browns this week.

The Saints are allowing a league-leading 25.2 fantasy points per game to the running back position. They have also given up the most touchdowns (11) to running backs.

Judkins has virtually no competition for carries in the Browns' backfield. Last week he had 11 carries versus Dylan Sampson's one. The week prior, he had 17 carries to Sampson's 6.

For the season, Judkins is operating on a 74.3% opportunity share and averages 14.4 carries per game. With a new quarterback, Monken may choose to lean on the run game.

KANSAS CITY, MISSOURI - SEPTEMBER 14: Bo Nix #10 of the Denver Broncos loses the ball against Felix Anudike-Uzomah #91 of the Kansas City Chiefs(Photo by David Eulitt/Getty Images)

The Panthers are coming off their bye week and trying to end a two-game slide. And although they were second in passing attempts per game (39.1) and 26th in rushing attempts per game (24.5) going into the bye, Hubbard is the answer to what ails the Panthers.

Hubbard is averaging 4.5 yards per carry, is 11th in evaded tackles (4.1 per game), 10th in breakaway runs (5% rate), and has a 76.7% opportunity share. He has rushed for over 100 yards in four of their seven games.

The Broncos' defense currently leads the NFL with 70 missed tackles and a 12.9% missed tackle rate. However, with the exception of the Kenneth Walker explosion in Week 1 (173 rushing yards), no running back has rushed for more than 88 yards against them (including the rematch with Walker, who finished with 69 yards on 21 carries the second time around). But their last three opponents include: the Arizona Cardinals without Jeremyah Love, the Seahawks without Jadarian Price or Zach Charbonnet, and the Los Angeles Chargers.

And while the group is not elite, the Broncos are still allowing the 11th most fantasy points to the position, 20.1.

This is less about Nix and more about the new defensive coordinator bump from Todd Wash. Well, it's a little about Nix too. In their eight games, he has thrown nine touchdown passes and three interceptions. Per playerprofiler.com, he is sixth in danger plays (1.9% rate) and ninth in interceptable passes (1.0% rate). He is averaging 5.9 yards per attempt (31st), and his 66.8% completion percentage is 27th among qualifying quarterbacks.

Before the bye, the Panthers were only giving up 16.9 fantasy points per game to the quarterback position (they were, however, giving up the second most to the running back position…if we could only trust Sean Payton to feature Jonah Coleman).

The Chargers are coming off their bye week, 0-7. Herbert has thrown five touchdowns and 10 interceptions. Their offense is 28th ranked in yards per game (288.3) and last in points per game (14.1).

It is a legitimate question as to whether Mike McDaniel has broken Herbert. The Texans' defense should be using this as their "get right" game.

Joe Milton will be the Eagles' starting quarterback for this game.

MIAMI GARDENS, FL - AUGUST 22: New York Giants running back Tyrone Tracy Jr. (29) rushes with the ball during the preseason (Photo by Peter Joneleit/Icon Sportswire via Getty Images)

Maybe this is more about the Eagles' defense than the small sample size McCarthy has with the Giants. The Eagles' passing defense is 24th, allowing 235.4 passing yards per game. Opposing quarterbacks have an average passer rating of 91.4 against them (15th); they give up explosive plays 13.6% (20th ranked), and they are 29th in EPA per play.

Gardner Minshew will be starting for the Cardinals. Drew Lock will be starting for the Seahawks.

The Seahawks defense is reeling from the Caleb Williams experience last Monday Night. Minshew is coming off an impressive performance in relief against the Dallas Cowboys' defense last week (188 passing yards and three touchdowns). But this is the Seahawks defense that is first in total yards allowed per game (233.9), first in passing yards allowed per game (158), and third in opponents scoring (17.9). And even with Williams' production against them last week, he only scored 14 fantasy points.

Lock started against the Cardinals in Week 2. He finished with 19 completions on 26 attempts for 235 yards and three touchdowns.

INDIANAPOLIS, INDIANA - SEPTEMBER 14: Jonathan Taylor #28 of the Indianapolis Colts(Photo by Michael Hickey/Getty Images)

With five backup quarterbacks starting this week, one new defensive coordinator, teams hedging their bets on the trade deadline (November 10), and NFL teams looking towards the playoffs (even when they are, say, 3-5 or 4-4, or 2-4-1) when their records may indicate no chance, chaos is the calling card for this week … er, season. Don't fight it. Play the stats, but trust your instincts.

#2832. Game Preview #10: Wolves at Pelicans Game Thread

Fresh off of a tough win against a talented Houston Rockets squad, the Minnesota Timberwolves take on Zion Williamson and the New Orleans Pelicans on Friday night.

Who: Minnesota Timberwolves (6-3) vs New Orleans Pelicans (4-4)

Where: Smoothie King Center — New Orleans, LA

After starting the year 0-4, the Pelicans have ripped off four straight wins to claw back to .500. Sprinkled into that winning streak are some impressive victories over the surging Memphis Grizzlies (handing them their only loss on the season thus far) and the Atlanta Hawks. New Orleans is not a squad that is projected to climb the Western Conference standings or necessarily strike fear in the hearts of opponents, though they are still a team with enough competitive and ascending players to keep opponents honest.

Considering the still shorthanded state of the Wolves, they may have their hands full with a healthy-for-now Pelicans team. The win over the Rockets was thoroughly impressive, though Chris Finch and company will need to remain focused if they are to come out of this game with a victory.

Here are a few things I am looking out for against New Orleans:

You know the guys – the dudes who love to turn up and have career games against the Wolves no matter what state their team is in. Most teams in the NBA have at least one Wolf Killer. The Pelicans have a few.

Perhaps most obvious of these players is Zion Williamson, who averages a whopping 28.5 points per game against Minnesota, nearly five points higher than his career average of 23.8. When healthy, Williamson is one of the premier tickets in the league and can score at will against nearly anyone. Jonathan Kuminga and Rudy Gobert will have their hands full with Zion, who will seek to outmuscle and overpower anyone that Finch decides to throw at him.

Saddiq Bey is another player that ramps up his production against the Timberwolves. A career 14.8 ppg scorer, his number increases to 19.5 for his career against Minnesota. As a member of the Pelicans, Bey's average jumps up to 24 ppg versus the Wolves. The guy just flat out doesn't like us.

As the Pelicans have other surging young players contributing to their recent run of success, this matchup may be more difficult than initially expected. Derik Queen has looked to be a solid NBA big man and Jeremiah Fears has been becoming a more well-rounded player. If Minnesota's defenders can keep the Wolf Killers from transforming into much better versions of themselves, the game is there for the taking.

Bones Hyland is a fun player. He's energetic, has a great smile, and keeps the fanbase engaged with his aesthetically pleasing style of play and affable three-point shot celebration. He's an ideal bench player that could fit in on many teams. We are fortunate to have him as a Timberwolf.

However, with a slew of injuries that continue to hamper Minnesota's preferred personnel, Bones continues to be thrust into heavier minutes than he would normally get. In the four games before Anthony Edwards got injured, Hyland played an average of eight minutes per night. Since then, his minutes have doubled, playing 16 minutes per game in the five matchups since Edwards (and now Terrence Shannon Jr.) went down.

Bones's 19 point, eight assist night against the Los Angeles Clippers last week was a true representation of what he can bring as a player when he's on. It's unrealistic to expect that kind of production and efficiency from him most nights, though he does help the Timberwolves find tempo and form whenever things are going south.

If the Timberwolves are going to have any sustained success while Edwards and TSJ are out, Hyland will need to continue to exert efficiency and production in his limited time on the court. He was electric against the Rockets, scoring 19 points in just 16 minutes of play. For a team that continues to lose players at a high clip, Bones will remain an integral part of the offensive flow moving forward.

There's not much more that can be said about just how putrid Minnesota's free throw shooting has been. Currently the team sits at 63.4%, good for dead last and a massive 7% behind the next closest team, which is the 1-7 Milwaukee Bucks. Minnesota shot a cool 53% (10-19) at the line just last night versus Houston in a 10-point victory to bring that percentage down even lower. So it goes.

It's not like this team has a problem shooting the ball, either. That's what makes this ineptitude so baffling. The Timberwolves shot 53% from the floor and 43% from distance against the Rockets. And yet, while standing at the free throw line, uncontested and still, they can't put the ball in the hoop.

Two players on the Wolves are shooting greater than 80% from the line this season. Everyone else is below them. That is unacceptable.

It would appear that Gobert's inability to consistently make shots from the charity stripe (42.6% on nearly six attempts per game) is becoming contagious to the rest of the team. While it hasn't been the biggest issue the Timberwolves have faced this season, it will become one as the team enters more late-game clutch time situations. We're not far off from a few tight losses spurring a dialogue that laments the team's lack of efficiency from the line. The recent defeat at the hands of the Golden State Warriors comes to mind, poor officiating be damned.

Chris Finch will need to address this problem somehow. Unfortunately, getting reps from the free throw line will not show itself as immediately game-changing. It will take time. Finishing this season anywhere but dead last should be seen as a success at this point. Until then, Minnesota needs to figure out how to capitalize on the opportunities they are given.

#2831. Hurricanes’ Sebastian Aho Passes Franchise Legend In Two Major Categories

The Carolina Hurricanes found themselves getting back on track on Thursday night. For Sebastian Aho, it was a game to remember.

Carolina's 5-3 victory over the New York Islanders was impressive. Aho chipped in with two assists and finished the game with a plus-3.

That may not have seemed like much, but it was a game for the record books. It was the 80th time Aho logged a multi-assist game.

Per Peter Dewar and Walt Ruff of Hurricanes.com, "Aho's 80th career multi-assist game passed Eric Staal for second-most in franchise history behind Ron Francis (163)."

Dewar and Ruff also wrote that, "Thursday's effort also marked Aho's 187th career multi-point game, once again surpassing Eric Staal for second place on the franchise's all-time list behind Ron Francis (297)."

Pretty impressive for a single night, especially with Eric Staal factored into the equation. It further solidifies Aho as one of the Hurricanes' franchise greats. And for a team that is looking to repeat as Stanley Cup Champions, the sense of urgency for Aho to snag multi-goal and multi-point games is even higher.

Aho now has 734 points, 319 goals, and 415 assists in 774 career games. With 23 points and nine goals in 18 games this season, Aho is on an 84-game pace for a career-best 107 points. His previous career-high was 89 points and 36 goals in 78 games in 2023-24.

Eric Staal was part of that first Carolina Hurricanes team to hoist the Cup in 2006. He spent 12 seasons in Raleigh, appearing in 909 games and ending his career in Carolina with 775 points and 322 goals.

Aho will almost certainly surpass Staal in goals scored in the near future — there is a good chance that will be the case by the end of November. He is also 41 points away from tying Staal, and 42 away from passing him.

Ron Francis spent two stints with the Hartford Whalers/Carolina Hurricanes. The first with the Whalers from 1981-82 until 1990-91. He returned to the franchise in 1998-99 when they became known as the Hurricanes and stayed until 2003-04.

Aho is still a few good seasons from passing Francis' points total of 1,175, which he reached in 1,186 games with the Whalers/Hurricanes. He also notched 382 goals and 793 assists. Keep an eye on both Francis' and Staal's numbers as Aho continues to chase legends and reach franchise milestones.

Aho is one of the faces of this Carolina Hurricanes team. He has passed the point-per-game mark in four of his 10 full seasons with the Hurricanes and has a Stanley Cup on his resume.

That said, Aho has never won a major award, despite appearing in three All-Star Games and being part of Team Finland at the 2026 Winter Olympics, helping them snag a Bronze Medal. Aho has top-10 finishes for the Hart and Selke Awards, and that may wind up being the case again this season should he keep performing at a legendary rate.

The more Aho passes franchise legends in these major categories, the more his own legend will grow in Hurricanes history. That trend is showing no signs of slowing down.

#2830. 4 things to watch as the Mavericks’ Cup quest continues against the Utah Jazz

The Dallas Mavericks (4-4) will meet the Utah Jazz (1-7) 7:00pm CST Friday at American Airlines Center in the the group stage of the in-season NBA Cup tournament. Dallas enters this contest on a two-game slide after their comeback attempt Wednesday against San Antonio came up short, 112-111. After a challenging stretch of the schedule to begin the season, the Mavericks face their second opponent this week to bring a record with fewer than two wins into the AAC; Monday the Sacramento Kings improved to 1-6 against a Mavericks team without Kyrie Irving, 115-100. The Jazz arrive having dropped six straight after the Pelicans prevailed Wednesday in overtime, 120-113. The Mavericks and Jazz share a common opponent this week, as Jazz fell Monday to the Spurs, 119-97.

The Jazz organization's goals remain more focused on developing their young core than immediate playoff contention, but in dealing for forward Jaren Jackson Jr. last spring, CEO Danny Ainge moved beyond the rebuilding phase of angling for draft position with a trade that greatly bolstered the team's chances (and expectations) of winning now. Jackson's departure against the Grizzlies Oct. 26 with a torn MCL means that the team will turn to the stockpile of talent amassed in recent drafts, like forward Ace Bailey, guard Darryn Peterson, and point guard Keyonte George.

All three of those players seem to have bright futures, but it is Lewisville's George who has emerged as the most NBA-ready player as of this writing. The big guard hit some big shots against New Orleans Wednesday, icing a jumper that seemed to have won it before replays revealed that the ball left his hands a split second late, then knocking down the first six points of overtime coming off high screens from center Lauri Markkanen. Though the Jazz got blown out by the Spurs Monday, the previous matchup of these teams Oct. 28, a 117-111 OT Spurs win, provided some insight as to how this Jazz team can be dangerous. Playing in their first game since the Jackson injury, George keyed a Utah comeback with a drive and layup that tied it in the final minute, and guard Brice Sensabaugh made the three that set up George's heroics, plus another three in overtime.

For the second time in a week the Mavericks face an electric scoring guard from the 2026 draft class. Sacramento's Darius Acuff's 10 of 21 performance against Dallas Monday improved his season shooting percentage to .406. Utah's Peterson, shooting 34% on the season, has shown flashes of his handle and shot creating ability, but at times has looked like a rookie trying to find his role in the offense.

Markkanen, the Jazz' other veteran big man who appears ready to win now, has been tested in Jackson's absence, chasing a 2-11 three-point shooting performance against Portland with an excellent 4-7 effort from deep against the Spurs. After disappearing for much of the second half against the Pelicans, Markkanen emerged in the final two minutes of regulation to drill a three that brought the Jazz to within one and a pair of foul shots that pushed them in front a possession later. Shouldering the load as Utah's primary frontcourt threat (starting center Jusuf Nurkic is out with a toe injury) has exposed Markkanen's strengths and flaws alike. Wednesday the Jazz produced their second 40-point quarter of the season in the third, as Markkanen and reserve center Mohamed Bamba pushed and stretched a small Pelicans lineup around the floor and the Jazz attacked the rim relentlessly. But when New Orleans went big late, Zion Williamson, Derik Queen, and Saddiq Bey all got the shots they needed against Markkanen.

Some of the Mavericks' most effective stretches against the Spurs came with PJ Washington Jr., Morez Johnson Jr., and Caleb Martin taking turns as the biggest Mavs on the floor, as Dallas' small lineup cut into the Spurs' lead even after Victor Webanyama re-entered the game. If coach Dusty May employs a similar strategy against Utah, Markkanen and Bamba will have to cover a lot of ground to keep up.

Friday's game will be the Mavericks' second of the group stage of the in-season tournament and the Jazz' first. Dallas beat Houston Friday, 116-111, to notch a 1-0 record in the group stage and a +5 point differential. Dallas trails Denver for first place in West group A, as the Nuggets' +7 point differential after their 127-120 win against Phoenix Friday gave them the tiebreaker.

Mavericks guard Walter Clayton Jr. appeared in 45 games for the Jazz last season with averages of 6.8 points and 5.5 assists before being included in the Feb. 3 trade with Memphis that brought Jaren Jackson Jr. to Utah. Jazz guard Josh Green, a Maverick fan favorite from 2020-24, comes off the bench for 3.0 points per game, and is still making things happen with hard cuts and hustle plays.

Friday's game will be shown on MAVSTV+ and KFAA Channel 29. Radio broadcast is 105.3fm KRLD (English) and 99.1fm KFZO (español).

#2829. Here’s where the Steelers’ defense still has room to grow

It's a good time to be a Steelers fan. Pittsburgh sits atop the AFC North entering a Week 9 bye, coming off a big win over the rival Cleveland Browns where the Steelers both scored a lot of points (27) and didn't give up many (six).

As a result, you're going to see a lot of positive Steelers coverage over the next two weeks. And for good reason. The team's running game and front seven were undoubtedly elite against Cleveland. Pittsburgh has been playing like one of the NFL's best teams as of the late.

But as knowledgeable NFL writers/podcasters Jon Ledyard and Derrick Bell quipped following the Browns game, a lot is going right for the Steelers, "but the forward pass, especially on offense, still stands between them and greatness."

Most are aware of the Steelers' passing deficiencies on offense, but I'd argue that on the other side of the ball, Pittsburgh's passing defense could be a similarly limiting factor on the team's long-term potential.

Pittsburgh's safety room has been an apparent issue since before the season started, and even with Robert Spears-Jennings blowing past Year 1 expectations as a seventh-round rookie, the play at the position has been a weaker spot on defense. DeShon Elliott missed much of the early season with injury and hasn't been that impactful since returning, while free agent signing Jaquan Brisker hasn't always been able to rise to the challenge when it comes to coverage ability and instincts.

Meanwhile at cornerback, the group's injury issues have largely subsided at this point in the season. But with Joey Porter Jr. — by far the best player in the secondary — never suiting up for the black and gold before being traded to Dallas, an expected strength on defense has turned into a position that's been solid at best in 2026.

All of that's to say, despite Pittsburgh having not allowed 20 points in each of its last three games, the "elite" label the defense has earned as of late doesn't feel accurate to the state of the secondary, which looks like a potentially ticking time bomb with Joe Burrow, CJ Stroud and Trevor Lawrence still waiting on the schedule.

Pittsburgh's season defensive stats match the eye test. While ranking near the top of the league in stopping the run (third), sacking the quarterback (first), and forcing a quick time to throw (third — partially explaining a surprisingly middling pressure rate), the Steelers are middle of the pack when stopping the pass (17th) and among the worst in yards after catch allowed (30th) and average separation allowed per target (32nd).

The dichotomy was obvious in Pittsburgh's recent big win over Cleveland, where the Steelers' front seven dominated to the tune of eight team sacks and only 34 rushing yards allowed, holding the Browns to single-digit points. However, Browns quarterback DeShaun Watson passed for 324 yards — and while it's more than fair to point out that Cleveland was in a must-pass situation for much of the game and against a prevent-oriented Steelers defense late, the Browns also missed a number of open passing opportunities that would've juiced Watson's numbers even more.

Essentially, in the rare instances when the Steelers' pass rush wasn't completely dominating on Sunday, the Pittsburgh secondary wasn't exactly holding up its end of the bargain.

Here are some of the bigger plays the Steelers gave up in the passing game on Sunday:

In the first clip above, Brisker just gets beat, plain and simple, when manned up against a good receiving tight end in the Browns' Harold Fannin Jr. On the second, the Browns find the open space in the Steelers' two-high coverage, with Brisker stuck in no man's land between the running back and the corner route. In the final clip, there's a miscommunication between Daylen Everette and Asante Samuel Jr., with both jumping the same route as KC Concepcion scoots by both for a 20-plus-yard gain.

To be fair, only the last play is a true breakdown in coverage, with the first two being the sort of man- and zone-beaters you expect to see work at some point in an NFL game (even if you would hope Brisker holds up better against tight ends in coverage).

In the first clip above, Samuel loses Jerry Jeudy (bottom of the screen wide receiver) in coverage; the Browns receiver was open for a 70-yard touchdown well before Watson threw the ball. Instead, the play goes for just a first down to Fannin.

On the next play, the Browns run a play-action concept, and with both safeties preoccupied by the deep route, Samuel is left to choose between covering Denzel Boston and going after Watson. He never really commits to either, and is saved by a terrible throw by Watson. It negates what would've been a 20- or 30-yard gain.

The next play is much tighter coverage and a bit more nit-picky than the other examples in this article, but Boston clearly gets a step on Jamel Dean with the Steelers in quarters. Brisker stops his feet and leaves the corner on an island, losing inside leverage. A good throw closer to the post is a touchdown.

Finally, there's a late-game extended play where Jeudy once again finds himself open, with no Steelers defender within five yards of him in any direction. Watson steps up in the pocket, draws Jeudy even more open, and then checks it down to the running back — who still gets the first down.

Of course, you can go back and watch any good NFL defensive performance and find open players who were missed. However, I'd argue there were a lot more than usual in the Steelers' Sunday win.

But there's also another level of nuance: It was a good gameplan by the Steelers, who knew they could beat the Browns with pressure, unleashing their second-highest single-game blitz rate of the season thus far (30%). Watson, who has actually had a solid 2026, looked uncomfortable and inaccurate all game. The Steelers' front seven feasted, the secondary benefited from off-target throws, and Pittsburgh didn't give up a touchdown in a three-score win.

Honestly, my thoughts in this article can best be summed up by the below play, where Robert Spears-Jennings jumps Denzel Boston's route, throwing Watson out of rhythm and into an eventual sack. With no coverage help behind Spears-Jennings, Boston was left wide open — but the aggression, paired with pressure, resulted in a positive play for the Steelers.

However, it won't always be that easy — especially later in the season and postseason against better quarterbacks and passing offenses (Frankly, besides Burrow and Drake Maye, have the Steelers faced many good passers this year?). The Steelers' pass defense as a whole has been adequate this season, but it hasn't been at the level the team's overall defensive numbers would indicate. Whether it's athleticism in man defense or chemistry in zone, there's still a lot of work to do.

But it still does feel like an improved Steelers defense than in past seasons. The pass rush has been revitalized, and as evidenced by the Browns game, that can cover for a number of sins on the back end. And the Steelers have two rookies in Robert Spears-Jennings and Daylen Everette who should only continue to develop and improve the secondary with time.

In the short term, the Steelers also have an entire bye week to aid their preparation ahead of a rematch with a high-flying Bengals passing game in Week 10. The Steelers' secondary has some notable issues, but it's far too early to call it a season killer.

#2825. Carmelo and La La Anthony reach divorce settlement 9 years after first splitting

Carmelo Anthony and La La Anthony (née Vázquez) reportedly have reached a settlement agreement after almost a decade-long split.

Although terms of the separation are confidential, there was a pending judgment of divorce that needed approval from the New York County Supreme Court as of Wednesday, according to People.

Once the former MTV VJ and the NBA star receive the official imprint from a judge, they'll be newly minted singles.

La La filed for divorce from the NBA star back in 2021, citing irreconcilable differences, according to TMZ, which first reported on the settlement Wednesday.

The couple began dating in 2003 after being introduced through a mutual friend, DJ Clue. They became engaged on Christmas Day 2004 and three years later welcomed their son, Kiyan Anthony, now a 19-year-old sophomore at Syracuse University, where he is playing college basketball.

The two tied the knot in July 2010 in a New York ceremony with hundreds of guests, among them sports and entertainment stars including LeBron James, Spike Lee, Serena Williams, Ciara, Kim Kardashian and Ludacris, The Times reported.

Reports of their separation surfaced in April 2017, and by August of that year, Carmelo had welcomed a daughter with ex Mia Burks.

Although the Anthonys reconciled in late 2018, they ultimately called it quits in July 2019, according to People.

La La was already using a divorce label despite it not yet being official.

In a 2022 episode of Alex Cooper's "Call Her Daddy" podcast, La La opened up about her split from Carmelo, saying that there were still difficult moments, especially regarding the co-parenting of Kiyan.

"I learned how strong I am," she said when asked about what she'd learned from the divorce. "You just find yourself getting stronger and stronger. What do they say? Like you don't know how strong you are until being strong is your only option."

La La also shared that the demise of their marriage began around the time that Carmelo was traded from the Denver Nuggets to the New York Knicks in 2011.

At the time of the 2022 interview, she shared that her perspective on love had changed. "I just don't know if it's in the cards for me," she said on the podcast.

However, in a more recent October interview on "The Jennifer Hudson Show," she revealed she was "open" to dating and that this was her "season of being open" for someone who is confident and adventurous.

As for the NBA Hall of Famer, he was rumored to be dating Racquel Smith, the widow of former NFL defensive end Will Smith. The two were seen together in June.
